Examination instructions, as printed
  • Answer SIX questions only.
  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. Every question is therefore recorded at 16 marks, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

Strip the novelty away and this question is about where information is. A seafarer's performance is limited far less often by not knowing something than by the knowledge being in the wrong place - in a manual on a bench, in a superintendent's head ashore, in a console behind them, or in an experience they have never had. Virtual and augmented reality are two different answers to that one problem. VR moves the person to the information: it builds an environment they could not otherwise enter safely or cheaply, which is why it belongs to training and familiarisation. AR moves the information to the person: it leaves them at the job and lays what they need over what they can see, which is why it belongs to operations and maintenance. Once you see them as opposite solutions to the same problem, the applications stop being a list to memorise and become predictable - and so do the limitations, because each one is simply a place where the information cannot be delivered convincingly enough: no weight or heat in a VR fire, no reliable tracking in a vibrating steel engine room, no bandwidth mid-ocean. And note the honest boundary: nothing in this question is governed by an IMO instrument. The only rule that bites is that a simulator used for certification must meet the STCW standards for simulators, which is why immersive training supplements approved training rather than replacing it.

Model written answer

1. What VR and AR are, and why the difference decides the application

Virtual Reality replaces the user's world. A head-mounted stereoscopic display and position tracking place the user inside a wholly computer-generated environment; the real surroundings are excluded. Augmented Reality keeps the real world and registers computer-generated information on top of it, through a visor, a helmet display or a tablet camera. Mixed reality lies between them, anchoring virtual objects to real surfaces.

That single distinction governs everything that follows. VR takes the user somewhere they cannot safely or economically be - so it belongs to training. AR keeps the user where the work is and adds what they do not know - so it belongs to operations and maintenance. An answer that treats the two as interchangeable cannot explain why they are deployed differently.

2. Integration into training

VR delivers what neither a classroom nor a real ship can: unlimited repetition of a rare and dangerous event at no risk and no cost. Enclosed-space entry and rescue, engine-room fire, flooding and damage control, lifeboat launching, high-voltage isolation and permit-to-work discipline can all be rehearsed to failure. It supports familiarisation before joining - a joining engineer can walk the machinery spaces, find the emergency escapes and locate the emergency generator before the ship is boarded, which is a direct answer to a known cause of early-tour incidents. It also allows whole-team drills across ranks in one shared environment, and it records every action, so debriefing is evidential rather than remembered.

One discipline matters here. STCW permits training and assessment by simulator, but it sets standards for the simulator and for those who conduct and assess the training. An immersive trainer is not automatically a recognised means of demonstrating competence; it must satisfy that regime first. Until it does, it supplements approved training rather than replacing it.

3. Integration into operations

AR works at the point of the task. On the bridge and in the engine control room it supports decision-making by putting data in the operator's line of sight instead of on a console behind them - overlaid targets and limits, a checklist that advances with the job, an alarm shown against the equipment it belongs to rather than as a line of text. In cargo and mooring operations it can display exclusion zones and load limits on the deck itself.

The strongest operational case is remote expert support. A shore specialist sees the seafarer's own view and annotates it live, so scarce expertise reaches a ship at sea without travel, visa or attendance delay - the same logic that already underpins remote survey.

4. Integration into maintenance

This is where the technology is furthest advanced. AR overlays the maintenance procedure onto the machine: the correct component highlighted, the sequence stepped, torque values and clearances shown in place, and the last recorded readings called up beside the unit. Hands stay free, and the manual is not on a bench three metres away. It supports guided fault-finding, gives remote maker support for an unfamiliar overhaul, and - linked to a digital twin - lets planned work be rehearsed in VR before the equipment is opened, so tooling, space and lifting problems are found before the ship is immobilised. Class remote survey is the same idea applied to statutory work.

5. The benefits, against safety, efficiency and decision-making

Safety. Hazardous evolutions are practised without exposure; competence is demonstrated before the first real attempt; and remote support removes travel to a hazardous or remote location. Efficiency. Shorter familiarisation, fewer errors and re-works, less waiting for an attending specialist, and less unplanned off-hire. Decision-making. Information appears in context and in time - which is the whole point, because most bad decisions on board are not caused by missing data but by data that arrived in the wrong place or the wrong form. Add two further gains: objective, recorded assessment of what a candidate actually did, and standardisation of training and procedure across a whole fleet.

6. The challenges, and a reasoned verdict

The obstacles are real. Cost and content - the headset is cheap and the validated, ship-specific content is not, and it must be maintained as the ship changes. Fidelity and false confidence - a VR trainer conveys sequence and layout but not weight, heat, vibration, smell or tool feel, so a trainee may be confident and not competent, which is worse than being neither. Physiological limits - latency between head movement and image causes simulator sickness, and headsets are hot and heavy for long use. The environment - engine rooms are hot, oily, noisy, vibrating and full of steel that disrupts tracking; equipment must be intrinsically safe in hazardous zones; and the visor itself becomes a hazard if it degrades peripheral vision or hearing near moving machinery. Connectivity - live remote support depends on bandwidth a ship at sea may not have. Cyber and data - the headset is a networked device and drawings, procedures and machinery data are commercially sensitive. And acceptance and regulation - crews must trust it, and the regulatory framework recognising immersive training for certification is not yet mature.

The honest verdict is that VR and AR are proven as an aid and not yet established as a substitute. Familiarisation, drill rehearsal, maintenance guidance and remote support already pay for themselves; assessment for certification and safety-critical real-time operational use are not there yet. They should be adopted where they add a capability that did not exist, and not where they merely digitise something that already works.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem contains two printed questions and the second is the one candidates run out of time for. First: how can VR and AR be integrated into training, operations and maintenance to enhance safety, efficiency and decision-making. Second: what are the potential challenges and benefits. Steps 2, 3 and 4 take the three named domains in the stem's own order; step 5 answers the three named outcome words; step 6 answers challenges and closes with a verdict.

Notice what the stem does not ask. It does not ask for a history of simulation, a survey of headsets, or a description of the maritime industry's digital transformation. Every paragraph should be attached to one of the three domains or to one of the three outcomes.

The one distinction that generates the whole answer

VR replaces the world; AR annotates it. Therefore VR takes you where you cannot safely go - which is training - and AR keeps you where the work is and tells you what you do not know - which is operations and maintenance. If you hold only that sentence you can reason out the domains, the benefits and most of the limitations without recalling anything else. It also protects you from the commonest structural error, which is writing one undifferentiated essay about 'immersive technology'.

Common mistakes

  • Using VR and AR interchangeably. They solve opposite problems and are deployed in different places.
  • Answering only benefits. The stem prints the word challenges first. An answer with no limitations section cannot score the second half.
  • Naming the three outcome words - safety, efficiency, decision-making - nowhere in the answer. They are the mark scheme; use them as labels.
  • Claiming VR training satisfies STCW. Simulator-based training is recognised, but the simulator and the trainer must meet the standards. Immersive kit is not automatically approved training.
  • Quoting percentage improvements in retention, training time or accident rate. Those figures are vendor-supplied and unverifiable.
  • Naming products, headsets or vendors. It reads as marketing and it dates instantly.
  • Forgetting the engine-room environment - heat, oil, vibration, noise, steel structure and hazardous zones defeat consumer hardware, and this is the practical objection an examiner at management level expects.
  • Ignoring haptics. VR conveys sequence and layout; it does not convey weight, heat or tool feel, and that gap is exactly where false confidence comes from.

Examiner traps

  • Is a VR fire drill a substitute for a real fire drill? No. It rehearses sequence, communication and decision under time pressure; it cannot reproduce heat, weight, breathing apparatus load or physical exhaustion.
  • Would you accept a VR certificate of competence? Only if the simulator and the assessment satisfied the STCW simulator standards and the flag or its recognised organisation accepted it. This is the discriminating answer.
  • Why is AR ahead of VR on board? Because AR augments a job already being done, so it needs no separate time, space or release from duty; VR needs the seafarer to stop working.
  • What is the single biggest technical obstacle in the engine room? The environment, and specifically tracking - steel structure, vibration and poor light defeat the registration on which an overlay depends. An overlay that points at the wrong valve is worse than no overlay.
  • Is this a cyber risk? Yes. It is a networked device carrying drawings, procedures and machinery data, and it must sit inside the ship's cyber risk management, not outside it.
  • What would you do first as Chief Engineer? Familiarisation and planned-overhaul rehearsal - the lowest-risk, highest-return application - not assessment.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Define virtual reality and augmented reality, and give one shipboard use of each.
  • How would you use VR in enclosed-space entry training?
  • What does a digital twin add to planned maintenance?
  • What is remote survey, and how is AR relevant to it?
  • What causes simulator sickness?
  • Which spaces on board would prevent you using a standard headset, and why?
  • How would you convince a sceptical superintendent that this is worth the money?
  • If the ship has no satellite bandwidth, which of these applications still work? The offline ones - VR training, stored AR procedures. Live remote support does not.

Memory framework

Two technologies, three domains, three outcomes, then the objections. VR replaces, AR annotates (1). Domains in the stem's order: TRAINING (2) - go where you cannot safely go; OPERATIONS (3) - data in your line of sight and an expert on your shoulder; MAINTENANCE (4) - the manual on the machine. Outcomes in the stem's words: safety, efficiency, decision-making (5). Then the objections (6), which chain physically: cost, fidelity, physiology, environment, connectivity, cyber, acceptance.

For the limitations use the ship itself as the cue - walk from the wallet (cost), to the hands (no haptics), to the head (sickness), to the engine room (heat, oil, tracking, hazardous zones), to the antenna (bandwidth), to the network (cyber), to the crew (acceptance).

Uncertainty and the limits of what can be claimed

This is a technology-appraisal question with no governing instrument, and the answer says so rather than borrowing authority it does not have. No IMO or statutory instrument prescribes the use of VR or AR, and none is named. The one genuine regulatory anchor - that simulator-based training and assessment must satisfy the STCW simulator standards - is stated at the level of the regime, because MIW holds no STCW Code text with a usable text layer, and no provision number is quoted.

No quantity of any kind is asserted. No adoption rate, no equipment cost, no training-time reduction, no retention improvement, no accident-reduction figure. Published numbers in this field are overwhelmingly vendor-supplied and are not independently verifiable; the production protocol requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision. No product, headset model, software house or classification society is named anywhere, for the same reason.

Finally, currency. This answer is anchored to November 2024 and states the position at that date - the technology proven as an aid, not established as a substitute, with the certification framework immature. That is a fast-moving field, and the correct answer for a later sitting is a different answer. What makes this one right is that it stops where the industry had actually got to.

Flashcards 8

1 What VR and AR are and why the difference decides the application. 2 Training. 3 Operations. 4 Maintenance. 5 Benefits against safety, efficiency and decision-making. 6 Challenges and a verdict.

Steps 2 to 4 are the three domains the stem names, in its order; step 5 uses the stem's three outcome words as labels; step 6 answers the half candidates run out of time for.

VR replaces the user's environment with a wholly computer-generated one, excluding the real surroundings. AR keeps the real world and registers computer-generated information on top of it. It matters because VR takes the user somewhere they cannot safely or economically be, which suits training, while AR keeps the user at the job and adds what they do not know, which suits operations and maintenance.

This single distinction generates the applications, the benefits and most of the limitations.

Not automatically. STCW recognises training and assessment conducted on a simulator, but it sets standards for the simulator's performance and for those conducting and assessing simulator-based training. An immersive trainer must satisfy that regime and be accepted by the Administration or its recognised organisation before it can count towards certification. Until then it supplements approved training rather than replacing it.

The discriminating answer. Claiming immersive kit is approved training is a straight error, and no instrument prescribes VR or AR at all.

One, the maintenance procedure overlaid on the machine itself with the correct component highlighted, the sequence stepped and torque values and clearances shown in place, leaving both hands free. Two, remote maker or superintendent support, where a shore specialist sees the engineer's own view and annotates it live during an unfamiliar overhaul. Three, rehearsal of a planned overhaul in VR against a digital twin before the equipment is opened, so tooling, space and lifting problems are found before the ship is immobilised. Class remote survey applies the same idea to statutory work.

Maintenance is the most mature domain and concrete examples score where generalities do not.

Because it transfers sequence, layout and communication but not the physical reality. There is no haptic feedback, no weight of a tool or a casualty, no heat, no vibration, no smell, no breathing-apparatus load and no physical exhaustion. A trainee may therefore have rehearsed the decisions perfectly and still be unprepared for the task, and confidence without capability is more dangerous than neither.

This is the strongest single criticism of immersive training and the one an examiner will probe.

The environment defeats the hardware. Heat, oil mist, noise and vibration are hostile to a headset; the surrounding steel structure and poor lighting disrupt the tracking on which registration depends, and an overlay that points at the wrong valve is worse than no overlay; hazardous zones require intrinsically safe equipment; and a visor that degrades peripheral vision or hearing near moving machinery is itself a hazard. Add limited satellite bandwidth for anything live, and the cyber exposure of a networked device carrying drawings and machinery data.

The management-level objection. Naming registration error and hazardous-zone certification shows real engineering judgement.

A maintained virtual model of the actual ship or item of machinery, kept current with its real configuration and condition data. It lets a planned overhaul be rehearsed and access, tooling and lifting checked before the equipment is opened, and it supplies the data an AR overlay displays - the last recorded readings and the current condition of the unit in front of the engineer.

It links the training case and the maintenance case, and it is the standard oral follow-up.

They are proven as an aid and not yet established as a substitute. Familiarisation, drill rehearsal, maintenance guidance and remote expert support already pay for themselves; assessment for certification and safety-critical real-time operational use do not yet. Adopt them where they add a capability that did not previously exist, and not where they merely digitise something that already works.

The question says 'discuss' and asks for challenges as well as benefits, so an appraisal with no conclusion is incomplete.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Distinction. VR replaces the world - rendered environment, head-mounted display, tracking. AR annotates it - information registered on the real scene. So VR = training (go where you cannot safely go); AR = operations and maintenance (stay where the work is). Training. Enclosed space, fire, flooding, lifeboat, HV isolation; ship-specific familiarisation before joining; team drills; every action recorded. But STCW simulator standards must be met - immersive kit is not automatically approved training. Operations. Data in the line of sight; alarms on the equipment; checklists that advance; remote expert support annotating the seafarer's own view. Maintenance - the most mature. Procedure overlaid on the machine, torque and clearance in place, hands free; guided fault-finding; digital twin rehearsal before opening up; remote survey. Benefits by the stem's three words. Safety - practise without exposure. Efficiency - less familiarisation, fewer re-works, less off-hire. Decision-making - information in context and in time. Plus recorded assessment and fleet standardisation. Challenges. Content cost; no haptics so false confidence; simulator sickness from latency; engine-room environment - heat, oil, vibration, steel defeating tracking, hazardous zones; bandwidth; cyber; acceptance and an immature certification framework. Verdict. An aid, not yet a substitute.

Critical numbers
  • No adoption rate, cost, training-time saving or retention figure is quoted anywhere - published values in this field are vendor-supplied and unverifiable
  • Three domains named in the stem: training, operations, maintenance
  • Three outcomes named in the stem: safety, efficiency, decision-making

Critical regulation
There is no instrument governing VR or AR, and the answer says so rather than inventing one. The single genuine anchor is the STCW simulator regime: training and assessment may be conducted on a simulator, but the simulator and those conducting and assessing the training must meet the Convention's standards. An immersive trainer is therefore not automatically a recognised means of demonstrating competence.
Major trap
Writing an enthusiastic essay on applications and never answering challenges, which the stem prints first. The second trap is using VR and AR interchangeably - they solve opposite problems, which is why one is a training tool and the other a working tool. The third is claiming VR training satisfies STCW; simulator-based training is recognised, but the simulator and the assessment must meet the standards.

Production metadata (review build only)
Reuse tier C · Verification FRESH RESEARCH, NO DONOR. This question sets no instrument and the answer does not invent one. NO IMO OR STATUTORY INSTRUMENT PRESCRIBES THE USE OF VR OR AR, AND NONE HAS BEEN NAMED TO GIVE THE ANSWER FALSE AUTHORITY. The one regulatory anchor that is genuinely available is the STCW simulator regime - the Convention permits training and assessment by simulator and sets standards for simulator performance and for the conduct of simulator-based training - and it is used only to make the single point that an immersive trainer is not automatically a recognised means of demonstrating competence. That statement is made at the level of the regime, not by quoting provisions, because MIW holds no licensed STCW Code text and the 2017 consolidated edition in the corpus carries no text layer. The technical content - stereoscopic head-mounted display, six-degree-of-freedom tracking, motion-to-photon latency and simulator sickness, registration and tracking error in AR, the difference between a rendered environment and an overlay on the real one - is engineering and human-factors reference class and is declared as such under PASTPAPER_PRODUCTION_PROTOCOL section 2.1. NO ADOPTION FIGURE, COST, TRAINING-TIME SAVING OR RETENTION PERCENTAGE IS QUOTED ANYWHERE. Vendor products, headset models and class-society branded services are named nowhere. NOTHING POST-DATING NOVEMBER 2024 APPEARS.

Sources
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - the STCW Convention and Code simulator regime: training and assessment may be conducted on a simulator, and the Convention sets performance standards for simulators and requirements for those conducting and assessing simulator-based training. Used ONLY for the proposition that an immersive trainer must satisfy that regime before it can count towards certification. MIW holds the 2017 consolidated edition but it carries no text layer, so this is stated at regime level and no provision is quoted.
  • P3 INDUSTRY GUIDANCE - remote survey and remote inspection techniques are established classification practice, and the surveyor-attended-remotely model is the direct commercial ancestor of the AR maintenance case discussed at step 4. Described as established practice; no class society is named.
  • ENGINEERING AND HUMAN-FACTORS REFERENCE - the distinction between a fully rendered virtual environment and a registered overlay on the real one; head-mounted display, tracking and latency; motion-to-photon delay as the mechanism of simulator sickness; registration error and its consequences for a maintenance overlay; the limits of hand tracking as a substitute for haptic feedback and real tool forces. Declared as engineering explanation under production protocol section 2.1, explained mechanistically rather than asserted.
  • ENGINEERING JUDGEMENT - the assessment at steps 5 and 6 of where the technology currently earns its place, and the verdict that maintenance and familiarisation are ahead of assessment and operations. Reasoned from the properties above and declared as judgement.
  • INTERNAL - the November 2024 anchor at docs/QP2411_TEMPORAL_AND_DONOR_ANCHOR.md section 3, which classifies this question as carrying no instrument boundary and records the STCW comprehensive review as an industry transition in progress rather than as law.
Unresolved
  • MIW holds no licensed STCW Code text with a usable text layer. The simulator-regime statements are made at the level of the regime and no regulation or section number is quoted. Acquiring a text-bearing STCW Code would promote this to primary.
  • No figure of any kind is given for adoption rates, headset or system cost, training-time reduction, knowledge retention or accident reduction. Published figures in this field are vendor-supplied, are not independently verifiable, and would be false precision in an examination answer.
  • The judgement at step 6 that maintenance support and familiarisation are commercially ahead of assessment and of bridge or engine-room operations is engineering and commercial reasoning, not a surveyed finding, and is presented as such.

Acquiring a text-bearing STCW Code would promote this to primary and allow the relevant regulation and Code section to be cited. It is also the most date-sensitive statement in the answer. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • That no IMO or statutory instrument governs the use of VR or AR at this sitting. — An assertion of ABSENCE, which is the hardest class to verify. It was established by searching the corpus instrument holdings and finding no instrument on the subject, and by the fact that the industry treats immersive training as supplementary to approved training rather than as an approved route. If an instrument on immersive training is ever found to pre-date November 2024, this answer changes. Note the direction of the risk: the danger is a later development being read back into this sitting. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • No adoption, cost, training-time, retention or accident-reduction figure is quoted anywhere, and no product, vendor or classification society is named. — A deliberate omission recorded so a later session does not read it as a gap and fill it with a vendor-supplied number. Production protocol section 2.1 requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision, and every published figure in this field is supplied by a party selling the technology. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
Understand this first

Ammonia interests shipping for one reason: the molecule NH3 contains no carbon, so burning it releases no CO2 at the funnel. Everything else about it is a problem to be solved. It carries less than half the energy of fuel oil per tonne, it will not burn readily on its own, and it is acutely toxic at concentrations far below those at which you would smell danger in any other fuel. So the honest way to answer is not to decide whether ammonia is good or bad, but to work through the four viability tests the stem names - technical, operational, safety and commercial - and show where each one stood. Note carefully when this paper was set. In November 2024 the IMO had no instrument in force, and none yet approved, governing ammonia as a fuel; a ship was approved through the alternative-design route with class rules. The drafting was close to done - it went to the Maritime Safety Committee the following month - but close to done is not done, and that distinction is worth more marks than any technical detail in the answer. The absence is not a gap in your answer; it is a large part of the answer.

Model written answer

1. What ammonia is, and why it is proposed

Ammonia is NH3. The molecule contains no carbon, so combustion produces no carbon dioxide at the point of use, and no sulphur means no SOx and no fuel-borne particulates. That one property is the entire reason it is under discussion.

It has two practical advantages over hydrogen, the other carbon-free candidate. It liquefies readily - by modest refrigeration or modest pressure - so it needs no near-absolute-zero cryogenics. And it is already manufactured, stored, shipped and handled worldwide in very large quantity as a fertiliser feedstock, so an industrial base, a seaborne trade and real handling experience already exist. It may itself be regarded as a practical hydrogen carrier.

2. Technical viability

The central difficulty is energy density. Ammonia carries less than half the energy of fuel oil per tonne, and in energy-per-unit-volume terms a ship needs roughly two and a half to three times the tank volume for the same energy. On a fixed hull that volume comes out of cargo, ballast or accommodation.

It is stored liquid: fully refrigerated at about minus 33 degrees Celsius at atmospheric pressure, pressurised at ambient temperature, or semi-refrigerated - the three options familiar from gas-carrier practice.

It is also a poor fuel to burn: narrow flammability range, high ignition energy, high autoignition temperature and a very slow flame speed. It will not reliably compression-ignite alone, so a marine two-stroke burns it dual-fuel with a pilot fuel - which means a conventional fuel system, and a residual carbon emission, stay on board. Materials matter too: ammonia attacks copper, brass, zinc and some alloys, so existing pipework, gauges and instrument fittings cannot simply be reused.

3. Safety, and the regulatory route at this sitting

Ammonia reverses the usual hazard priority. With LNG the dominant hazard is flammability; with ammonia it is acute toxicity. It is harmful, then rapidly fatal, at concentrations far below those at which it would burn, so a release presenting no fire risk at all may still be lethal. It is corrosive to eyes and respiratory tract, and its strong warning odour cannot be relied on at dangerous concentrations.

Design therefore concentrates on containment and separation: double-walled fuel piping with the annular space monitored, fuel-preparation machinery in gas-tight enclosures with dedicated extraction, extensive gas detection, water deluge and absorption, controlled release and venting, and protected escape routes and safe refuge for the crew.

The regulatory position at the date of this paper is the key point, and it is one of absence. There was no IMO instrument in force, and none yet approved, governing the use of ammonia as a marine fuel. The IGF Code is the mandatory code for low-flashpoint fuels, but its detailed prescriptive provisions covered natural gas only. Where a fuel is not prescribed by the Code, the ship is approved through the alternative design and arrangements route of SOLAS regulation II-1/55 - equivalence to the Code's goal and functional requirements demonstrated by risk assessment and approved by the Administration or a recognized organization acting for it. In practice the technical detail was carried by classification-society rules. IMO work was well advanced: the draft interim guidelines had been worked through the sub-committee stage during 2024 and were awaiting approval by the Maritime Safety Committee, which had not yet been given. Do not write that interim guidelines were in place at this sitting.

4. Operational viability

There is no established bunkering infrastructure for ammonia as a fuel. Ammonia terminals exist for the chemical trade, but bunkering a ship is a different operation and is not widely available. It cannot be an open transfer: it needs closed, vapour-returned, leak-monitored connections with emergency release couplings and a controlled exclusion zone.

Crew competence is a genuine gap. The STCW training regime for low-flashpoint fuels was framed around gas. Toxic-fuel operation demands additional training, different personal protective equipment, respiratory protection, dedicated medical first aid and antidote arrangements, and rehearsed rescue from an enclosed space filled with a toxic vapour rather than an asphyxiant one. Purging, inerting and gas-freeing become routine engineering operations rather than occasional ones.

5. Commercial viability

Two costs dominate. First, the fuel: almost all ammonia produced today is grey - made from natural gas by steam reforming and the Haber-Bosch process - so it is carbon-intensive upstream even though it is carbon-free at the funnel. Green ammonia, from renewable hydrogen, is what the sustainability claim actually depends on, and it is scarce and considerably dearer. Second, the ship: larger tanks, double-walled systems, gas-tight enclosures and safety systems raise capital cost, and the space they occupy is a permanent loss of earning capacity.

So the commercial case does not rest on fuel price - on that basis ammonia loses. It rests on regulation putting a price on carbon and on charterers valuing a low-carbon voyage. At this sitting that pressure was real and increasing: the EU Emissions Trading System had applied to maritime transport since 1 January 2024, and the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy of July 2023 had set the direction and the timetable for the measures that follow. Ammonia is best judged as a long-term candidate whose economics are created by regulation.

6. The environmental balance

Carbon-free at the funnel is not the same as sustainable overall, and a good answer says so. Three qualifications matter. The production pathway decides whether any real carbon saving occurs at all. Combustion produces nitrous oxide (N2O), a greenhouse gas hundreds of times more potent than CO2, so even a small quantity erodes the benefit - and NOx control is still required. And unburnt ammonia slip is both a toxic hazard and an environmental one, requiring after-treatment. The gain is real but conditional on green production and effective exhaust after-treatment, not automatic.

7. Reasoned conclusion

Ammonia is technically feasible - engines and containment can be built, and orders had been placed by this date - but it is not yet mature. The binding constraints are not the engine; they are safety engineering, crew competence, bunkering supply and the availability of green ammonia, with the IMO framework drafted but not yet approved at this sitting. It is best described as a credible long-term candidate for deep-sea decarbonisation whose viability is conditional - on green production, on infrastructure and on a carbon price - rather than as a fuel ready for general adoption.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem names four viability tests - technical, operational, safety and commercial. They are the mark scheme. Steps 2 to 5 answer them one at a time and in the stem's own words, so the examiner can tick each. The commonest way to lose marks is a long enthusiastic essay about ammonia in general that answers two of the four well and never mentions the other two.

Steps 1 and 7 are cheap and valuable. A short factual opening shows you know what the fuel is, and a reasoned conclusion answers the command verb discuss. A discussion with no verdict is incomplete however good the middle is.

The date of this paper - what applied and what did not

This is the highest-risk point on the question. At November 2024 there was no IMO instrument in force, and none yet approved, governing ammonia as a marine fuel. The IGF Code's detailed provisions covered natural gas only, and an ammonia-fuelled ship was approved through SOLAS II-1/55 alternative design and arrangements, with class rules and a risk assessment carrying the detail.

If you revise this topic from current material you will find a set of IMO interim guidelines for the safety of ships using ammonia as fuel. They post-date this paper. They were approved at MSC 109, which sat 2 to 6 December 2024 - the month after this examination - and were issued in February 2025. Writing them into this answer is a factual error about the state of the law at the sitting, and it is the easiest way to be wrong here while sounding well informed.

Note the nuance that makes a November answer better than a copied April one. By this sitting the work was further along than earlier in 2024 - the drafting had been through the sub-committee stage and the instrument was waiting on the Committee - but waiting on approval is not approval. The correct sentence is: drafted, not approved, not in force.

The one idea that organises the whole question

Ammonia inverts the hazard you are trained for. Every low-flashpoint fuel course, and the whole shape of the IGF Code, is built around flammability - keep it from ignition sources, contain the gas, ventilate the space. Ammonia is a poor fuel and a serious poison. It barely burns, and it kills far below the flammable range. Once you see that, the rest follows: the pilot fuel from poor combustion, the double-walled piping and safe refuge from toxicity, the training gap from both, and the regulatory vacuum from the fact that the existing Code was written for the opposite hazard.

Common mistakes

  • Writing the IMO interim guidelines for ammonia as fuel into this sitting. They were approved in December 2024 and issued in February 2025. This is the trap the question is built on.
  • Saying the guidelines were 'about to be approved' and then treating them as effective. Drafted is not approved; approved is not in force.
  • Confusing ammonia as cargo with ammonia as fuel. Carriage is long settled under the IGC Code; use as fuel was not. Do not merge them.
  • Claiming ammonia is zero-emission or zero-carbon without qualification. It is carbon-free at the point of combustion; well-to-wake it depends entirely on how it was made.
  • Forgetting N2O and ammonia slip. An answer presenting combustion as clean has missed the most interesting environmental point in the topic.
  • Treating it as a pure ammonia engine. In compression ignition it needs a pilot fuel, so it is dual-fuel.
  • Answering only two or three of the four named viability lenses.
  • Quoting fuel prices, efficiency percentages or newbuilding costs. These move constantly and are not verifiable; state the direction, not a figure.

Examiner traps

  • The word 'sustainable' in the stem. It is an assumption, not a finding. A strong answer tests it and distinguishes green from grey.
  • 'Future maritime applications'. The tense invites you to describe something that has not arrived. Be accurate about what exists and what does not.
  • Toxicity versus flammability. The examiner will probe whether you understand that the dominant hazard is not the one the IGF Code was designed around.
  • Energy density. Expect to be asked what it means for the ship - lost cargo space or reduced range, which is a commercial consequence, not just a technical one.
  • Under what rule was such a ship approved? SOLAS II-1/55 alternative design and arrangements. This is the single most discriminating fact in the answer.
  • Comparison with methanol and LNG. A short comparative sentence shows judgement; an unprompted three-page comparison answers a different question.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Why does an ammonia-fuelled two-stroke need a pilot fuel?
  • What is the storage condition of ammonia on board, and what are the alternatives?
  • Which materials must be avoided in an ammonia fuel system, and why?
  • What would you do on discovering an ammonia leak in the engine room?
  • How would you bunker ammonia, and how does it differ from bunkering fuel oil?
  • If ammonia produces no CO2, why is it not automatically a green fuel?
  • What additional training would your engineers need before the ship converted?
  • Under what regulation was an ammonia-fuelled ship approved at the date of this paper?

Memory framework

Take the four lenses in the stem's order and hang one dominant idea on each: TECHNICAL - it does not burn well and takes up too much room. SAFETY - it is a poison, not a fire, and there was no rule for it. OPERATIONAL - nowhere to get it and nobody trained. COMMERCIAL - only a carbon price makes it pay. Then finish with the honest environmental qualification: green or grey decides everything.

Uncertainty and currency

This answer states the position as at November 2024, and that position has since changed - IMO interim guidelines for the safety of ships using ammonia as fuel were approved at MSC 109 in December 2024 and issued in February 2025. They are deliberately excluded, because the answer must be true for the paper it belongs to. If the same question is set at a later sitting, the regulatory limb is the part that must be re-derived; the technical, operational and safety engineering is largely unchanged.

Two evidence limits are declared. MIW holds neither SOLAS nor the IGF Code, so the II-1/55 route and the natural-gas-only scope are authoritative restatement rather than instrument text, and no provision wording is quoted. And the precise sub-committee session that finalised the draft during 2024 is not named, because no held source establishes it; the answer relies only on the fact of non-approval, which the MSC 109 date establishes.

No fuel price, efficiency figure, newbuilding cost or orderbook count is quoted anywhere. Those numbers are installation-specific or move month to month, and false precision is worse in an examination than an accurate qualitative statement. The two quantitative statements that are made - roughly two and a half to three times the tank volume, and storage near minus 33 degrees Celsius - are standard engineering reference values, not values read from an IMO instrument, and are declared as such.

  7. __________Reasoned conclusion
Flashcards 8

NH3 contains no carbon, so combustion produces no CO2 at the point of use, and no SOx or fuel-borne particulates. It also liquefies far more easily than hydrogen - by modest refrigeration or pressure rather than deep cryogenics - and is already produced, shipped and handled worldwide in large quantity as a fertiliser feedstock, so an industrial base and real handling experience exist.

The opening mark, and the sentence the whole answer hangs on.

No IMO instrument did. Nothing was in force and nothing had yet been approved. The IGF Code carried detailed provisions for natural gas only, so an ammonia-fuelled ship was approved through SOLAS II-1/55 alternative design and arrangements, demonstrating equivalence to the Code's goal and functional requirements by risk assessment, with class rules carrying the detail. The IMO interim guidelines were approved at MSC 109 in December 2024, the month after this paper, and issued in February 2025.

The load-bearing temporal proposition, and the single easiest way to be wrong on this question while sounding well informed.

For LNG the dominant hazard is flammability. For ammonia it is acute toxicity. Ammonia is harmful and then fatal at concentrations far below its flammable range, so a release presenting no fire risk at all can still be lethal. Its warning odour cannot be relied on at dangerous concentrations. This reverses the assumption the IGF Code was built around, which is why the Code did not simply extend to cover it.

The organising idea of the whole question, and the examiner's favourite probe.

Stored as a liquid: fully refrigerated at about minus 33 degrees Celsius at atmospheric pressure, or pressurised at ambient temperature, or semi-refrigerated. It carries under half the energy of fuel oil per tonne, so roughly two and a half to three times the tank volume is needed for the same energy - volume taken from cargo, ballast or accommodation.

The technical mark, and the bridge to the commercial one - lost volume is lost earning capacity.

Ammonia has a narrow flammability range, a high ignition energy, a high autoignition temperature and a very slow flame speed, so it will not reliably compression-ignite on its own. A pilot injection of conventional fuel provides the ignition source, which means the engine is dual-fuel and a conventional fuel system - and a residual carbon emission - remain on board.

Explains why 'ammonia-fuelled' never means pure ammonia, which candidates routinely get wrong.

It is carbon-free only at the point of combustion. Most ammonia today is grey, made from natural gas by steam reforming and Haber-Bosch, so it is carbon-intensive upstream; only green ammonia from renewable hydrogen delivers a real well-to-wake saving. Combustion also produces nitrous oxide, a greenhouse gas hundreds of times more potent than CO2, plus NOx requiring control, and unburnt ammonia slip which is both a toxic and an environmental problem.

Tests the unexamined premise in the stem's own word 'sustainable'.

Live: the EU Emissions Trading System had applied to maritime transport since 1 January 2024, and the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy - resolution MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023 - had set the direction and timetable. Not live: FuelEU Maritime was adopted in September 2023 but applies only from 1 January 2025, so it must not be described as applying at this sitting.

Two dated regional facts, one operative and one not. Getting the pair the right way round is a discriminator.

Technical, operational, safety and commercial - all four must be visibly answered because they are the mark scheme. The regulatory position sits inside the safety limb, because the absence of a code is a direct consequence of ammonia's dominant hazard being toxicity rather than flammability, which is not what the IGF Code was written for.

Answering only two or three lenses is the commonest structural failure on this question.

Critical regulation
SOLAS regulation II-1/55 - alternative design and arrangements. At this sitting this is how an ammonia-fuelled ship was approved, because the IGF Code's detailed provisions covered natural gas only and no IMO instrument governing ammonia as a fuel was in force or even approved. Major trap
Writing the IMO interim guidelines for ships using ammonia as fuel into a November 2024 answer. They were approved at MSC 109, 2-6 December 2024 - the month AFTER this examination - and issued in February 2025. The trap is sharper here than at earlier 2024 sittings precisely because the work was nearly finished: drafted is not approved, and approved is not in force. At this paper's date the correct answer is that no IMO instrument governed ammonia as a fuel and approval ran through SOLAS II-1/55.

Understand this first

The forebody and the aft body are not doing the same job. The forebody is shaped to reduce resistance - principally wave-making. The aft body is shaped to deliver good water to the propeller. Twin skeg is an aft body decision, taken when restricted draught will not let a single propeller be big enough. If you hold that, you can reason out most of this answer rather than recall it. The second thing to hold is why the topic became urgent again. A hull used to be optimised once, for one speed and one draught, and then left alone. Slow steaming broke that: ships now spend their lives away from the point their hull was shaped for, which is why optimisation is done over an operating profile and why cutting the bow off an existing ship can pay. And the commercial pressure is regulatory - hull form is one of the very few genuine levers a shipowner has on the design indices in MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, and on the operational carbon intensity rating the ship earns every year.

Model written answer

1. Why the hull form governs the power the ship needs

Total resistance divides into frictional resistance, which depends on wetted surface area and hull roughness; wave-making resistance, the energy left behind in the wave system; and viscous pressure or form resistance, arising from the way the flow separates and thickens around the hull. For a full-form, relatively slow merchant ship friction dominates, but wave-making and form resistance are the components the designer can actually change by reshaping the hull.

Hull form also sets the second term in the propulsion equation. Delivered power is resistance times speed divided by the propulsive coefficient, and the hull efficiency element of that coefficient - the relationship between wake fraction and thrust deduction - is a direct product of the aftbody shape. The hull therefore appears on both sides of the problem, which is why it is the largest single design lever available.

2. How hull form optimisation is done, and what changed

The modern method is parametric hull modelling coupled to computational fluid dynamics. The hull is described by a set of driving parameters, thousands of variants are generated and evaluated by CFD - potential flow for wave making, viscous RANS solvers for the wake field - and the promising forms are verified by model tank testing. CFD proposes; the tank still confirms.

The decisive change is what the hull is optimised for. Historically a hull was optimised at one design speed and one design draught. Since slow steaming became normal, ships spend their lives away from that point. Optimisation is now carried out over a matrix of speeds and draughts weighted by the ship's real operating profile - which is also why retrofit optimisation of existing ships is worthwhile, since many were shaped for a speed they will never see again.

3. (i) Forebody optimisation

Scope. The bulbous bow - its volume, length, shape and vertical position; the waterline entrance angle; the fairing of the forward shoulder; and the stem profile. On existing ships this is the classic bow retrofit: the bulb is cut off and replaced, or removed altogether in favour of a straight stem.

Impact. The forebody governs the wave-making component. A bulb works by generating a wave system in antiphase with the bow wave so that the two partially cancel - but that cancellation is tuned to a particular speed and draught, and off that point the bulb can add resistance rather than remove it. A bulb designed for a laden design speed may be actively harmful in ballast or at slow-steaming speed. Reshaping also improves added resistance in waves, which matters because the ship meets real seas rather than calm water, and improves ballast-condition performance where the old bulb sat near the surface.

4. (ii) Aft body optimisation

Scope. The run of the buttock lines, the choice between U-form and V-form sections, the stern bulb, the shaft bossing and skeg arrangement, and the propeller aperture and tip clearances.

Impact. The aft body's real job is not resistance but the quality of the water delivered to the propeller. A full stern produces a thick, distorted boundary layer, so each blade passes through a strongly varying inflow once per revolution. That non-uniform wake causes cavitation, pressure pulses, hull vibration and erosion, and lowers propulsive efficiency. Optimisation aims at a more uniform, better organised wake field, which allows higher blade loading without cavitation and improves the hull efficiency term. There is a genuine trade-off: a wake that is too uniform loses some of the wake gain that makes a single-screw ship efficient in the first place.

Keep the distinction clear. Pre-swirl ducts, stators, fins and boss-cap fins are energy saving devices bolted to the hull. They exploit a poor wake; they are not aft body form optimisation, and an answer that treats them as the same thing has missed what the question asked.

5. (iii) Twin skeg design

Scope. Two separate skegs, each carrying its own shaft line and propeller, in place of a single centreline screw. It suits ships with high block coefficient, large beam and restricted draught, and high installed power - large LNG carriers, ro-ro and car carriers, and bulkers and tankers on draught-limited trades.

Impact - what it buys. On a shallow-draught ship, propeller diameter is the binding constraint. Two smaller propellers permit a larger total disc area and lower revolutions than one propeller squeezed under a flat stern, and open-water efficiency improves accordingly. Each propeller sits in cleaner, more uniform inflow outboard of the centreline, so cavitation and vibration fall. The hull can carry a fuller midbody without the usual penalty, protecting cargo capacity. Redundancy is a real operational benefit.

Impact - what it costs. Two skegs add wetted surface area, and therefore frictional resistance, which on a friction-dominated hull is a significant debit. Two shafts, sterntubes, bearings and sets of machinery raise capital cost and maintenance. Twin skeg is therefore a net gain only where the propulsive efficiency benefit outweighs the added friction - which is precisely the high-block, restricted-draught, high-power case.

6. What limits the gains, and where they are measured

The gains are design-specific and do not simply add: improving the wake field alters the flow the bulb sees, so the hull must be optimised as a whole. Because hull form is a design property it is one of the few genuine levers on the attained EEDI of a newbuilding and the attained EEXI of an existing ship, under chapter 4 of the 2021 revised MARPOL Annex VI - regulation 22 and regulation 23 respectively, with the required values at regulations 24 and 25. In service the benefit appears as reduced fuel per unit of transport work and therefore in the operational carbon intensity rating under regulation 28, and the improvement is planned, implemented and reviewed through Part III of the SEEMP under regulation 26 - for which the operative guidance at this sitting is the 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024. Those requirements have applied since 1 January 2023, which is the commercial reason the subject has become urgent again.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The question names three items and asks for scope and impact of each. The highest-scoring habit here is trivially simple: write the words Scope and Impact as sub-labels under each of steps 3, 4 and 5. It guarantees both halves are answered for all three items, and it makes the examiner's marking obvious.

Steps 1 and 2 are not padding. Without the resistance breakdown you cannot explain why the forebody and the aft body are optimised for different objectives, which is the single insight separating a good answer from a list.

The idea underneath the question

Forward and aft are solving different problems. The forebody fights resistance. The aft body feeds the propeller. Almost every mark can be reasoned from that one sentence: it explains why bulb design is speed- and draught-sensitive, why aft body work is discussed in terms of wake and cavitation rather than drag, and why twin skeg - which is an aft body decision - is justified by propeller diameter and inflow rather than by reduced resistance.

Get the MARPOL Annex VI regulation numbers right

This is where a well-read candidate goes wrong, because most published material still carries the pre-2021 numbering. The 2021 revised MARPOL Annex VI was adopted by resolution MEPC.328(76) and entered into force on 1 November 2022, and it renumbered chapter 4. At the date of this paper the numbering is:

  • Regulation 21 - Functional requirements. It is not EEDI. This is the trap.
  • Regulation 22 - attained EEDI; regulation 24 - required EEDI.
  • Regulation 23 - attained EEXI; regulation 25 - required EEXI.
  • Regulation 26 - SEEMP; regulation 27 - fuel oil consumption data collection.
  • Regulation 28 - operational carbon intensity.

If you cannot remember the numbers under pressure, name the measures and do not guess a number. A correct measure with no number scores; a confident wrong number invites the examiner to probe the one thing you do not know.

Common mistakes

  • Treating a bulbous bow as always beneficial. It is tuned to a design point and can add resistance off it.
  • Discussing ducts, stators and fins as 'aft body optimisation'. They are appendages.
  • Claiming twin skeg reduces resistance. It increases wetted surface and frictional resistance; the gain is in propulsive efficiency.
  • Giving a single percentage saving for each measure as though it were a constant. It is design-specific.
  • Forgetting that hull form optimisation is now done for an operating profile, which is the whole reason retrofits are viable.
  • Answering only 'scope' and never 'impact', or the reverse.
  • Quoting the old Annex VI numbering - regulation 21 for EEDI, regulation 22 for SEEMP. That scheme was superseded on 1 November 2022.

Examiner traps

  • Why remove a bulbous bow? Because the ship no longer runs at the speed and draught the bulb was tuned for. This catches candidates who learned that bulbs are simply good.
  • Is a perfectly uniform wake ideal? No. Some non-uniformity is what gives a single-screw ship its wake gain; the aim is a well-organised wake, not a flat one.
  • Twin skeg on a fine-form fast ship? Poor choice - friction dominates the debit and there is no draught constraint to justify it.
  • Which of EEDI, EEXI and CII does hull form affect? All three - but EEDI and EEXI are design and technical indices while the carbon intensity rating is operational.
  • Where does the improvement get planned and recorded? Part III of the SEEMP, regulation 26. At this sitting the operative guidance is the 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.395(82) - not the 2022 edition, which was revoked in October 2024.
  • Being asked to quantify. Decline to invent a number and explain what it depends on - ship type, operating profile, and how far the existing form is from its optimum.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Name the components of ship resistance and say which dominates on a loaded bulk carrier. Frictional.
  • What is thrust deduction, and why does the aft body affect it? The propeller lowers pressure ahead of itself and increases hull resistance; the stern shape governs how strongly.
  • How is a candidate hull verified before build? CFD screening, then model tank test.
  • Why do twin skeg ships tend to be LNG carriers and car carriers? Large beam, restricted draught, high power, and cargo capacity that must not be sacrificed.
  • What is added resistance in waves and why is it now designed for?
  • Which regulation carries attained EEXI? Regulation 23 of MARPOL Annex VI.
  • Which SEEMP guidelines were current at this date, and what did they replace? MEPC.395(82) of 4 October 2024, which revoked MEPC.346(78).

Memory framework

Two jobs, three items. Forward fights resistance; aft feeds the propeller; twin skeg is an aft decision driven by draught. Then for each of the three, the same two words: Scope, Impact. For the physics use F-W-V: Frictional, Wave-making, Viscous pressure.

For the regulation numbers, count up from EEDI in pairs: 22 and 23 are the attained pair (EEDI, EEXI), 24 and 25 are the required pair, then 26 SEEMP, 27 data, 28 carbon intensity. Regulation 21 is only the functional requirements that sit above them all.

Uncertainty and the limits of what can be quantified

This is a hydrodynamics and design question and the answer is written as such. No numeric percentage saving is quoted anywhere for any of the three measures. That is deliberate: published gains for bow retrofits, aft body reshaping and twin skeg arrangements vary by ship type, by how far the existing form sits from its optimum, and by the operating profile assumed, and a single figure presented as general would be false precision. The production protocol requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than invented.

The regulatory statements at step 6 were read at source for this paper rather than carried forward, and that mattered. The February 2024 answer in the same family maps EEDI to regulation 21 - which is the numbering used before the 2021 revised Annex VI came into force on 1 November 2022, and under which regulation 21 was required EEDI. In the Annex VI actually in force at this sitting, regulation 21 is 'Functional requirements'. The mapping used here was read off the annex to MEPC.328(76) directly.

The reading of 'Twin Skeg Design' as a twin-screw twin-skeg arrangement is stated in the answer rather than assumed silently, because the printed phrase is capable of a second reading. Energy saving devices are expressly excluded from aft body optimisation rather than blurred into it.

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  4. __________(ii) (ii) Aft body optimisation
  5. __________(iii) (iii) Twin skeg design

Flashcards 8

1 Why hull form governs required power. 2 How optimisation is done and what changed. 3 (i) Forebody. 4 (ii) Aft body. 5 (iii) Twin skeg. 6 Limits and where the gain is measured. Under 3, 4 and 5 write Scope then Impact.

The question names three items and asks for scope and impact of each; the sub-labels guarantee both halves are answered.

The forebody is shaped to reduce resistance, principally the wave-making component. The aft body is shaped to deliver a uniform, well-organised wake to the propeller, governing cavitation, pressure pulses, vibration and the hull efficiency term of the propulsive coefficient.

This single distinction generates most of the answer.

Because a bulb cancels the bow wave only at the speed and draught it was tuned for. After slow steaming or a change of trade the ship no longer operates there, and the bulb may be adding resistance rather than removing it. Retrofit re-tunes the forebody to the real operating profile, and also improves ballast-condition performance and added resistance in waves.

The classic follow-up, and it tests whether the candidate understands the mechanism rather than the slogan.

Two separate skegs each carrying its own shaft and propeller instead of a single centreline screw. It suits high block coefficient, large beam, restricted draught and high installed power - large LNG carriers, ro-ro and car carriers, and draught-limited bulkers and tankers.

Scope for item (iii); the ship types are the quickest way to show you understand why.

No. Two skegs add wetted surface and therefore frictional resistance. The benefit is propulsive - larger total propeller disc area at lower revolutions, and cleaner inflow to each propeller - and it outweighs the friction penalty only on high-block, draught-restricted, high-power ships.

The most common wrong statement made about twin skeg.

No. They are energy saving devices - appendages fitted to the hull that exploit the existing wake. Aft body optimisation means changing the shape of the hull itself: buttock lines, section form, stern bulb, bossings and propeller clearances.

The question asks about hull form; conflating form with appendages loses the point of item (ii).

Hull form is a design property, so it bears on attained EEDI at regulation 22 for a newbuilding and attained EEXI at regulation 23 for an existing ship, with the required values at regulations 24 and 25. In service the benefit shows in operational carbon intensity at regulation 28, planned and reviewed through SEEMP Part III at regulation 26. The trap is that regulation 21 is Functional requirements, not EEDI - the pre-2021 numbering was superseded when the revised Annex VI, MEPC.328(76), entered into force on 1 November 2022.

Step 6, and the one place on this question where confident recall from older material produces a wrong answer.

The 2024 Guidelines for the development of a SEEMP, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted on 4 October 2024. Their operative paragraph 3 revokes the 2022 Guidelines, MEPC.346(78). MEPC 82 sat from 30 September to 4 October 2024, one month before this examination, so the 2024 edition is the current one by a narrow margin.

A November 2024 answer differs from a September 2024 answer on exactly this point.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Physics. Resistance = frictional + wave-making + viscous pressure. Hull form changes the last two and sets the wake. Power = R x V / propulsive coefficient, and hull efficiency comes from wake fraction and thrust deduction, which the aft body governs. Method. Parametric modelling + CFD, verified in the tank; the change that matters is optimising for an operating profile, not one design point - which is why retrofit pays. Forward fights resistance; aft feeds the propeller. (i) Forebody. Scope - bulb, entrance angle, shoulder, stem. Impact - wave-making; the bulb cancels the bow wave only at its design speed and draught, and off that point can add resistance. (ii) Aft body. Scope - buttocks, sections, stern bulb, bossings, clearances. Impact - wake quality: uniform organised wake for less cavitation, pressure pulse and vibration, and better hull efficiency. ESDs are appendages, not form. (iii) Twin skeg. Two skegs, shafts, propellers; suits high block, restricted draught, high power; buys diameter and clean inflow; costs wetted surface and machinery. Where measured. Attained EEDI reg 22, attained EEXI reg 23 (required 24 and 25), SEEMP reg 26 under MEPC.395(82), operational carbon intensity reg 28 - all in force since 1 January 2023. Regulation 21 is Functional requirements, NOT EEDI.

Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, as revised by MEPC.328(76) and in force since 1 November 2022. Hull form is a design property, so it bears directly on attained EEDI (regulation 22) for a newbuilding and attained EEXI (regulation 23) for an existing ship, and in service on operational carbon intensity (regulation 28), planned through SEEMP Part III (regulation 26) under the 2024 Guidelines, MEPC.395(82).
Major trap
Saying twin skeg reduces resistance. It increases wetted surface and therefore friction; the gain is propulsive efficiency from larger, slower propellers in cleaner inflow. The second trap is presenting a bulbous bow as unconditionally beneficial. The third is the pre-2021 Annex VI numbering - regulation 21 is Functional requirements, not EEDI, and regulation 22 is attained EEDI, not SEEMP. That scheme was superseded when the revised Annex VI entered into force on 1 November 2022.

Unresolved

Published gains vary by ship type, by how far the existing form sits from its optimum and by the operating profile assumed, and a single figure presented as general would be false precision.

Understand this first

This question is really about a hole in the law and how it was patched twice. For centuries the only universally recognised crime at sea was piracy, and piracy is defined tightly: it must be for private ends, and it must involve two ships - one attacking the other. In 1985 a group of men who had boarded the Achille Lauro as ordinary passengers seized her for openly political ends and murdered a passenger. On the definition, that was not piracy. There was no second ship and no private end, and so there was no offence that every State was entitled to prosecute, and a genuine argument about jurisdiction followed. The SUA Convention is the patch: it does not try to police ships or prevent attacks - the ISPS Code does that - it simply creates the offences and makes sure somebody has to try them, wherever the offender turns up. Then the world's fear changed. After 2001 the worry was no longer that someone would take a ship hostage; it was that someone would use a ship - as a bomb, or as the quiet way to move a nuclear component across an ocean. So the 2005 Protocol patches the patch: new offences in which the ship is the weapon or the delivery vehicle, transport alone made criminal, and for the first time a procedure for stopping the ship before it arrives. Hold that arc - target becomes weapon, prosecution becomes interdiction - and the whole of limb (b) writes itself.
