Model written answer

1. (a) Why the Convention exists - the gap it closed

Before 1988 international law dealt with violence at sea principally through piracy, and the definition of piracy is narrow: it requires private ends, and it requires two ships. An attack mounted for political ends, by people who were already lawfully on board as passengers, is therefore not piracy at all. The seizure of the Achille Lauro in October 1985 demonstrated the consequence - a serious armed attack on a ship, with a passenger murdered, that fell outside the one offence the law of the sea universally recognised, leaving States arguing about who could prosecute.

2. (a) The purpose of the Convention, and how it works

Its purpose is to ensure that appropriate action is taken against persons committing unlawful acts against the safety of maritime navigation, and it achieves this not by policing ships but by closing the jurisdictional gaps in which such offenders could shelter. The mechanism has three parts. It defines a set of offences and requires each State Party to make them punishable by appropriate penalties in its own law. It requires States Parties to establish jurisdiction over those offences on the familiar bases - flag, territory and nationality of the offender - and permits others. And it obliges a State Party in whose territory an alleged offender is found either to extradite him or to submit the case to its own competent authorities for prosecution - the aut dedere aut judicare obligation at Article 10. The offences are also treated as extraditable offences between Parties.

3. (a) Its importance in enhancing maritime security

It matters for four reasons. It removes the safe haven - there is no longer a State in which such an offender can be found and simply released for want of jurisdiction. It reclassifies the conduct as crime rather than misfortune, which brings police, prosecutors and mutual legal assistance to bear instead of leaving it as a shipping matter. It reaches beyond piracy, covering political motive, insiders already lawfully on board, and attacks on navigational facilities ashore. And it completes the security architecture: the ISPS Code under SOLAS chapter XI-2 is preventive - security levels, ship and port facility security plans and officers, access control - while SUA is what happens after prevention fails. One keeps the attacker off the ship; the other ensures that if he gets on, he is tried.

4. (b) The offences under the 1988 Convention

Article 3 makes it an offence for any person unlawfully and intentionally to:

  1. seize or exercise control over a ship by force, threat of force or any other form of intimidation;
  2. perform an act of violence against a person on board a ship, if it is likely to endanger the safe navigation of that ship;
  3. destroy a ship, or cause damage to a ship or to its cargo, which is likely to endanger its safe navigation;
  4. place, or cause to be placed, on a ship a device or substance likely to destroy or damage it;
  5. destroy or seriously damage maritime navigational facilities, or seriously interfere with their operation, if likely to endanger safe navigation;
  6. communicate information known to be false, thereby endangering safe navigation; and
  7. injure or kill any person in connection with any of the above.

Attempt, abetting and threat are also covered. Note the recurring qualification - likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship. That phrase is the boundary of the Convention: it is a navigation-safety instrument, not a general criminal code for the sea. A companion Protocol of the same date extends the scheme to fixed platforms on the continental shelf.

5. (b) The new offences added by the 2005 Protocol

The 2005 Protocol was the response to 11 September 2001 and to concern about the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. It inserts a substantial new body of offences, principally at Article 3bis, committed unlawfully and intentionally when the purpose is to intimidate a population or to compel a Government or an international organization to do or abstain from doing any act:

  • using a ship itself as a weapon - using against or on a ship, or discharging from it, any explosive, radioactive material or biological, chemical or nuclear (BCN) weapon in a manner causing or likely to cause death, serious injury or damage;
  • discharging from a ship oil, liquefied natural gas or other hazardous or noxious substance in such quantity or concentration as to cause or be likely to cause death, serious injury or damage;
  • using a ship in a manner that causes death, serious injury or damage;
  • transporting on board a BCN weapon, or explosive or radioactive material, knowing it is intended for such use; and transporting dual-use equipment, materials, software or technology that significantly contributes to the design or manufacture of a BCN weapon, with intent that it be so used.

Article 3ter makes it an offence to transport a person known to have committed a SUA offence, intending to help him evade prosecution. Article 3quater deals with attempt, threat, participation, organising and directing, and contribution to a group acting with a common purpose.

6. (b) The boarding regime - the enforcement half of the 2005 Protocol

New offences without a means of intercepting the ship would have been of limited value, so the Protocol adds a ship-boarding procedure at Article 8bis. Where a State Party has reasonable grounds to suspect that a ship is involved in a SUA offence, it may request the flag State's authorisation to board and search. The flag State may authorise, may board itself, may board jointly, or may decline. A State may also lodge a standing notification with the Secretary-General giving advance authorisation, or providing that authorisation is deemed granted if no response is received within four hours. Boarding is hedged with safeguards - the safety of life at sea, the security and commercial interests of the ship, humane treatment, and liability for damage where the suspicion proves unfounded. Critically, nothing displaces the primacy of the flag State: the Protocol creates a consent procedure, not a right of unilateral boarding.

7. (b) How the amendments reflect evolving security concerns

The change is a change of fear. 1988 was written about hijacking - taking a ship and the people on it as leverage, so the harm contemplated was to the ship and to those on board, and every offence is tied to endangering safe navigation. 2005 was written about terrorism and proliferation, where the ship is not the target but the weapon or the delivery vehicle, and the harm contemplated is to a population ashore. Four shifts follow. The object of protection widens from the ship to the public. The ship becomes a means, not an end. Mere transport becomes criminal, which is a significant step - the offence is complete without any violence at all, and it makes SUA an instrument of non-proliferation alongside initiatives such as the Proliferation Security Initiative. And the treaty moves from prosecution to interdiction: 1988 dealt with offenders after the event, whereas Article 8bis is designed to stop the cargo before it arrives. The limits are equally revealing - boarding still depends on flag State consent, the offences still require a terrorist purpose, and the whole scheme still depends on each Party enacting and enforcing it domestically.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 6 and 10, and the split tells you where the writing goes. Limb (a) is worth 6 and asks two things - purpose and importance in enhancing maritime security. Limb (b) is worth 10 and asks two things - the main offences under the 1988 Convention and its 2005 Protocol, and how these amendments reflect evolving security concerns.

The commonest failure is to spend the whole answer listing offences and never reach the word reflect. That final analysis is worth several marks on its own and it is the only part of the question that asks the candidate to think rather than recall. Step 7 exists to guarantee it gets written.

The one idea that organises the whole question

SUA exists because piracy is defined too narrowly. Piracy needs private ends and two ships. The Achille Lauro attackers had political ends and were already on board as passengers - so on the law as it then stood, no piracy, and a serious argument about who could try them. Everything in this question descends from that single gap: the 1988 offences fill it, the jurisdiction and extradite-or-prosecute machinery makes it enforceable, and the 2005 Protocol widens it again when the threat changed shape.

For limb (b), hold the shift in one line: 1988 - the ship is the TARGET. 2005 - the ship is the WEAPON or the DELIVERY VEHICLE. Every one of the new offences and the boarding regime can be reasoned from that sentence.

The dates, which are the easy marks

  • Achille Lauro seized October 1985 - the trigger.
  • SUA Convention adopted at Rome 10 March 1988, in force 1 March 1992. A companion Protocol of the same date covers fixed platforms on the continental shelf.
  • 2005 Protocols adopted in London 14 October 2005, in force 28 July 2010.
  • Both are in force at the date of this paper by a wide margin - there is no adopted-but-not-in-force problem on this question, which is unusual and worth saying.

Common mistakes

  • Treating SUA as an anti-piracy convention. It exists precisely because the conduct it covers is not piracy.
  • Confusing it with the ISPS Code. ISPS is preventive and sits under SOLAS chapter XI-2; SUA is criminal law and applies after the event.
  • Saying the Convention lets a warship board any suspect ship. Article 8bis is a flag State consent procedure, not a right of unilateral boarding.
  • Forgetting the qualifier 'likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship' on the 1988 offences. It is the boundary of the whole instrument.
  • Listing the offences and never answering how the amendments reflect evolving security concerns.
  • Omitting the transport offences from 2005. They are the most significant single addition, because the offence is complete without any violence.
  • Inventing article numbers. Give the ones you are sure of and describe the rest.
  • Quoting a number of States Parties. It changes constantly and cannot be verified in an examination.

Examiner traps

  • Why was SUA needed when piracy was already an international crime? Because piracy requires private ends and two ships. This is the discriminating answer.
  • Does SUA apply to a ship in port? The Convention is framed around navigation and applies where the ship is navigating or scheduled to navigate into, through or from waters beyond the territorial sea of a single State. Do not claim it covers every event on every ship everywhere.
  • Who prosecutes? Any State Party with jurisdiction; and the State where the offender is found must extradite or submit the case to its own authorities - Article 10.
  • Can I board a suspect ship on the high seas under SUA? Only with the flag State's authorisation, which may be given in advance or deemed after four hours if that State has so notified. The flag State's primacy is preserved.
  • What is the single biggest change made in 2005? Criminalising transport of BCN weapons and related dual-use material - the ship becomes a proliferation route, and no violence need occur.
  • Is SUA self-executing? No. Each State Party must make the offences punishable in its own law, which is where the practical weakness lies.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What incident led to the SUA Convention?
  • Why was that incident not piracy?
  • Name four offences under the 1988 Convention.
  • What does aut dedere aut judicare mean?
  • What did the 2005 Protocol add, and why?
  • What does BCN stand for?
  • As Master, a warship requests to board on suspicion of a SUA offence. What is the position?
  • How does SUA differ from the ISPS Code?
  • Is there a companion instrument for offshore installations?

Memory framework

Three blocks. (a) WHY, WHAT, WHY IT MATTERS - the piracy gap and Achille Lauro (1), purpose and the three-part mechanism (2), importance and the contrast with ISPS (3). (b) OFFENCES - 1988 (4), then 2005 new offences (5), then boarding (6). (b) ANALYSIS - the shift of fear (7).

For the 1988 offence list use SEIZE - VIOLENCE - DESTROY - PLACE - FACILITIES - FALSE INFORMATION - KILL, and remember that all of them hang on 'likely to endanger safe navigation'. For 2005 use USE - DISCHARGE - TRANSPORT - HARBOUR: use the ship as a weapon, discharge something lethal from it, transport a BCN weapon or dual-use material, or carry a fugitive offender.

Uncertainty and the evidence position

This is the weakest evidence position on the paper and it is declared rather than concealed. The MIW True Source corpus holds neither the SUA Convention 1988 nor either of the 2005 Protocols. Every treaty statement above is therefore authoritative restatement, classified P2, not primary. No article is quoted anywhere.

Article identities are given only where the answer can carry them honestly - Article 3 for the 1988 offences, Articles 3bis, 3ter and 3quater for the 2005 additions, Article 8bis for boarding and Article 10 for prosecute-or-extradite. No other article number is asserted, and none has been invented to make the answer look better sourced than it is. Acquiring the consolidated 2005 texts would promote this whole answer to primary, and that is recorded as an acquisition need.

Two further omissions are deliberate. No number of States Parties, no tonnage coverage figure and no prosecution statistic is given - party counts move continuously and MIW holds no status list, so any figure stated as at November 2024 would be unverifiable. And no Indian implementing statute is named; the question does not ask for one, the corpus supports none, and inventing a statutory reference would be worse than omitting it.

On currency: this is one of the few questions on the paper with no 2024 boundary at all. Both instruments were in force long before the sitting - by thirty-two and fourteen years respectively - so there is nothing here that a later or earlier sitting would change. The answer is stable across sittings, which is itself worth knowing.

Flashcards 8

Limb (a): 1 the gap - why it is not piracy, and the Achille Lauro. 2 purpose and the three-part mechanism. 3 importance, and the ISPS contrast. Limb (b): 4 the 1988 offences. 5 the 2005 new offences. 6 the Article 8bis boarding regime. 7 how the amendments reflect evolving security concerns.

The 6/10 split puts the weight in limb (b), and step 7 exists to guarantee the analytical demand is answered rather than crowded out by the offence list.

Because piracy is defined too narrowly. It requires an act committed for private ends and it requires two ships - one attacking another. The Achille Lauro attackers acted for political ends and were already lawfully on board as passengers, so no piracy was committed and no State had clear universal jurisdiction. SUA creates offences that do not depend on motive or on a second ship.

The single most discriminating fact in the answer, and the reason the Convention exists at all.

Unlawfully and intentionally: seizing or exercising control over a ship by force, threat or intimidation; performing an act of violence against a person on board if likely to endanger safe navigation; destroying a ship or damaging it or its cargo so as to endanger safe navigation; placing on board a device or substance likely to destroy or damage it; destroying or seriously damaging maritime navigational facilities or seriously interfering with their operation; communicating information known to be false and thereby endangering safe navigation; and injuring or killing any person in connection with any of these. Attempt, abetting and threat are also covered.

Seven limbs, and they are countable marks. Note that almost every one is qualified by 'likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship'.

Prosecute or extradite. Under Article 10, a State Party in whose territory an alleged offender is found must either extradite him or, without exception and without undue delay, submit the case to its own competent authorities for the purpose of prosecution. This is the mechanism that removes the safe haven, and it is what makes the Convention effective rather than declaratory.

The heart of the Convention's purpose limb, and a standard oral follow-up.

The ISPS Code, under SOLAS chapter XI-2, is a preventive regime: security levels, ship and port facility security plans, security officers, access control and assessments, aimed at stopping an attack happening. The SUA Convention is criminal law: it defines offences, requires States Parties to criminalise them, allocates jurisdiction and obliges prosecution or extradition after the event. One keeps the attacker off the ship; the other ensures that if he gets on, he is tried.

The two are constantly confused, and the contrast is worth marks in limb (a)'s importance demand.

Principally at Article 3bis, offences committed unlawfully and intentionally where the purpose is to intimidate a population or compel a Government or international organisation: using a ship as a weapon or discharging from it any explosive, radioactive material or biological, chemical or nuclear weapon so as to cause death, serious injury or damage; discharging oil, LNG or other hazardous or noxious substance in lethal quantity; using the ship in a manner causing death or serious injury; and transporting a BCN weapon or explosive or radioactive material, or dual-use equipment, materials, software or technology contributing to a BCN weapon. Article 3ter criminalises transporting a person known to have committed a SUA offence, and Article 3quater covers attempt, threat, participation, organising and directing.

The transport offences are the most significant addition, because the offence is complete without any violence at all.

Under Article 8bis the requesting State must seek the authorisation of your flag State. The flag State may authorise the boarding, may board itself, may board jointly, or may refuse. It may also have notified the Secretary-General in advance that it gives standing authorisation, or that authorisation is deemed granted if it does not respond within four hours. Safeguards apply: safety of life at sea, the security and commercial interests of the ship, humane treatment, and liability for damage if the suspicion proves unfounded. There is no right of unilateral boarding - flag State primacy is preserved.

The practical question a Chief Engineer or Master could actually face, and the point on which candidates most often overstate the Convention.

In 1988 the ship was the target and the fear was hijacking for leverage, so every offence is tied to endangering the safe navigation of that ship; by 2005 the fear was terrorism and proliferation, in which the ship is the weapon or the delivery vehicle and the intended victims are ashore - so the protected object widens from the ship to the population, mere transport of weapons or dual-use material becomes criminal without any violence, and the treaty moves from prosecuting after the event to interdicting before it.

This is the printed analytical demand in limb (b) and it is what most candidates never reach.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Why it exists. Piracy needs private ends and two ships, so the Achille Lauro attack of October 1985 - political motive, attackers already on board - was not piracy, and nobody was clearly entitled to prosecute. Instrument. SUA Convention, adopted Rome 10 March 1988, in force 1 March 1992; companion Protocol on fixed platforms. Purpose. Ensure appropriate action against unlawful acts against safe navigation, by closing jurisdictional gaps: define offences, require Parties to criminalise them, establish jurisdiction, and prosecute or extradite (Article 10). Importance. No safe haven; conduct becomes crime; reaches beyond piracy; and complements ISPS under SOLAS XI-2 - ISPS prevents, SUA punishes. 1988 offences (Article 3). SEIZE a ship by force; VIOLENCE against a person on board; DESTROY/DAMAGE ship or cargo; PLACE a device or substance; damage NAVIGATIONAL FACILITIES; FALSE INFORMATION; INJURE OR KILL in connection - all 'likely to endanger safe navigation'. 2005 Protocol, adopted 14 October 2005, in force 28 July 2010. Article 3bis - using the ship as a weapon; discharging explosive, radioactive material or a BCN weapon; discharging oil/LNG/HNS lethally; TRANSPORTING BCN weapons or dual-use material. 3ter - carrying a fugitive offender. 3quater - attempt, threat, participation. Article 8bis - boarding with FLAG STATE authorisation, advance consent or deemed after four hours, with safeguards. The shift. 1988 ship = TARGET; 2005 ship = WEAPON or DELIVERY VEHICLE - protection widens to the public, transport alone becomes criminal, and the treaty moves from prosecution to interdiction.

Critical numbers

  • SUA Convention adopted Rome 10 March 1988; entered into force 1 March 1992
  • 2005 Protocols adopted London 14 October 2005; entered into force 28 July 2010
  • Article 3 - the 1988 offences; Articles 3bis, 3ter and 3quater - the 2005 offences
  • Article 8bis - ship boarding; Article 10 - prosecute or extradite
  • Four hours - the period after which flag State authorisation to board may be deemed given, where that State has so notified
  • No number of States Parties is quoted - it changes continuously and cannot be verified
Critical regulation
Article 3 of the SUA Convention 1988, which creates the offences and ties them to acts likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship, and Article 10, which obliges the State in whose territory the offender is found either to extradite him or to submit the case for prosecution. In the 2005 Protocol, Article 3bis creates the terrorism and proliferation offences and Article 8bis creates the flag-State-consent boarding procedure. MIW holds no copy of these instruments; they are cited by identity and nothing is quoted.
Major trap
Answering this as an anti-piracy question. SUA exists precisely because the conduct is not piracy - piracy requires private ends and two ships. The second trap is claiming Article 8bis permits a warship to board any suspect ship; it is a flag State consent procedure and flag State primacy is preserved. The third is listing offences for the whole of limb (b) and never answering the printed words 'how do these amendments reflect evolving security concerns', which is where the analytical marks are.

Understand this first

Salvage law has always run on a single hard bargain: no cure, no pay. Save the property and you get a reward out of it; save nothing and you get nothing, however heroic the attempt. That bargain worked for centuries because the only thing at risk was property. Then tankers got large. When a laden tanker goes ashore, the thing society most wants is a professional salvor alongside her fast - but that is precisely the case where the ship and her cargo are most likely to be a total loss, and under the old rule a total loss meant no salved fund and therefore no reward at all. So the law was paying salvors to attend the casualties that mattered least and paying them nothing to attend the ones that mattered most. The 1989 Convention is the fix, and it works on two levels, which is why the examiner named two articles. Article 13 keeps the reward but changes what it is calculated on - it adds the salvor's environmental effort to the list of things he is paid for, so protecting the coast makes him more money when he succeeds. Article 14 adds a floor - if there was an environmental threat and the reward comes to less than his costs, the shipowner must cover his expenses anyway, with a bonus if he actually prevented damage. One rewards success, the other underwrites the attempt. Hold that and the numbers, the caps and even SCOPIC all follow.

Model written answer

1. (a) What the Convention is, and the failure it was written to cure

The International Convention on Salvage, 1989 was adopted in London on 28 April 1989 and entered into force on 14 July 1996, replacing the Brussels Convention of 1910. The 1910 regime rested on one commercial rule - no cure, no pay: a salvor who produced no useful result earned nothing, however hard or however skilfully he worked.

That rule failed in exactly the case where society most needed a salvor. When a laden tanker went ashore and broke up, as in the Torrey Canyon and Amoco Cadiz class of casualty, the ship and cargo were lost, so there was no salved fund and no reward - yet the salvor's presence might have prevented an enormous pollution incident. The economics therefore discouraged the professional salvor from attending the most environmentally dangerous casualties, and encouraged him to leave once the property looked unsavable. The 1989 Convention exists to correct that.

2. (a) The primary objectives

Four, and they should be stated as such:

  1. To provide uniform international rules on salvage operations, replacing the 1910 Convention.
  2. To preserve the incentive to salve - the reward regime is retained, and enlarged, so that professional salvors remain available and equipped.
  3. To make protection of the environment an object of salvage law in its own right, rather than an incidental benefit - both by making environmental effort a criterion of the reward and by creating a separate entitlement where no reward is earned.
  4. To impose duties on the parties - the salvor to exercise due care and to prevent or minimise damage to the environment, and the owner and master to co-operate, to exercise the same care, and to accept redelivery.

Article 12 retains no cure, no pay as the general rule; Article 14 is the deliberate exception to it.

3. (a) How it balances the interests of salvors and owners

The balance is struck by giving each side what it most needs and capping what it fears. The salvor gets a reward fixed on criteria that reward skill, risk, promptness and readiness - not merely outcome - so that maintaining expensive standing capability is economically rational; and he gets a safety net under Article 14 so that attending a dangerous, low-value casualty does not bankrupt him. The owner and his underwriters get certainty: the Article 13 reward can never exceed the salved value, so liability is bounded by what was actually saved; special compensation is measured on expenses rather than as a share of value, so it cannot become a windfall; it is payable only to the extent it exceeds the Article 13 reward, so the salvor cannot be paid twice; and a negligent salvor may be deprived of it. The Convention also leaves the parties free to contract, which is why Lloyd's Open Form remains the usual instrument - the Convention sets the floor, not the whole bargain.

4. (b) Article 13 - the criteria for fixing the reward

Article 13 requires the reward to be fixed with a view to encouraging salvage operations, taking the following criteria into account without regard to the order in which they are presented:

  1. the salved value of the vessel and other property;
  2. the skill and efforts of the salvors in preventing or minimising damage to the environment;
  3. the measure of success obtained by the salvor;
  4. the nature and degree of the danger;
  5. the skill and efforts of the salvors in salving the vessel, other property and life;
  6. the time used and the expenses and losses incurred by the salvors;
  7. the risk of liability and other risks run by the salvors or their equipment;
  8. the promptness of the services rendered;
  9. the availability and use of vessels or other equipment intended for salvage operations; and
  10. the state of readiness, efficiency and value of the salvor's equipment.

Two features carry marks. The list is not ranked - salved value is listed first but is not the dominant factor. And criterion two, environmental effort, sits inside the ordinary reward. That is the 1989 Convention's first environmental mechanism, and it is separate from and prior to Article 14: a salvor who protects the environment is paid more even where the salvage succeeds.

5. (b) Article 13 - apportionment, and the cap

The reward is paid by the vessel and other property interests in proportion to their respective salved values - so ship and cargo contribute according to what each had saved. And the reward, exclusive of interest and recoverable legal costs, shall not exceed the salved value of the vessel and other property. That cap is the owner's and the underwriter's protection, and it is precisely why Article 14 had to be created: where salved value is nil or trivial, a cap expressed as a proportion of it is worth nothing.

6. (b) Article 14 - special compensation

Article 14 applies where the salvor has carried out operations in respect of a vessel which by itself or its cargo threatened damage to the environment and has failed to earn a reward under Article 13 at least equivalent to the special compensation assessable. He is then entitled to special compensation from the owner of the vessel, equivalent to his expenses.

  • The uplift. If the salvor has by his operations actually prevented or minimised damage to the environment, the special compensation may be increased by up to 30 per cent of his expenses; and a tribunal, if it thinks it fair and just, may increase it further - but never beyond 100 per cent of the expenses incurred.
  • What expenses means. The out-of-pocket expenses reasonably incurred in the operation, plus a fair rate for equipment and personnel actually and reasonably used.
  • The offset. Special compensation is payable only if, and to the extent that, it exceeds any reward recoverable under Article 13. It is a safety net, not a second payment.
  • Forfeiture. The salvor may be deprived of the whole or part of it if he was negligent and has thereby failed to prevent or minimise damage to the environment.

Note who pays. The Article 13 reward comes from ship and cargo in proportion to salved values; special compensation comes from the shipowner alone - which in practice means his P&I club, whereas the reward falls largely on hull and cargo underwriters.

7. (b) Why the two articles matter together

Their significance is that together they made environmental protection commercially rational. Article 13 pays a salvor more for protecting the environment when he succeeds; Article 14 pays him something for trying when he fails. The gap between them - the case of the dangerous, low-value wreck - was exactly the gap that had kept salvors away. The Convention thereby converted an environmental duty into an economic incentive without abandoning no cure, no pay as the general rule.

The practical sequel is worth a line. Article 14's fair rate was construed as covering expenses without any profit element, which made the entitlement unattractive and made every claim a lengthy argument about what had been spent. The industry's answer was contractual, not legislative: the SCOPIC clause, incorporated into Lloyd's Open Form, which substitutes an agreed tariff for the Article 14 assessment and can be invoked by the salvor regardless of environmental threat, with the shipowner providing security. SCOPIC is a contractual mechanism and forms no part of the Convention - but it is the reason Article 14 rarely has to be assessed in practice today.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 6 and 10. Limb (a) asks two things - the primary objectives, and how the Convention balances the interests of salvors and vessel owners. Limb (b) asks three - the significance of Articles 13 and 14, the criteria for determining the reward under Article 13, and the provisions of Article 14 on special compensation.

The word significance is doing real work and it is routinely ignored. It is not asking what the articles say - the rest of the limb asks that. It is asking why they matter, which means why the two together changed the economics of salvage. Step 7 exists so that demand is answered.

The one idea that organises the whole question

No cure, no pay fails exactly where the environment needs a salvor most. Under the 1910 regime, a salvor was paid out of what he saved. If the tanker broke up and the cargo was lost, there was nothing saved, so there was nothing to pay him from - even if his presence had prevented a far worse spill. The rational salvor therefore stayed away from the most dangerous casualties, or left once the property looked unsavable.

Everything in the 1989 Convention follows from fixing that. Article 13 pays you more for protecting the environment when you succeed. Article 14 pays you something for trying when you fail. If you can write those two sentences you can reconstruct the rest of the answer.

Get the two articles apart - the commonest confusion on this question

Article 13 rewardArticle 14 special compensation
TriggerA useful result - property salvedThreat of damage to the environment, and reward less than the compensation assessable
Measured onTen criteria, including salved valueThe salvor's EXPENSES
CeilingCannot exceed the salved valueExpenses, plus up to 30 per cent uplift, and never beyond 100 per cent
Paid byShip AND cargo, in proportion to salved valuesThe SHIPOWNER alone
Insurer in practiceHull and cargo underwritersThe P&I club
RelationshipThe primary entitlementA safety net - payable only to the extent it EXCEEDS the Article 13 reward

If you remember only one row, remember who pays. The reward comes out of the salved property; special compensation comes out of the shipowner's pocket. That single distinction explains why the two are argued about so fiercely and why SCOPIC exists.

The numbers that must be exact

  • Adopted 28 April 1989, in force 14 July 1996, replacing the 1910 Brussels Convention.
  • Ten criteria in Article 13, taken without regard to the order in which they are presented.
  • The Article 13 reward shall not exceed the salved value of vessel and other property.
  • Article 14 uplift: up to 30 per cent of expenses where environmental damage was prevented or minimised; a tribunal may go further but never beyond 100 per cent.
  • Expenses = out-of-pocket expenses reasonably incurred plus a fair rate for equipment and personnel actually and reasonably used.

Common mistakes

  • Saying the 1989 Convention abolished no cure, no pay. It did not. Article 12 keeps it as the general rule; Article 14 is a defined exception.
  • Treating Article 14 as an addition to the Article 13 reward. It is payable only to the extent it exceeds the reward.
  • Forgetting that environmental effort is already a criterion under Article 13. Candidates routinely present Article 13 as purely commercial and Article 14 as the only green provision. It is the second criterion in the list.
  • Giving the 30 per cent as the absolute maximum. The tribunal may go beyond it, up to 100 per cent of expenses.
  • Saying special compensation is paid by ship and cargo. It is paid by the owner of the vessel.
  • Presenting SCOPIC as part of the Convention. It is a clause in Lloyd's Open Form - industry contract, not treaty.
  • Ranking the Article 13 criteria, or saying salved value is the dominant one. The article says the criteria are taken without regard to their order.
  • Quoting an award figure or a percentage of salved value from a decided case. Awards are fact-specific.

Examiner traps

  • Did 1989 end no cure, no pay? No - it created a bounded exception for environmental cases.
  • Which article protects the environment? Both. Article 13 through the second criterion; Article 14 through the separate entitlement. Answering 'Article 14' alone is a half mark.
  • Who pays special compensation, and which insurer? The shipowner, and in practice his P&I club - not the hull and cargo underwriters who fund the reward.
  • What is the maximum special compensation? Expenses plus an uplift of up to 30 per cent, which a tribunal may increase but never beyond 100 per cent of expenses.
  • Why was SCOPIC needed if Article 14 existed? Because 'fair rate' was construed as expenses without a profit element, so the entitlement was unattractive and every claim became an argument about quantum.
  • Can a salvor lose special compensation? Yes - he may be deprived of all or part of it if his negligence has failed to prevent or minimise environmental damage.
  • Does the reward have a ceiling? Yes - the salved value of the vessel and other property. That ceiling is why Article 14 had to exist at all.

Likely oral follow-up

  • When was the Salvage Convention adopted and when did it come into force?
  • What did it replace?
  • State five of the Article 13 criteria.
  • What is the ceiling on a salvage reward?
  • What triggers an entitlement to special compensation?
  • What are the two percentages in Article 14 and what do they mean?
  • Who pays the reward, and who pays the special compensation?
  • What is Lloyd's Open Form, and what is SCOPIC?
  • As Chief Engineer, what duties does the Convention place on your ship's side?

Memory framework

Two limbs, seven steps. Limb (a) - THE FAILURE (1), the objectives (2), the balance (3). Limb (b) - CRITERIA (4), the cap (5), SPECIAL COMPENSATION (6), and why the two matter together (7).

For the ten Article 13 criteria, group them in three: WHAT WAS AT STAKE - salved value, environmental effort, measure of success, nature and degree of danger. WHAT THE SALVOR PUT IN - skill and efforts, time and expenses, risk run, promptness. WHAT HE HAD READY - availability and use of salvage craft, and the state, efficiency and value of his equipment. Four, four, two.

For Article 14 use E-30-100: Expenses, uplift up to 30 per cent, tribunal may go to 100 per cent, never beyond.

Uncertainty and the evidence position

The MIW True Source corpus does not hold the International Convention on Salvage, 1989. Every treaty statement above is therefore authoritative restatement, classified P2, not primary, and no article is quoted anywhere. Articles 13 and 14 are named because the examiner named them; the three further identities used - Article 12 for no cure, no pay, Article 8 for the duties and Article 1 for the environmental definition - are given as identities only, and no other article number has been invented. Acquiring the Convention would promote this whole answer to primary, and that is recorded as an acquisition need.

Three deliberate omissions. No SCOPIC edition year, tariff rate or uplift percentage is given: MIW holds no copy of the clause, it has been revised more than once, and an edition stated as current at November 2024 could not be verified. No salvage award figure or percentage of salved value from any decided case is quoted, because awards are fact-specific and a general figure would be false precision. And no case is cited by name for the construction of 'fair rate'; the proposition is given as the settled understanding that made SCOPIC necessary, which is all the answer needs, because MIW holds no law report.

On currency: this is one of the few questions on the paper with no 2024 boundary at all. The Convention entered into force twenty-eight years before this sitting and Articles 13 and 14 are unamended, so nothing here would change at an earlier or later sitting in the same period. Note only that the surrounding contractual layer - LOF and SCOPIC - does move, which is precisely why no edition is asserted.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) (a) What the Convention is, and the failure it was written to cure
  2. __________a) (a) The primary objectives
  3. __________a) (a) How it balances the interests of salvors and owners
  4. __________b) (b) Article 13 - the criteria for fixing the reward
  5. __________b) (b) Article 13 - apportionment, and the cap
  6. __________b) (b) Article 14 - special compensation
  7. __________b) (b) Why the two articles matter together
Flashcards 8

Limb (a): 1 the commercial failure of no cure, no pay. 2 the four primary objectives. 3 how the interests are balanced. Limb (b): 4 the ten Article 13 criteria. 5 apportionment and the salved-value cap. 6 Article 14 special compensation. 7 why the two articles matter together.

The printed word 'significance' in limb (b) is answered by step 7, and it is the demand candidates most often crowd out with description.

No. Article 12 retains it as the general rule - salvage operations which have had a useful result give a right to a reward, and no payment is due if they have had no useful result. Article 14 is a bounded exception for cases where the vessel or its cargo threatened damage to the environment. The Convention modified the bargain; it did not abandon it.

The single most common error on this question.

Ten, taken without regard to the order in which they are presented: the salved value of the vessel and other property; the skill and efforts of the salvors in preventing or minimising damage to the environment; the measure of success obtained; the nature and degree of the danger; the skill and efforts in salving the vessel, other property and life; the time used and the expenses and losses incurred; the risk of liability and other risks run; the promptness of the services; the availability and use of vessels or other equipment intended for salvage; and the state of readiness, efficiency and value of the salvor's equipment.

The examiner named the article, so a partial list reads as partial knowledge. Note that the criteria are expressly unranked.

The reward, exclusive of interest and recoverable legal costs, shall not exceed the salved value of the vessel and other property. It is paid by the vessel and the other property interests in proportion to their respective salved values, so ship and cargo each contribute according to what was saved for them. That ceiling is the owner's and underwriter's protection - and it is exactly why Article 14 was necessary, because a cap expressed as a share of a nil salved value is worth nothing.

The cap is the hinge between the two articles; explaining it is what makes the Article 14 answer coherent.

Where the salvor has worked on a vessel which by itself or its cargo threatened damage to the environment and has failed to earn a reward at least equivalent to the special compensation assessable, he is entitled to special compensation from the owner of the vessel equal to his expenses. If he actually prevented or minimised damage to the environment it may be increased by up to 30 per cent of those expenses, and a tribunal may go further if fair and just but never beyond 100 per cent. Expenses means out-of-pocket costs reasonably incurred plus a fair rate for equipment and personnel actually and reasonably used. It is payable only to the extent it exceeds the Article 13 reward, and may be forfeited in whole or part for salvor negligence.

Six components. The two percentages and the offset are the marks most often lost.

The Article 13 reward is paid by the vessel and the other property interests in proportion to their salved values, so it falls in practice on hull and cargo underwriters. Special compensation under Article 14 is paid by the owner of the vessel alone, and falls in practice on his P&I club. It is not shared with cargo.

The distinction that explains why the two articles are argued over so fiercely, and why SCOPIC security is a P&I matter.

Both. Article 13 does it inside the ordinary reward, because the salvor's skill and efforts in preventing or minimising damage to the environment is the second of the ten criteria - so environmental effort increases the reward even where the salvage succeeds. Article 14 does it by creating a separate entitlement where no adequate reward is earned. Answering Article 14 alone misses half of the Convention's environmental mechanism.

A very common half-answer, and the examiner's natural probe on a question that names both articles.

Lloyd's Open Form is the standard no-cure-no-pay salvage contract under which the Convention regime is most often operated. SCOPIC is a clause incorporated into it which replaces the Article 14 assessment with an agreed tariff, can be invoked by the salvor regardless of environmental threat, and is secured by the shipowner. Both are industry contract and form no part of the Convention. SCOPIC exists because the Article 14 'fair rate' was construed as covering expenses without a profit element, which made the statutory entitlement unattractive and every claim an argument about quantum.

Explains the practical significance of Article 14, but must be labelled as contract - presenting SCOPIC as treaty is a clear error.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Instrument. International Convention on Salvage, adopted London 28 April 1989, in force 14 July 1996, replacing the 1910 Brussels Convention. The failure it cured. Under no cure, no pay a salvor was paid out of what he saved - so where the tanker broke up there was no fund and no reward, and salvors stayed away from the most environmentally dangerous casualties. Objectives. Uniform rules; preserve the incentive to salve; make environmental protection an object of salvage law; impose duties on salvor, owner and master. Article 12 keeps no cure, no pay; Article 14 is the exception. Balance. Salvor gets reward for skill, risk, promptness, readiness, plus a safety net; owner gets a cap at salved value, a compensation measured on expenses not value, an offset so no double payment, and forfeiture for a negligent salvor. Article 13 - TEN criteria, unranked: salved value; skill and efforts in preventing or minimising environmental damage; measure of success; nature and degree of danger; skill and efforts in salving; time, expenses and losses; risk of liability; promptness; availability and use of salvage craft; state of readiness, efficiency and value of equipment. Paid by ship and cargo in proportion to salved values; shall not exceed the salved value. Article 14 - special compensation. Trigger: vessel or cargo threatened damage to the environment and the reward is less than the compensation assessable. Paid by the SHIPOWNER alone, equal to expenses; uplift up to 30 per cent if damage actually prevented or minimised; tribunal may go further but never beyond 100 per cent. Expenses = out-of-pocket plus a fair rate for equipment and personnel actually and reasonably used. Payable only to the extent it exceeds the Article 13 reward; forfeitable for negligence. Significance. 13 pays you more for succeeding greenly; 14 pays you something for trying. 'Fair rate' meant expenses without profit, so the industry wrote SCOPIC into LOF - contract, not treaty.

Critical numbers
  • Adopted London 28 April 1989; entered into force 14 July 1996; replaced the Brussels Convention of 1910
  • Article 13 lists TEN criteria, taken without regard to the order in which they are presented
  • The Article 13 reward shall NOT EXCEED the salved value of the vessel and other property
  • Article 14 uplift: up to 30 per cent of expenses where environmental damage was prevented or minimised
  • A tribunal may increase it further if fair and just, but NEVER BEYOND 100 PER CENT of the expenses incurred
  • Special compensation is payable only if, and to the extent that, it EXCEEDS the Article 13 reward
Critical regulation
Articles 13 and 14 of the International Convention on Salvage, 1989. Article 13 fixes the reward on ten unranked criteria - the second of which is the salvor's skill and efforts in preventing or minimising damage to the environment - paid by ship and cargo in proportion to salved values and capped at the salved value. Article 14 gives special compensation from the shipowner alone, measured on expenses, with an uplift of up to 30 per cent and a tribunal discretion never exceeding 100 per cent, payable only to the extent it exceeds the reward. MIW holds no copy of the Convention; both are cited by identity and nothing is quoted.
Major trap
Saying the 1989 Convention abolished no cure, no pay. It did not - Article 12 retains it and Article 14 is a bounded exception. The second trap is treating special compensation as an addition to the reward; it is payable only to the extent it exceeds it. The third is presenting Article 14 as the only environmental provision: environmental effort is already the second of the ten Article 13 criteria. The fourth is giving 30 per cent as the absolute maximum - a tribunal may go beyond it, up to 100 per cent of expenses.

Understand this first

There are exactly two ways a shipping company is destroyed by a single event. It can lose its ship, or it can owe more money than it is worth. Those are completely different problems, and the market solved them in completely different ways - which is the whole content of this question. Losing the ship is an insurable thing: a vessel has a value, that value can be agreed in advance, and a commercial underwriter can price the chance of losing it and make a profit. So hull and machinery cover looks like any other property insurance - a premium, an agreed value, a deductible, standard clauses. Owing money to other people is not like that at all. You cannot value in advance what a pollution incident, a passenger casualty or a lost cargo will cost, and a liability that might be enormous cannot be sensibly written for a fixed premium by someone trying to make a margin on it. So shipowners did the only thing that works: they insured each other. A P&I club is not an insurance company - it is the members, pooling their liabilities, paying calls instead of premiums, writing their own rules, and grouping together internationally so that even a catastrophic claim can be carried. Once you see that the structure follows from the nature of the risk, every difference in the comparison stops being a list to memorise: mutual because unlimited, calls because unpredictable, rules because self-made, and a service organisation because the members want their own problems solved rather than merely paid for.

Knowledge map
P&I and H&M Insurance Compared
  1. a)
    1. 1(a) What hull and machinery insurance is
      • insurance of the ship herself - a property cover on the owner's own asset
      • indemnifies physical loss of and damage to hull, machinery, equipment and fittings
      • placed in the commercial market for a fixed premium, usually for twelve months
    2. 2(a) What P&I insurance is
      • insurance of the owner's legal liabilities to third parties arising from operating the ship
      • written by mutual associations - clubs - owned by the shipowner members themselves
      • each member is simultaneously insurer and assured; no shareholders and no profit motive
    3. 3(a) The main differences in coverage and purpose
      • H&M insures a thing; P&I insures a liability
      • first-party loss against third-party loss
      • commercial underwriter for profit against mutual club owned by members
    4. 4(a) Why a shipowner needs both
      • neither covers the other's field, and each exposure alone can destroy the business
      • without hull, a total loss destroys the asset and the mortgagee's security
      • mortgagees therefore require hull cover as a condition of lending
  2. b)
    1. 5(b) Typical risks covered by H&M, and when it responds
      • perils of the seas, fire and explosion, violent theft, jettison, piracy, contact damage
      • Inchmaree or negligence clause - boilers, shafts, latent defect, crew, pilot and repairer negligence
      • subject to due diligence by the assured, and to the deductible
    2. 6(b) Typical risks covered by P&I, and when it responds
      • crew illness, injury, death, repatriation, wages and substitutes
      • passengers and other persons; cargo loss, damage and shortage
      • pollution liability and clean-up - the one major head carrying a specific monetary cap
    3. 7(b) The boundary - where both are engaged at once
      • a collision produces two different losses from one event
      • damage to your own ship is hull; liability to the other ship is split
      • traditionally three-fourths hull and one-fourth club, plus any excess above the hull limit
    4. 8(b) Their roles in managing liabilities and operational risk
      • H&M manages capital risk - it converts catastrophic asset loss into a predictable premium
      • it protects the mortgagee's security and keeps an unfundable repair from becoming insolvency
      • its discipline is the deductible, and the assumption that the ship is in class and properly manned

Remember8 route headings
Cover53 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 53 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. (a) What hull and machinery insurance is
  • insurance of the ship herself - a property cover on the owner's own asset
  • indemnifies physical loss of and damage to hull, machinery, equipment and fittings
  • placed in the commercial market for a fixed premium, usually for twelve months
  • written on an agreed value and subject to a deductible
  • on standard forms - Institute Time Clauses Hulls, International Hull Clauses, Nordic Plan
  • the insurer is a commercial insurer writing for profit
2. (a) What P&I insurance is
  • insurance of the owner's legal liabilities to third parties arising from operating the ship
  • written by mutual associations - clubs - owned by the shipowner members themselves
  • each member is simultaneously insurer and assured; no shareholders and no profit motive
  • funded by calls - advance, supplementary and overspill - not by premium
  • cover defined by club rules, with a Board discretion for claims outside them
  • the International Group gives retention, a claims pool, collective market reinsurance and overspill
3. (a) The main differences in coverage and purpose
  • H&M insures a thing; P&I insures a liability
  • first-party loss against third-party loss
  • commercial underwriter for profit against mutual club owned by members
  • premium against calls; policy on market forms against club rules
  • limited by the agreed value against effectively unlimited, save capped heads such as oil pollution
  • purpose - protect the capital asset, against protect solvency
4. (a) Why a shipowner needs both
  • neither covers the other's field, and each exposure alone can destroy the business
  • without hull, a total loss destroys the asset and the mortgagee's security
  • mortgagees therefore require hull cover as a condition of lending
  • without P&I, liabilities are not bounded by the ship's value at all
  • pollution, passenger and large cargo claims can exceed the vessel's worth many times
  • and club entry supports the compulsory-insurance certification without which the ship cannot trade
5. (b) Typical risks covered by H&M, and when it responds
  • perils of the seas, fire and explosion, violent theft, jettison, piracy, contact damage
  • Inchmaree or negligence clause - boilers, shafts, latent defect, crew, pilot and repairer negligence
  • subject to due diligence by the assured, and to the deductible
  • total loss actual or constructive, and particular average
  • the ship's contribution to general average and salvage, and sue and labour
  • collision liability to the other ship, traditionally three-fourths
  • incidents - crankshaft failure, grounding damage, engine-room fire, constructive total loss
6. (b) Typical risks covered by P&I, and when it responds
  • crew illness, injury, death, repatriation, wages and substitutes
  • passengers and other persons; cargo loss, damage and shortage
  • pollution liability and clean-up - the one major head carrying a specific monetary cap
  • wreck removal where compulsory; damage to fixed and floating objects
  • the collision liability the hull policy does not carry, and any excess
  • fines, quarantine, diversion, stowaways, life salvage, towage liabilities and legal costs
  • incidents - bunker overflow, injured oiler, condemned reefer cargo, demolished berth crane
7. (b) The boundary - where both are engaged at once
  • a collision produces two different losses from one event
  • damage to your own ship is hull; liability to the other ship is split
  • traditionally three-fourths hull and one-fourth club, plus any excess above the hull limit
  • modern forms may write collision liability at four-fourths by agreement
  • if oil is spilled, the pollution liability is entirely the club's
  • in general average the ship's contribution is a hull claim
  • and in salvage the reward falls on salved property while special compensation falls on the club
8. (b) Their roles in managing liabilities and operational risk
  • H&M manages capital risk - it converts catastrophic asset loss into a predictable premium
  • it protects the mortgagee's security and keeps an unfundable repair from becoming insolvency
  • its discipline is the deductible, and the assumption that the ship is in class and properly manned
  • P&I manages liability risk and is also a service organisation
  • correspondents in every port, lawyers and surveyors, security to lift an arrest
  • certification support, and loss-prevention guidance drawn from the whole entered fleet
  • its discipline is underwriting the member rather than the risk, and the pay-to-be-paid principle
  • together they cover the two ways a shipping company fails - losing the ship, or owing more than it is worth
Model written answer

1. (a) What hull and machinery insurance is

H&M is insurance of the ship herself. It is a property cover: it indemnifies the owner for physical loss of or damage to the hull, machinery, equipment and fittings, and for certain associated liabilities. It is placed in the commercial market - Lloyd's, company underwriters or the Nordic market - for a fixed premium, usually for a twelve-month period, on an agreed value, and subject to a deductible below which the owner carries the loss himself.

It is written on standard market forms rather than bespoke wordings - principally the Institute Time Clauses - Hulls, the International Hull Clauses or the Nordic Marine Insurance Plan. The insurer is a commercial insurer writing for profit, and the relationship is an ordinary contract of indemnity.

2. (a) What P&I insurance is

P&I is insurance of the owner's legal liabilities to third parties arising out of the operation of the ship. It is not property insurance at all: it responds to what the owner must pay somebody else.

Its structure is quite different. It is written by mutual associations - P&I clubs - which the shipowners themselves own. There are no external shareholders and no profit motive; each member is simultaneously insurer and assured. Members contribute by calls rather than premium: an advance call at the start of the policy year, a supplementary call if the year's claims exceed expectation, and in an extreme year an overspill call. Cover is defined by the club rules, which the members make, and the Board retains a discretion to meet claims that fall outside them.

Most of the world fleet is entered with clubs in the International Group, which allows the mutual system to carry catastrophic exposure: each club retains claims up to its own retention, claims above that are pooled and shared among the Group clubs, above the pool sits a collective market reinsurance programme, and above that an overspill arrangement. That structure is why a single mutual can stand behind a liability far larger than any one club.

3. (a) The main differences in coverage and purpose

Hull and MachineryProtection and Indemnity
What is insuredThe ship - a physical asset the owner ownsThe owner's legal liability to third parties
Nature of lossFirst-party: the owner's own lossThird-party: what the owner must pay others
Who writes itCommercial underwriters, for profitMutual clubs owned by the members themselves
What is paid inA fixed premiumCalls - advance, supplementary and overspill
Contract basisStandard market forms and a policyClub rules, made by the members
Limit of coverThe agreed value of the shipEffectively unlimited, save for specific heads such as oil pollution which carry a cap
PurposeProtect the owner's capital assetProtect the owner's solvency against liabilities that can exceed the ship's value many times

The single sentence that carries the distinction: H&M answers the question 'what has happened to my ship?'; P&I answers the question 'what do I now owe to somebody else?'

The governing law, which both covers sit inside. This is an Indian examination and the operative statute is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. It is modelled on the United Kingdom Marine Insurance Act 1906 but it is the 1963 Act that governs, and it supplies the general law of the contract within which both covers operate - insurable interest, the duty of utmost good faith (section 19) and the consequent duty of disclosure (section 20), warranties, the classification of losses, and general average loss (section 66). Two cautions follow. The UK Insurance Act 2015 reform of the avoidance remedy is not part of Indian law and must not be imported. And the statute governs the contract of marine insurance; a P&I club entry is a mutual arrangement governed by the club's own rules, which is one more reason the two covers are not simply two policies of the same kind.

4. (a) Why a shipowner needs both

Because neither covers the other's field, and each exposure alone can destroy the business. Without H&M, a total loss destroys the asset - and with it the security on which the ship was financed, which is why mortgagees require it as a condition of lending. Without P&I, a single incident can produce liabilities that are not bounded by the ship's value at all: a serious pollution incident, a passenger casualty or a large cargo claim can exceed the vessel's worth many times over, and no property policy would respond to a penny of it.

There is also a hard practical reason. Compulsory insurance certification under the liability conventions is supported by the club, so a ship that is not entered in an acceptable P&I club cannot obtain the certificates it needs and will not be permitted to trade. In practice, entry is a condition of trading, and hull cover is a condition of financing.

5. (b) Typical risks covered by H&M, and when it responds

The hull policy covers physical damage to and loss of the ship, from the traditional maritime perils and from a set of specified additional causes:

  • Perils of the seas, fire and explosion, violent theft, jettison, piracy, and contact with aircraft, dock or harbour installations.
  • Under the Inchmaree or negligence clause, and subject to due diligence by the assured: bursting of boilers, breakage of shafts, latent defect in machinery or hull, and the negligence of master, officers, crew, pilots and repairers.
  • Total loss, actual or constructive, and particular average - partial damage.
  • The ship's contribution to general average and to salvage.
  • Sue and labour - the reasonable cost of steps taken to avert or minimise a covered loss.
  • Collision liability to another ship, traditionally three-fourths of it under the running-down clause.

Incidents where H&M responds. A main engine crankshaft fractures at sea and the engine is destroyed - hull, under the negligence and latent-defect cover, subject to due diligence and the deductible. The ship grounds and bottom plating is set up - hull, particular average. An engine-room fire gutters the machinery space - hull. The ship is a constructive total loss after grounding - hull pays the agreed value. In each case note what is being repaired: the owner's own property.

6. (b) Typical risks covered by P&I, and when it responds

The club covers the owner's liabilities, costs and expenses to others:

  • Crew - illness, injury and death, hospital and repatriation costs, wages during incapacity, and the cost of substitutes.
  • Passengers and other persons - injury, death and loss of effects.
  • Cargo - loss of, damage to and shortage of cargo carried.
  • Pollution - liability for oil and other pollution, clean-up costs and the resulting claims. This is the one major head that carries a specific monetary limit rather than the general unlimited cover.
  • Wreck removal where it is compulsorily required.
  • Damage to fixed and floating objects - berths, buoys, cables, cranes.
  • The collision liability not covered by the hull policy - the remaining fourth and any excess above the hull limit.
  • Fines, quarantine and diversion expenses, stowaways, life salvage, towage and contractual liabilities, and legal costs.

Incidents where P&I responds. A bunker overflow oils a berth and the harbour - club, for the clean-up and the third-party claims. An oiler is injured by a falling grating and repatriated - club. A reefer container's cargo is condemned on discharge - club. The ship strikes and demolishes a berth crane - club, as damage to a fixed object. The ship is arrested and the crew must be maintained and repatriated - club. In each case note who is being paid: somebody other than the owner.

7. (b) The boundary - where both are engaged at once

The two covers meet in the same casualty more often than a table suggests, and this is where marks are won.

Collision. One event produces two quite different losses. Damage to your own ship is a hull claim. Liability to the other ship and her cargo is a liability claim, and it is split: the hull policy has traditionally taken three-fourths under the running-down clause and the club the remaining fourth and anything above the hull limit - though modern forms may write collision liability at four-fourths by agreement. If the collision also spills oil, the pollution liability is entirely the club's. So a single collision can involve the hull underwriter twice and the club twice, which is precisely why both must be placed with the casualty in mind.

General average. When the master makes a voluntary and extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure for the common safety - beaching a burning ship, engaging salvors, putting into a port of refuge - the loss is shared between ship, cargo and freight in proportion to value. The ship's contribution is a hull claim. The club's involvement is in the liability consequences, and in the salvage context it is the club that meets special compensation, where the reward itself falls on the salved property.

8. (b) Their roles in managing liabilities and operational risk

They do different jobs and the difference is not only which cheque is written.

H&M manages capital risk. It converts an unpredictable catastrophic loss of the asset into a predictable annual premium, protects the mortgagee's security, and keeps a repair the owner cannot fund from becoming an insolvency. Its discipline runs through the deductible - the owner keeps small damage, so the incentive to maintain remains with him - and through warranties and class, since cover assumes the ship is in class and properly manned.

P&I manages liability risk, and it does far more than pay. Because the members own it, the club is also a service organisation: it appoints correspondents in every port, instructs lawyers and surveyors, provides security to lift an arrest, supports the compulsory-insurance certification without which the ship cannot trade, and issues loss-prevention guidance drawn from the whole fleet's claims. Its discipline runs through underwriting the member rather than the risk - a poorly managed owner meets rising calls, or is refused entry - and through the pay-to-be-paid principle, under which the member must generally discharge the liability before he is indemnified.

Read together, they cover the two ways a shipping company fails: it loses its ship, or it owes more than it is worth. H&M answers the first; P&I answers the second. Neither is a substitute for the other, and a responsible owner supplements both - with war risks, loss of hire and charterers' liability cover - according to the trade.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 8 and 8 - an even split, which is unusual and tells you the two limbs are equally weighted. Limb (a) asks three things: define both covers, outline the main differences in coverage and purpose, and say why both are essential. Limb (b) asks three: the typical risks covered by each, examples of incidents where each would respond, and their roles in managing liabilities and operational risks.

The word examples is printed and it is the cheapest set of marks on the question. Two or three concrete incidents per cover - a crankshaft failure, a bunker spill, an injured oiler, a demolished berth crane - are worth more than another paragraph of definition, and most candidates never write them.

The one sentence that carries the whole question

H&M answers 'what has happened to my ship?'. P&I answers 'what do I now owe to somebody else?'

Every difference in the table is a consequence of that. Because H&M insures a thing, it can be valued, so it has an agreed value and a limit. Because P&I insures a liability, it cannot be valued in advance, so the cover is effectively unlimited and had to be organised mutually - no commercial insurer would write unlimited liability for a profit margin. And because the members own the club, it behaves like a service organisation rather than a counterparty.

Common mistakes

  • Citing the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 in an Indian examination. The operative statute is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963; the 1906 Act is its model, not the law here. The UK Insurance Act 2015 reform of the avoidance remedy does not apply either.
  • Saying P&I covers damage to the ship. It does not. That is the whole point of the distinction.
  • Saying H&M covers pollution. It does not. Pollution liability is a club matter throughout.
  • Calling a P&I contribution a premium. It is a call - and the difference matters, because a supplementary call can be levied after the event.
  • Describing a club as an insurance company. It is a mutual association owned by its members, who are both insurer and assured.
  • Getting the collision split backwards. The hull policy has traditionally taken three-fourths of the collision liability; the club takes the remaining fourth and any excess.
  • Forgetting that a collision produces two kinds of claim - damage to your own ship, and liability to theirs.
  • Answering limb (b) with more definitions and no incidents. The stem asks for examples.
  • Quoting monetary limits - the oil-pollution limit, club retentions, pooling layers. They are reset annually and cannot be verified in an examination.
  • Omitting the service role of the club - correspondents, security to lift an arrest, certification support, loss prevention. It is half of why P&I matters.

Examiner traps

  • Which statute governs a contract of marine insurance here? The Marine Insurance Act, 1963 - not the UK 1906 Act. Utmost good faith at s.19, disclosure at s.20, general average loss at s.66.
  • Your ship spills oil in a collision. Who pays what? Damage to your own ship - hull. Liability to the other ship - three-fourths hull, one-fourth club, plus any excess. Pollution - entirely the club. This one question tests the whole answer.
  • Is P&I cover unlimited? Effectively yes for most heads, but not for oil pollution, which carries a specific cap. Say that without inventing the figure.
  • Why is P&I mutual and H&M commercial? Because an unquantifiable liability cannot be profitably underwritten at a fixed premium, whereas a ship has a value.
  • What is the Inchmaree clause? The negligence clause extending hull cover to bursting of boilers, breakage of shafts, latent defect and the negligence of crew, pilots and repairers - subject to due diligence by the assured.
  • What is 'pay to be paid'? The member must generally discharge the liability himself before the club indemnifies him.
  • Could you trade without P&I? In practice no - the certification for compulsory insurance under the liability conventions is supported by the club.
  • Who insists on hull cover? The mortgagee. It is the security for the loan.
  • What is a supplementary call? An additional contribution levied when the policy year's claims exceed what the advance calls raised - a feature no commercial policy has.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Define P&I in one sentence.
  • Name six heads of P&I cover.
  • What does the hull policy cover that a candidate would not expect? Latent defect and crew negligence, under the Inchmaree clause.
  • What is a deductible, and why does it exist?
  • What is the International Group, and why does it exist?
  • Your engine fails from a latent defect. Which policy, and what must you show? Hull, and due diligence.
  • A stevedore is injured by your crane. Which policy?
  • What is sue and labour?
  • Which insurance covers loss of earnings while the ship is repaired? Neither - that is loss of hire, a separate cover.
  • Which Act governs marine insurance in India, and what does section 19 provide?

Memory framework

Two limbs, eight steps, and one axis. Limb (a) - define HULL (1), define CLUB (2), tabulate the differences (3), say why both (4). Limb (b) - hull risks and incidents (5), club risks and incidents (6), the BOUNDARY (7), and the two kinds of risk management (8).

For the seven rows of the comparison, walk one question down the table: what, whose loss, who writes it, what is paid in, on what terms, how much, and why. For P&I heads of cover walk the ship from the inside out: CREW, PASSENGERS, CARGO, POLLUTION, WRECK, FIXED OBJECTS, COLLISION EXCESS, FINES AND COSTS - people first, then what you carry, then what you damage outside the ship.
