Uncertainty and the evidence position

This question has no governing instrument, and the answer does not invent one. Marine insurance is a matter of contract and market practice, not of IMO regulation. MIW holds no policy wording, no set of Institute or International Hull Clauses, no club rule book and no International Group pooling agreement - and these are commercial documents rather than public instruments, so this is not a gap that ordinary corpus acquisition closes. Every statement about cover is therefore industry practice or authoritative restatement, classified P2 or P3, never primary. No clause is quoted and no clause number is asserted anywhere.

No monetary figure of any kind appears in this answer, and that is deliberate rather than an omission. There is no oil-pollution limit, no passenger or crew limit, no club retention, no pooling layer, no reinsurance or overspill figure, no premium or call rate, no deductible and no claims statistic. The International Group's limits and layers are reset annually at renewal; MIW holds no source for them; and a figure given as at November 2024 could not be verified. The structure is described instead - retention, pool, market reinsurance, overspill - which is what the examiner is actually testing. No edition year is given for any clause set, for the same reason.

One point is stated with its variation rather than flattened. The three-fourths collision proportion under the running-down clause is the settled market convention and is what an examiner expects, but modern hull forms may write collision liability at four-fourths by agreement. Which applies in a given case depends on the actual policy, and the answer says so rather than presenting the traditional split as invariable.

On currency: this is one of the few questions on the paper with no 2024 boundary at all. Nothing in the structure of hull or P&I cover moved during 2024. The only moving parts are the annual limits and layers, which is exactly why none is quoted.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) (a) What hull and machinery insurance is
  2. __________a) (a) What P&I insurance is
  3. __________a) (a) The main differences in coverage and purpose
  4. __________a) (a) Why a shipowner needs both
  5. __________b) (b) Typical risks covered by H&M, and when it responds
  6. __________b) (b) Typical risks covered by P&I, and when it responds
  7. __________b) (b) The boundary - where both are engaged at once
  8. __________b) (b) Their roles in managing liabilities and operational risk
Flashcards 9

Hull and machinery insurance is a property cover placed in the commercial market which indemnifies the owner for physical loss of or damage to the ship, her machinery and equipment, on an agreed value and subject to a deductible. P&I insurance is a third-party liability cover, written by a mutual association owned by its shipowner members and funded by calls, which indemnifies the owner against his legal liabilities, costs and expenses to others arising out of the operation of the ship.

The definitions must carry the structural contrast - commercial and property against mutual and liability - because everything else follows from it.

The Marine Insurance Act, 1963. It is modelled on the United Kingdom Marine Insurance Act 1906, but the 1906 Act is not operative here. It supplies insurable interest, the duty of utmost good faith at section 19 and the consequent duty of disclosure at section 20, warranties, the classification of losses, and general average loss at section 66. The UK Insurance Act 2015 reform of the avoidance remedy forms no part of Indian law.

Citing the 1906 Act is the standing error on Indian marine insurance questions, and much secondary material invites it.

Because a ship has a value and a liability does not. A vessel can be valued in advance, so the risk of losing it can be priced and profitably underwritten at a fixed premium. Third-party liabilities cannot be quantified in advance and can exceed the ship's value many times over, so no commercial insurer would write them on effectively unlimited terms for a margin. Shipowners therefore insure each other, contributing by calls, writing their own rules, and grouping internationally so that a catastrophic claim can be shared.

The structural insight the question is really testing, and it explains every row of the comparison.

Crew illness, injury, death, repatriation and wages; passengers and other persons; cargo loss, damage and shortage; pollution liability and clean-up; wreck removal where compulsory; damage to fixed and floating objects; the portion of collision liability not carried by the hull policy and any excess; and fines, quarantine and diversion expenses, stowaways, life salvage, towage and contractual liabilities and legal costs.

Eight countable heads. Walking them from the inside out - people, then cargo, then what you damage outside the ship - keeps the list complete under pressure.

Damage to your own ship is a hull claim. Liability to the other ship and her cargo is split: traditionally three-fourths falls on the hull policy under the running-down clause and the remaining fourth on the club, together with any excess above the hull limit - though modern hull forms may write collision liability at four-fourths by agreement. The pollution liability and clean-up costs are entirely the club's. So one casualty engages the hull underwriter twice and the club twice.

The single question that tests the whole answer, and the examiner's favourite.

The negligence clause in the hull policy, which extends cover beyond the traditional perils of the seas to bursting of boilers, breakage of shafts, latent defect in the hull or machinery, and the negligence of the master, officers, crew, pilots, repairers and charterers. It is subject to the assured, owners or managers exercising due diligence, so cover is lost where the loss results from want of diligence at management level.

The commonest oral follow-up on the hull half, and the due-diligence proviso is the part candidates omit.

Members pay an advance call at the start of the policy year, a supplementary call if that year's claims exceed expectation, and in an extreme year an overspill call. The International Group lets the mutual system carry catastrophic exposure: each club retains claims up to its own retention, claims above that are pooled and shared among the Group clubs, above the pool sits a collective market reinsurance programme, and above that an overspill arrangement.

The structure is the mark. No figure should be quoted - retentions and layers are reset at every renewal.

Because the compulsory insurance certification required under the liability conventions is supported by the club, so a ship not entered with an acceptable club cannot obtain the certificates it needs and will be refused entry to port. In practice club entry is a condition of trading, just as hull cover is a condition of financing - the mortgagee requires it as security for the loan.

Answers 'why are both essential' concretely rather than as a truism, which is what limb (a) is asking for.

It acts as a service organisation for its members: it maintains correspondents in ports worldwide, instructs lawyers and surveyors, provides security to lift an arrest, supports the compulsory-insurance certification the ship needs to trade, and issues loss-prevention guidance drawn from the claims experience of the whole entered fleet. It also underwrites the member rather than the risk, so a poorly managed owner meets rising calls or is refused entry.

Half of why P&I matters, and the part candidates leave out when they treat a club as merely another insurer.

Quick revision
15-second recall
The axis. H&M = 'what happened to MY ship?'; P&I = 'what do I OWE someone else?' H&M. Property cover on hull, machinery and equipment; commercial underwriters writing for profit; fixed premium; agreed value; deductible; standard forms - Institute Time Clauses Hulls, International Hull Clauses, Nordic Plan. Covers perils of the seas, fire, explosion, theft, jettison, piracy, contact damage; plus - under the Inchmaree/negligence clause, subject to due diligence - bursting boilers, breakage of shafts, latent defect, negligence of crew, pilots and repairers; total loss and particular average; GA and salvage contribution; sue and labour; and traditionally 3/4 collision liability. P&I. Third-party liability; written by MUTUAL CLUBS owned by the members - insurer and assured are the same people; funded by CALLS (advance, supplementary, overspill), not premium; cover set by club rules with Board discretion; International Group gives retention - pool - market reinsurance - overspill. Covers CREW (injury, illness, death, repatriation, wages), PASSENGERS, CARGO, POLLUTION (the one head with a specific cap), WRECK REMOVAL, FIXED AND FLOATING OBJECTS, the remaining 1/4 collision and excess, plus fines, stowaways, diversion, legal costs. Why both. No hull, no asset and no mortgage security; no club, no compulsory-insurance certification and no trading. Boundary. One collision = own damage (hull) + liability to the other ship (3/4 hull, 1/4 club) + pollution (all club). Roles. Hull manages capital risk via premium and deductible; club manages liability risk and is also a service organisation - correspondents, security to lift arrest, certification, loss prevention - disciplined by pay to be paid.

Critical regulation
The Marine Insurance Act, 1963 is the operative Indian statute for a contract of marine insurance - insurable interest, utmost good faith (s.19), disclosure (s.20), warranties, classification of losses and general average loss (s.66). The UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 is its model but is not the law here, and the UK Insurance Act 2015 does not apply. Beyond that statute there is no governing instrument: the covers themselves are contract and market practice, resting on the standard hull forms and on the individual club's rules. The single regulatory contact point is that compulsory insurance certification under the liability conventions is supported by the club.
Major trap
Citing the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 in an Indian examination - the operative statute is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. Then: saying P&I covers damage to the ship, or that H&M covers pollution, either of which destroys the distinction the question is built on; calling a P&I contribution a premium when it is a call; answering limb (b) with more definitions and no incidents when the stem prints the word 'examples'; and quoting monetary limits, which are reset at every renewal.

  • P3 INDUSTRY GUIDANCE - the Inchmaree or negligence clause, extending hull cover beyond perils of the seas to bursting of boilers, breakage of shafts, latent defect, and negligence of master, officers, crew, pilots and repairers, subject to due diligence by the assured. Named by its established market name; no wording quoted.
  • P3 INDUSTRY GUIDANCE - the running-down clause, under which the hull policy has traditionally carried three-fourths of the assured's collision liability to the other ship, the remaining fourth and any excess falling to P&I. This proportion is the settled market convention and is stated as such; the modern hull forms may write it at four-fourths by agreement, and that variation is stated rather than concealed.
  • P3 INDUSTRY GUIDANCE - the mutual structure of P&I: members contribute by advance calls, with supplementary calls where the year's claims exceed expectation and overspill arrangements above the reinsurance programme; the International Group of clubs; the claims-sharing pool above each club's retention, the collective market reinsurance above the pool, and the overspill layer above that; the pay-to-be-paid principle; and the Board's discretion under an omnibus rule. Structure only - no retention figure, pool layer, reinsurance limit or call rate is given.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - that compulsory insurance certification under the liability conventions is supported by the P&I club, which is why a ship's certificates in this area depend on club entry. Stated functionally; no convention article is asserted and no certificate wording is quoted.
  • ENGINEERING AND COMMERCIAL JUDGEMENT - the analysis at steps 3, 4, 7 and 8: the systematic comparison, the reason both covers are indispensable, the treatment of the collision and general-average boundary, and the assessment of each cover's role in managing operational risk. Reasoned from the structure above and declared as judgement.
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED - general average as a contribution mechanism between ship, cargo and freight following a voluntary and extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure for the common safety, carried at concept level from the substantial solved general-average family in the corpus. Used only at the boundary step; no York-Antwerp Rule is quoted or numbered.
  • INTERNAL - the November 2024 anchor at docs/QP2411_TEMPORAL_AND_DONOR_ANCHOR.md sections 2.5, 3 and 4.3, which records that no instrument exists to hold, classifies this question as carrying no 2024 boundary, and records the rejection of the 2026 donors.
Unresolved
  • MIW holds no copy of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. It is cited by identity and by the three section numbers carried from the repository's known-traps register; no provision is quoted and no further section is asserted. Acquiring the Act would promote this to primary.
  • THE PRINCIPAL LIMITATION: MIW holds no policy wording, no Institute or International Hull Clauses, no club rule book and no International Group pooling agreement. No clause is quoted and no clause number is asserted anywhere. This is not a corpus gap that can be closed in the ordinary way, because these are commercial documents rather than public instruments.
  • NO MONETARY FIGURE OF ANY KIND IS QUOTED. Specifically: no oil-pollution limit, no passenger or crew limit, no club retention, no pooling layer, no market reinsurance limit, no overspill figure, no premium or call rate, no deductible and no claims statistic. The International Group's limits and layers are reset annually at renewal, MIW holds no source for them, and any figure stated as at November 2024 would be unverifiable.
  • No edition year is given for the Institute Time Clauses, the International Hull Clauses or the Nordic Marine Insurance Plan, because MIW holds none of them and each has been revised.
  • The three-fourths collision proportion is stated as the settled market convention, with the note that modern forms may write four-fourths by agreement. Which proportion applies in any given case is a matter of the actual policy and cannot be established generally.
  • The analysis at steps 3, 4, 7 and 8 is commercial judgement reasoned from the structure of the two covers. It is not a finding of any market body and is not presented as one.
Re-verify before publication
  • That the operative statute for a contract of marine insurance in this jurisdiction is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, and the section identities given for utmost good faith, disclosure and general average loss. — MIW HOLDS NO COPY OF THE ACT. It is cited by identity and section only and nothing is quoted. The section identities are carried from the repository's own known-traps register (entry 8), which records this as a standing requirement for Indian marine insurance answers precisely because candidates and secondary material routinely cite the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 instead. Acquiring the 1963 Act would promote this to primary. Note the express caution that the UK Insurance Act 2015 reform of the avoidance remedy forms no part of Indian law. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • Every statement in this answer about what hull and machinery cover and P&I cover actually cover, including the heads of cover, the Inchmaree clause extensions, the mutual structure, the call system and the International Group architecture. — MIW HOLDS NO POLICY WORDING, NO INSTITUTE OR INTERNATIONAL HULL CLAUSES, NO CLUB RULE BOOK AND NO POOLING AGREEMENT, and these are commercial documents rather than public instruments, so this is not a gap ordinary corpus acquisition closes. Everything is authoritative restatement or industry practice, classified P2 or P3; no clause is quoted and no clause number is asserted. It ships as-is because the propositions are settled market understanding and the limitation is stated in the answer itself. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • That no monetary figure of any kind appears anywhere in this answer - no oil-pollution limit, passenger or crew limit, club retention, pooling layer, reinsurance or overspill figure, premium, call rate, deductible or claims statistic. — THE ONLY GENUINE CURRENCY EXPOSURE ON THIS QUESTION, AND IT IS CLOSED BY OMISSION RATHER THAN BY A GUESS. International Group limits and layers are reset annually at renewal and MIW holds no source for them, so any figure given as at November 2024 would be unverifiable. A later session must not read this as a gap and fill it with the figure current at the time of reading, which would silently mis-date the answer. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
  • That collision liability is traditionally carried three-fourths by the hull policy under the running-down clause, with the remaining fourth and any excess falling on the club. — This is the settled market convention and is what the examiner expects, but it is not invariable - modern hull forms may write collision liability at four-fourths by agreement, and which applies depends on the actual policy. The answer states the variation rather than flattening a conditional statement into a categorical one. Recorded so that no derived layer - flashcard, recall line or cheat sheet - reduces it to an unqualified rule. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • That no clause-set edition year is given for the Institute Time Clauses, the International Hull Clauses or the Nordic Marine Insurance Plan. — Each has been revised more than once and MIW holds none of them, so an edition stated as current at November 2024 could not be verified. The forms are named by identity only, which is all the question requires. A deliberate omission, recorded so it is not later read as a gap. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
Understand this first

Read this question as two layers that were built ten years apart and are trying to solve the same problem at different speeds. The Strategy is the Organization writing down, in one place, how much shipping must reduce, by when, and by what process the binding rules will be produced. It is not law and it makes nobody do anything; it is a schedule for making law, and it deliberately splits that schedule into three horizons defined purely by timing - what was agreed by 2023, what must be agreed by 2025, and what will not even be designed until the 2028 review. The short-term measures are the part of that schedule which has already been delivered, and they are the only part that is actually binding on your ship. There are two of them and they attack the problem from opposite ends. EEXI asks what the ship is: a one-off calculation from her design, done once, satisfied for most ships by simply capping the power she is allowed to use. The carbon intensity rating asks what the ship does: an annual measurement from the fuel she actually burned, producing a public grade from A to E that follows her around the market. One is a certificate; the other is a report card that arrives every year. And the reason a mid-term basket exists at all is that both of these measures only reward burning less fuel - neither of them cares what the fuel is made of. That is the gap a goal-based fuel standard and a carbon price are meant to close. Finally, note where this paper sits: MEPC 82 finished four weeks before the examination, so the SEEMP guidelines had just been replaced, and the mid-term measures were fully designed and not yet adopted.

Memory cue. Eight steps in the two blocks the stem prints. For the five CII guidelines walk the calculation: how you work it out, what you compare against, how much tighter each year, what grade that earns, what does not count against you.

Model written answer

1. What the operative Strategy is, and what it replaced

The governing instrument is the 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, adopted by resolution MEPC.377(80) on 7 July 2023. It expressly revokes the 2018 Initial Strategy from that date, so the Initial Strategy's targets are no longer the answer to this question. It is a policy instrument, not law: it binds nobody directly and works by committing the Committee to develop measures which then become law as amendments to MARPOL Annex VI. It is to be kept under review with a view to a further revised Strategy in 2028.

2. The vision, the levels of ambition and the indicative checkpoints

The vision is to reduce GHG emissions from international shipping and, as a matter of urgency, to phase them out as soon as possible, while promoting a just and equitable transition. Four levels of ambition follow: the carbon intensity of the ship to decline through better design of new ships; the carbon intensity of international shipping to decline, with CO2 per transport work down at least 40% by 2030 against 2008; the uptake of zero or near-zero GHG technologies, fuels and energy sources to reach at least 5%, striving for 10%, of the energy used by 2030; and to peak emissions as soon as possible and reach net-zero GHG emissions by or around, i.e. close to, 2050.

The indicative checkpoints are different in kind and this is where marks are lost. They are absolute, not intensity-based - to reduce total annual GHG emissions, against 2008, by at least 20%, striving for 30%, by 2030, and by at least 70%, striving for 80%, by 2040. So 40% is intensity and 20% is absolute, both by 2030 and both against 2008. Two further features matter: the targets are GHG, not CO2 alone, so methane counts; and they are measured well-to-wake under the LCA guidelines, MEPC.376(80).

3. The pathway - short, mid and long term as the Strategy defines them

The Strategy defines the three horizons by timing, not by subject matter:

  • Short-term measures - those finalized and agreed between 2018 and 2023. These are already law in MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 and are the subject of the second half of this question.
  • Mid-term measures - a basket to be finalized and agreed by the Committee by 2025, comprising both a technical element - a goal-based marine fuel standard regulating the phased reduction of the GHG intensity of marine fuel - and an economic element, on the basis of a maritime GHG emissions pricing mechanism. The Strategy requires both together, and requires synergies with existing measures such as the carbon intensity indicator to be considered.
  • Long-term measures - deliberately undefined. They are described only as measures finalized and agreed beyond 2030, to be developed as part of the 2028 review. There is no list, and inventing one would be fabrication.

4. Where the process stood at this examination

The Strategy's own milestone table places this sitting. MEPC 81 (spring 2024) has happened, and so has MEPC 82, which sat from 30 September to 4 October 2024 - the session at which the finalized comprehensive impact assessment was due. What has not happened is anything binding: approval of the measures is scheduled for MEPC 83 in spring 2025, adoption at an extraordinary MEPC session about six months later, and entry into force 16 months after adoption. So the position at this date is stated in one sentence: the architecture of the mid-term basket is agreed and nothing in it is adopted. Any answer describing a binding IMO carbon price or fuel-intensity limit as existing law is wrong for November 2024.

5. The first short-term measure - EEXI

The two short-term measures were adopted together by resolution MEPC.328(76) on 17 June 2021, entered into force on 1 November 2022 and applied from 1 January 2023. The first is technical.

The Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index extends to the existing fleet the design discipline the EEDI had imposed only on newbuildings. Each existing ship above the applicable size threshold must calculate an attained EEXI - regulation 23 - representing the CO2 emitted per tonne-mile of transport capacity from its design characteristics, and that attained value must be no greater than the required EEXI for its type and size - regulation 25. It is a one-off, design-based requirement, verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey on or after 1 January 2023 and endorsed on the ship's International Energy Efficiency Certificate.

Because the index is calculated from design parameters, most existing ships comply not by rebuilding but by limiting installed power - a shaft power limitation or engine power limitation, with the limitation recorded in an onboard management manual and an emergency override available to the master when full power is needed for safety. A minority achieve compliance by energy saving devices, hull and propeller improvements or derating.

6. The second short-term measure - the operational carbon intensity rating

The second is operational, and it is the one that bites every year. Under regulation 28, a ship above the applicable size threshold must determine its attained annual operational carbon intensity indicator from the fuel oil consumption data it already reports under the data collection system at regulation 27, and compare it with the required annual operational CII.

The ship is then given a rating from A to E - A being the best. The consequences are graduated rather than penal: a ship rated D for three consecutive years, or E for a single year, must develop a plan of corrective actions in Part III of its SEEMP, and that plan must be approved. The SEEMP itself is required by regulation 26, and compliance is evidenced by a Statement of Compliance. The rating is therefore a continuous operational obligation where the EEXI is a one-off design check - which is the essential contrast between the two.

7. The guidelines that make the two measures workable

Neither measure could operate on the regulation text alone, because both depend on calculation methods and reference values that had to be agreed separately. That is what the question means by with their guidelines.

For the carbon intensity indicator there are five, lettered G1 to G5:

  1. G1 - MEPC.352(78), adopted 10 June 2022: the operational carbon intensity indicators and the calculation methods.
  2. G2 - MEPC.353(78), 10 June 2022: the reference lines against which a ship's performance is judged.
  3. G3 - MEPC.338(76), adopted 17 June 2021: the reduction factors applied to those reference lines year by year, which is what makes the requirement tighten over time.
  4. G4 - MEPC.354(78), 10 June 2022: the rating of ships, that is the A to E boundaries.
  5. G5 - MEPC.355(78), 10 June 2022: interim guidelines on correction factors and voyage adjustments, which prevent a ship being penalised for time spent in ice, at anchor awaiting berth, or on other voyages outside its ordinary trade.

Sitting alongside them are the SEEMP development guidelines, and the edition matters at this date. The operative guidelines are the 2024 Guidelines for the development of a SEEMP, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024 - one month before this examination - whose operative paragraph 3 revokes the 2022 Guidelines, MEPC.346(78). They set out the SEEMP in three parts: Part I, the ship management plan to improve energy efficiency; Part II, the fuel oil consumption data-collection plan; and Part III, the ship operational carbon intensity plan in which the required CII for the next three years, the implementation plan and any corrective actions are recorded.

For the EEXI there is a corresponding set covering the method of calculating the attained EEXI, the survey and certification procedure, and the shaft and engine power limitation route to compliance including the management manual and override arrangements.

8. What the two measures do in practice, and the limits of the short-term approach

Together they attack the problem from both ends: EEXI constrains what the ship is, and CII constrains how she is used. In practice the effects are commercial as much as technical. Power limitation caps the speed that can be offered, which changes what the ship can be chartered for. An annual rating is a public performance grade, so charterers began to ask for it and lenders to price it, and clauses allocating responsibility for the rating between owner and charterer became necessary - because the party that decides the speed and the party that holds the certificate are usually not the same.

The limits are equally clear, and stating them answers the word discuss. Both measures are tank-to-wake in effect - they reward burning less fuel, not burning better fuel - so neither creates any real incentive to adopt a zero-carbon fuel. Both are intensity measures, so a growing fleet can improve its rating while total emissions rise, which is precisely why the Strategy also carries absolute checkpoints. And the CII is acknowledged to reward slow steaming and long ballast legs in ways that do not always reflect real efficiency. That is the reason the mid-term basket exists at all: a well-to-wake goal-based fuel standard plus a price on emissions is designed to reach exactly what the short-term measures cannot.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem sets two distinct tasks and the second is far more specific than candidates expect. First, briefly discuss the ambitions and pathways with focus on short, mid and long-term goals. Second - and note the change of command verb - explain the two short-term measures approved by the IMO for implementation, with their guidelines.

The word briefly governs the first half and is an instruction, not a courtesy: the examiner is telling you not to spend the paper on the Strategy. The word guidelines governs the second half and is where the discriminating marks are. Almost every candidate can describe EEXI and CII; very few can name the guideline layer that makes them operable. Steps 5 to 7 are therefore where this answer earns its marks, and step 7 is the single highest-value paragraph on the question.

Which are 'the two short-term measures'?

EEXI and the operational carbon intensity rating (CII). Both were adopted together by MEPC.328(76) on 17 June 2021, entered into force 1 November 2022 and applied from 1 January 2023. One is technical and one-off; the other is operational and annual. If you hold only that contrast you can construct both descriptions.

Do not answer 'EEDI and SEEMP'. The EEDI applies to newbuildings and long predates the short-term measure package; the SEEMP is the vehicle through which the CII obligation is discharged, not a separate measure. Saying so explicitly is a good discriminator.

The MARPOL Annex VI numbers, and the trap in them

The 2021 revised Annex VI entered into force on 1 November 2022 and renumbered chapter 4. Most published material still carries the old scheme, so a well-read candidate is the one most likely to get this wrong.

  • Regulation 21 - Functional requirements. It is not EEDI. This is the trap.
  • 22 - attained EEDI; 24 - required EEDI.
  • 23 - attained EEXI; 25 - required EEXI.
  • 26 - SEEMP; 27 - fuel oil consumption data collection; 28 - operational carbon intensity.

If the numbers desert you under pressure, name the measures and do not guess. A correct measure with no number scores; a confident wrong number invites a probe.

Get the two families of Strategy numbers apart

  • Levels of ambition are mostly intensity: CO2 per transport work down at least 40% by 2030; zero or near-zero fuels at at least 5%, striving for 10%, of energy used by 2030; and net zero by or around 2050.
  • Indicative checkpoints are absolute: total annual GHG emissions down at least 20%, striving for 30%, by 2030 and at least 70%, striving for 80%, by 2040.

So 40 is intensity, 20 is absolute, both by 2030 and both against 2008. Write them under separate headings and the confusion cannot happen. Never drop the 'striving for' figures - they are half of each checkpoint.

The date of this paper - the sharpest edge on the question

MEPC 82 sat from 30 September to 4 October 2024, before this examination. That single fact separates a November 2024 answer from a September 2024 one, and it has one concrete consequence you must get right:

  • The operative SEEMP guidelines are the 2024 Guidelines, MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024, which revoked MEPC.346(78). A September 2024 answer citing MEPC.346(78) would be correct; the same answer in November is wrong.
  • The chain is worth holding whole: MEPC.346(78) of 2022, amended by MEPC.388(81) at MEPC 81 in March 2024, then revoked and replaced by MEPC.395(82) in October 2024.

In the other direction, the IMO Net-Zero Framework and the GHG Fuel Intensity measure did not exist at this sitting - they came out of MEPC 83 in April 2025. Writing them in is the single most likely way to be wrong on this question today, because almost all current material on IMO decarbonisation uses that vocabulary.

Common mistakes

  • Answering EEDI and SEEMP as the two short-term measures. It is EEXI and CII.
  • Citing MEPC.346(78) as the current SEEMP guidelines. It was revoked on 4 October 2024, one month before this paper.
  • Naming the Net-Zero Framework or GFI. They did not exist - MEPC 83, April 2025.
  • Quoting the 2018 Initial Strategy targets. The 2023 Strategy revoked it.
  • Saying 'net zero by 2050' flatly. The text is 'by or around, i.e. close to, 2050'.
  • Confusing the two families of numbers - 40% intensity, 20% absolute.
  • Describing the mid-term measures as one measure. It is a basket of two, technical and economic, required together.
  • Inventing long-term measures. The Strategy defines them only by timing.
  • Answering 'with their guidelines' by naming none. That is where the marks are.
  • Quoting a CII reduction factor or rating boundary. Those come from the guidelines for a specific ship type, size and year.
  • Using regulation 21 for EEDI - that is the pre-2021 numbering.

Examiner traps

  • Name the two short-term measures. EEXI and the operational carbon intensity rating.
  • Which is technical and which operational? EEXI is technical and one-off; CII is operational and annual.
  • How does a ship usually achieve EEXI compliance? By shaft or engine power limitation, with a management manual and an emergency override.
  • What happens to a ship rated D three years running? A plan of corrective actions in SEEMP Part III, which must be approved.
  • Name the CII guidelines. G1 calculation, G2 reference lines, G3 reduction factors, G4 rating, G5 correction factors and voyage adjustments.
  • Which SEEMP guidelines apply at this date? The 2024 Guidelines, MEPC.395(82), which revoked MEPC.346(78) in October 2024.
  • What is in SEEMP Part III? The ship operational carbon intensity plan - required CII for the next three years, the implementation plan, self-evaluation and any corrective actions.
  • Is the mid-term basket law yet? No. Approval was scheduled for MEPC 83 in spring 2025 and adoption for an extraordinary session thereafter.
  • Why is a mid-term basket needed at all if EEXI and CII exist? Because both short-term measures reward burning less fuel rather than better fuel, and both are intensity-based.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What replaced the 2018 Initial Strategy, and when?
  • State the 2030 and 2040 indicative checkpoints with the striving figures.
  • Is that 40% absolute or intensity?
  • What are the two elements of the mid-term basket?
  • Which regulation carries attained EEXI? And SEEMP?
  • Explain shaft power limitation and the override.
  • How is the attained CII calculated, and from what data?
  • What are the rating bands and what triggers corrective action?
  • Which resolution carries the current SEEMP guidelines, and what did it revoke?

Memory framework

Eight steps in two blocks matching the two printed demands. THE STRATEGY (briefly) - the instrument and what it revoked (1), ambitions and checkpoints (2), the three horizons (3), where we actually stand (4). THE TWO MEASURES (in detail) - EEXI (5), CII (6), THE GUIDELINES (7), and what they do and cannot do (8).

For the measures: EEXI is what the ship IS; CII is what the ship DOES. One-off against annual; design against operation; certificate endorsement against an A-to-E grade.

For the five CII guidelines walk the calculation in order: how you work it out (G1), what you compare it against (G2), how much tighter each year (G3), what grade that earns (G4), and what does not count against you (G5). Then SEEMP Part III is where the answer is written down.

For the Strategy numbers hold one pair and one hedge: 40 intensity and 20 absolute by 2030, 70 by 2040, and net zero by or around 2050. Every 'at least' has a 'striving for' behind it.

Uncertainty and the currency boundary

The evidence position on this answer is strong and almost entirely primary. MEPC.377(80), MEPC.328(76), MEPC.395(82) and all five CII guidelines were read at source from copies held in the MIW True Source corpus, and every target, date, regulation number and resolution identity above was read off the instrument rather than recalled.

Three limits are declared. The EEXI guideline resolutions are not held, so that part of step 7 describes them by function and no resolution number has been invented for them - a deliberate refusal to guess. MEPC.376(80) was not read; it is named only because MEPC.377(80) points to it, and only the well-to-wake principle stated in MEPC.377(80) itself is used. And no CII reduction factor, rating boundary, reference-line value or required-EEXI reduction percentage is quoted anywhere, because those are read from the guidelines for a particular ship type, size band and calendar year, and a general figure would be false precision.

One further honesty. The specific business outcome of MEPC 82 on the mid-term measures is not asserted. What is stated is what the Strategy's own milestone table schedules for that session, and - decisively - that nothing in the basket was adopted at this sitting, which follows from the milestone table itself since adoption is scheduled for an extraordinary session after MEPC 83.

Finally the currency warning, which matters more here than anywhere else on the paper. This answer is anchored to November 2024 and must not be updated in place. The measures were approved, renamed and reshaped from April 2025 onwards, and the correct answer for a later paper is a different answer. What makes this one right is precisely that it stops where the Organization had actually got to - after MEPC 82, before MEPC 83.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What the operative Strategy is, and what it replaced
  2. __________The vision, the levels of ambition and the indicative checkpoints
  3. __________The pathway - short, mid and long term as the Strategy defines them
  4. __________Where the process stood at this examination
  5. __________The first short-term measure - EEXI
  6. __________The second short-term measure - the operational carbon intensity rating
  7. __________The guidelines that make the two measures workable
  8. __________What the two measures do in practice, and the limits of the short-term approach
Flashcards 9

1 The Strategy and what it revoked. 2 Ambitions and indicative checkpoints. 3 The three horizons defined by timing. 4 Where the process stood at this sitting. 5 EEXI. 6 The operational carbon intensity rating. 7 The guidelines. 8 What the measures do and cannot do.

The stem prints 'briefly' for the Strategy half and 'explain ... with their guidelines' for the second, so the weight belongs in steps 5 to 7.

The Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index, EEXI, which is technical and one-off, and the operational carbon intensity indicator rating, which is operational and annual. Both were adopted together by resolution MEPC.328(76) on 17 June 2021, entered into force on 1 November 2022 and applied from 1 January 2023. They are not EEDI and SEEMP: the EEDI applies to newbuildings and long predates the package, and the SEEMP is the vehicle through which the CII obligation is discharged rather than a separate measure.

Identifying the wrong two measures loses the larger half of the question at the first sentence.

Levels of ambition: carbon intensity of new ships to improve by design; CO2 per transport work down by at least 40% by 2030 against 2008; zero or near-zero technologies, fuels and energy sources to be at least 5%, striving for 10%, of the energy used by 2030; and net-zero GHG emissions by or around, i.e. close to, 2050. Indicative checkpoints: total annual GHG emissions down at least 20%, striving for 30%, by 2030 and at least 70%, striving for 80%, by 2040, both against 2008. They differ because the checkpoints are measured on total absolute emissions whereas the corresponding ambition is measured on intensity.

40% intensity against 20% absolute, both by 2030 against 2008, is the most confused pair of numbers on this topic.

Each existing ship above the applicable size threshold must calculate an attained EEXI under regulation 23, representing CO2 emitted per tonne-mile of transport capacity derived from her design characteristics, and that value must not exceed the required EEXI for her type and size under regulation 25. It is a one-off design-based check verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey on or after 1 January 2023 and endorsed on the International Energy Efficiency Certificate. Because it is calculated from design parameters, most ships comply by shaft or engine power limitation, recorded in an onboard management manual with an emergency override available to the master, rather than by physical modification.

The power-limitation route is what makes EEXI a practical rather than a theoretical measure, and the override is the detail examiners probe.

Under regulation 28 a ship above the applicable threshold determines her attained annual operational CII from the fuel oil consumption data reported under the data collection system at regulation 27, and compares it with the required annual operational CII. She is then rated from A to E. A ship rated D for three consecutive years, or E for a single year, must develop a plan of corrective actions in Part III of her SEEMP, required by regulation 26, and that plan must be approved. Compliance is evidenced by a Statement of Compliance.

The rating trigger - D for three years or E for one - is the most commonly misremembered detail in the measure.

G1, resolution MEPC.352(78), the operational carbon intensity indicators and calculation methods. G2, MEPC.353(78), the reference lines. G3, MEPC.338(76), the reduction factors applied to those reference lines year by year. G4, MEPC.354(78), the rating of ships, that is the A to E boundaries. G5, MEPC.355(78), interim guidelines on correction factors and voyage adjustments.

The stem asks for the measures 'with their guidelines'. Naming all five is the single highest-value thing on this question.

The 2024 Guidelines for the development of a SEEMP, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted on 4 October 2024, whose operative paragraph 3 revokes the 2022 Guidelines, MEPC.346(78). The date matters because MEPC 82 sat from 30 September to 4 October 2024, four weeks before this examination, so an answer written for a September 2024 sitting would correctly cite MEPC.346(78) and the same citation here would be wrong. The full chain is MEPC.346(78) of 2022, amended by MEPC.388(81) at MEPC 81 in March 2024, then revoked and replaced by MEPC.395(82).

This is the one fact that distinguishes a November 2024 answer from a September 2024 one.

The architecture was agreed and nothing was adopted. Under the Strategy's own milestone table, MEPC 81 in spring 2024 had finalized the basket, MEPC 82 had sat from 30 September to 4 October 2024, approval of the measures was scheduled for MEPC 83 in spring 2025, adoption for an extraordinary session about six months after that, and entry into force 16 months after adoption. The IMO Net-Zero Framework and the GHG Fuel Intensity measure did not exist at this sitting - they came out of MEPC 83 in April 2025.

Writing a post-2025 answer into a 2024 paper is the commonest way to get this question wrong today.

Because both short-term measures are tank-to-wake in effect and intensity-based. They reward burning less fuel, not burning better fuel, so neither creates any real incentive to adopt a zero or near-zero carbon fuel; and because they measure intensity, a growing fleet can improve its ratings while total emissions still rise. The mid-term basket answers both gaps directly: a goal-based marine fuel standard regulating the well-to-wake GHG intensity of the fuel, and an economic element pricing emissions.

This is the closing evaluation, and it links the two halves of the question into one argument rather than two lists.

Critical regulation
Two instruments carry this answer. Resolution MEPC.377(80), the 2023 Strategy, which by operative paragraph 6 revokes the 2018 Initial Strategy and by section 4.5 requires the mid-term basket to contain both a goal-based marine fuel standard and a GHG pricing mechanism. And resolution MEPC.328(76), the 2021 Revised MARPOL Annex VI, in force 1 November 2022, which introduced the two short-term measures - attained EEXI at regulation 23 against required EEXI at regulation 25, and operational carbon intensity at regulation 28 fed by the data collection system at regulation 27 and discharged through the SEEMP at regulation 26.
Major trap
Three traps, and the middle one is specific to this sitting. First, naming the wrong two measures: it is EEXI and the operational carbon intensity rating, not EEDI and SEEMP - EEDI applies to newbuildings and the SEEMP is the vehicle, not a separate measure. Second, citing MEPC.346(78) as the current SEEMP guidelines: it was revoked by MEPC.395(82) on 4 October 2024, one month before this examination, so a September 2024 answer is correct and the same words here are wrong. Third, writing a post-2025 answer into a 2024 paper: the IMO Net-Zero Framework and the GHG Fuel Intensity measure came out of MEPC 83 in April 2025 and did not exist at this sitting.

Understand this first

The sentence in the question is one of the most repeated in the industry and one of the least examined, and limb (a) exists to see whether you will examine it. Ask where a number like that could come from. Nobody ran a controlled study of accidents with and without people. What happens is that an investigator works backwards from the wreckage and stops when he reaches something that feels like an explanation - and because a human being stands at the end of very nearly every chain of events on a ship, there is always a human act available to stop at. So the figure does not measure human unreliability; it measures where investigations stop looking. The phrase the examiner adds - complex hierarchical socio-technical system - is the corrective, and the load-bearing word in it is hierarchical. A ship is not one person and one machine; it is layers. Somebody in an office chose the manning level. Somebody in a shipyard put two identical valves side by side. Somebody in a chartering department set the schedule. Those decisions were all taken long before the incident and far from the ship, and they are what determined whether the person at the bottom of the hierarchy could succeed. His act is simply the last thing that happened, and calling it 'the cause' is a choice about where to stop, not a discovery. Once that lands, limb (b) becomes easy, because the three confused terms turn out to sit at three different levels of exactly that system: the event at the sharp end, the discipline that explains and designs against it, and the whole territory of human involvement in which both sit. And the reason the distinction matters is entirely practical - each one points at a different intervention, and only the widest of them can actually remove the condition.

Model written answer

1. (a) Where the 80 per cent claim comes from, and what it is

The figure is quoted everywhere and is almost always misused. It does not come from a study which established that four accidents in five are caused by a defective human being. It is an artefact of how investigations attribute causes. An investigator works backwards from the damage and stops when a satisfying answer appears - and because a person is present at the end of almost every chain of events, the last human act before the loss is nearly always available as an answer.

So the statement is better read as: in about 80 per cent of accidents, a human action or decision can be identified in the causal chain. That is close to a tautology in a system operated by people, and it says almost nothing about what should be done. Two things follow. It is not a finding, it is a stopping rule. And treating it as a finding produces the wrong response - exhortation, blame and another procedure - because if the cause is a careless individual, the cure appears to be a more careful one.

2. (a) What 'a complex hierarchical socio-technical system' means for a ship

The phrase in the question is the corrective, and each word does work.

  • Socio-technical - the ship is not a machine with an operator attached. Performance is produced by people and technology together, plus the procedures and the environment. Neither half can be assessed alone.
  • Complex - the parts are tightly coupled and interact in ways not fully foreseeable. A change made for one good reason - a new alarm, a revised checklist, a reduced crew - has effects elsewhere that nobody designed.
  • Hierarchical - and this is the decisive word. The system has layers: the regulator and flag State, the classification society, the shipyard and equipment designer, the company and its shore management, the master, and finally the watchkeeper with his hand on the valve. Decisions taken at the upper layers - manning levels, maintenance budget, schedule pressure, equipment selection, procedure design - create the conditions in which the person at the lowest layer acts.

The consequence is the heart of limb (a). Accidents in such a system are not usually caused by one failure. They occur when latent conditions, created earlier and elsewhere by people who were nowhere near the ship at the time, lie dormant until they combine with an active failure at the sharp end and with the particular circumstances of the day. The defences that should have stopped it - design, procedure, training, supervision, alarms - each had a gap, and on that occasion the gaps lined up.

3. (a) What 'human error' therefore means here, and what follows

Read against that system, 'human error' denotes the last human action in a chain, at the sharp end, where the investigation happened to stop. It is a label applied at the point where somebody ceased to ask 'why'. It is therefore a symptom, not a cause - the visible end of a process whose real determinants lie upstream in design, staffing, scheduling and management decision.

Two management consequences follow, and they are what a management-level candidate is being examined on. First, the correct question after an incident is not 'who erred?' but 'why did this action make sense to a competent person at the time, given what he had in front of him?' - because it almost always did. Second, errors cannot be eliminated by instructing people not to make them. They are reduced by changing the conditions that produce them: better interfaces, workable procedures, adequate manning and rest, honest reporting and a management system that acts on what is reported. That is precisely why the international standard for casualty investigation - the Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008 - is directed at establishing the circumstances and causes of a casualty so that safety may be enhanced, and is expressly not an exercise in apportioning blame.

4. (b) Human Element - the domain

The Human Element is the subject area. It is the recognition that people are an integral part of the maritime system, and it is the broadest of the three terms. The Organization treats it as a complex multi-dimensional issue affecting safety, security and protection of the marine environment, and - crucially - as involving the entire spectrum of human activity: ships' crews, shore-based management, regulatory bodies, recognized organizations, shipyards, legislators and others. That framing is set out in the IMO's Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals, resolution A.947(23).

It is therefore not confined to seafarers, and that is the commonest misunderstanding of the term. Its regulatory expression is the whole apparatus that deals with people: the ISM Code for safety management, resources and the master's authority; STCW for competence, training and fitness for duty; and MLC 2006 for hours of work and rest and for living and working conditions. Human Element answers the question 'who and what is in scope?'

5. (b) Human Error - the outcome

Human Error is an event. It is a specific occasion on which a planned action fails to achieve its intended outcome, or on which the plan itself was inadequate. It is the narrowest of the three terms, it is retrospective, and it describes what happened, not why.

It is not a single thing, and distinguishing the types is worth marks because each has a different remedy:

  • Skill-based slips and lapses - the intention was right and the execution failed. Operating the adjacent valve; omitting a step through interruption. Remedy: design and interruption management.
  • Rule-based mistakes - a good rule applied in the wrong situation, or a bad rule applied correctly. Remedy: better procedures and better diagnosis training.
  • Knowledge-based mistakes - a novel situation with no rule available, reasoned through incorrectly under time pressure. Remedy: training, decision support and expert access.
  • Violations - deliberate departures from a rule, which are not errors at all. They may be routine (the shortcut everyone uses), situational (the rule cannot be complied with in the conditions given) or exceptional. Remedy: find out why the rule is not workable, because a routine violation is a statement about the procedure, not about the person.

6. (b) Human Factors - the discipline

Human Factors is the scientific discipline, and its application. It is the study of the interaction between people, equipment, procedures, organisation and environment, and the use of that knowledge to design systems that fit the people who must operate them - rather than expecting people to adapt to whatever has been built.

It is the only one of the three that is proactive and prospective. Its subject matter is workload and attention, fatigue and circadian rhythm, the design of controls, displays and alarms, automation and mode awareness, anthropometry and the physical environment, procedure design, communication and team behaviour. Human Factors answers the question 'how do we study this, and what do we change?'

7. (b) How the three differ, worked through one incident

The distinction is easiest to see on a single event. An engineer, at the end of a long watch, opens the wrong valve and floods a space.

TermWhat it isIn this incident
Human ElementThe domain - the whole scope of human involvement, from the regulator to the ratingEverything that put him there: manning level, the company's schedule, rest-hour compliance, training given, the safety culture that decided whether he would report a near miss
Human ErrorThe outcome - the specific act that failedHe opened the wrong valve. A skill-based slip
Human FactorsThe discipline - why the act happened, and what to changeTwo identical valves, adjacent, unlabelled, poorly lit, operated at the end of a 6-on 6-off watch. The interface and the watch pattern made this error probable

So the three terms sit at different levels: Human Element is the field, Human Factors is the method, and Human Error is the event. Their relationship can be put in one sentence - Human Error is what you see; Human Factors is how you explain and prevent it; the Human Element is the whole territory in which both live.

The distinction is not academic, because each term implies a different response. Treat the flooding as human error alone and you issue a warning and add a line to a checklist, and the next engineer makes the same slip. Treat it as a human-factors problem and you label and re-site the valves, improve the lighting and review the watch pattern, and the error becomes difficult to make. Treat it as a human-element problem and you ask why the ship was manned and scheduled so that a fatigued engineer was working alone at that hour - which is the only level at which the condition can actually be removed.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 6 and 10. Limb (a) is a single focused demand: discuss the term 'human error' as referred to here - and the words 'of a complex hierarchical socio-technical system' are not decoration, they are the instruction. The examiner is telling you what frame to use. An answer that defines human error as a mistake made by a person, without engaging that phrase, cannot score well however fluent it is.

Limb (b) asks how three terms differ, with suitable examples. The word examples is printed and is the cheapest set of marks on the question. One worked incident that distinguishes all three - as at step 7 - is worth more than three separate definitions, because it demonstrates the distinction rather than asserting it.

The one idea that organises the whole question

Human error is a symptom, not a cause. It is the label an investigator applies at the point where he stops asking why.

Everything else follows. The 80 per cent figure is high because a person is present at the end of nearly every chain, not because people are unusually unreliable. The word hierarchical matters because the conditions for the error were set by people higher up the system and long before the event. And the three terms differ because they sit at three different levels of that same system - the event, the explanation, and the territory.

The three terms in one line each

TermWhat kind of thing it isQuestion it answersTime direction
Human ElementA domain or subject area - the broadestWho and what is in scope?Neutral - it is a field
Human FactorsA scientific discipline and its applicationHow do we study it and what do we change?PROACTIVE - it designs
Human ErrorAn event or outcome - the narrowestWhat happened?RETROSPECTIVE - it describes

If you can write that table you have limb (b)'s first half. Then add one worked incident and the taxonomy of error types, and it is complete.

Common mistakes

  • Accepting the 80 per cent figure as a finding and building the answer on it. Limb (a) is asking you to examine the claim, not repeat it.
  • Ignoring the phrase 'complex hierarchical socio-technical system'. It is the examiner handing you the frame.
  • Using Human Element and Human Factors interchangeably. One is a domain, the other a discipline - which is precisely what limb (b) says people do wrongly.
  • Confining the Human Element to seafarers. The IMO's framing expressly includes shore management, regulators, recognized organizations, shipyards and legislators.
  • Treating violations as errors. A violation is deliberate. A routine violation is evidence that the procedure is unworkable, not that the person is careless.
  • Giving no examples in limb (b) when the stem prints the word.
  • Concluding that the answer to human error is more training and more care. That is the response the question is testing you against.
  • Naming a real casualty and misdescribing it. If you are not certain of the findings, use a constructed example instead.
  • Quoting an author, a model name or a study. Explain the mechanism instead - it scores the same and cannot be wrong.

Examiner traps

  • Do you accept that 80 per cent of accidents are caused by human error? The expected answer is a qualified no - a human action can be identified in most causal chains, which is a different statement, and the figure reflects where investigations stop.
  • What does 'hierarchical' add? That the conditions for a sharp-end error are created by decisions taken higher in the system and earlier in time - manning, budget, schedule, design, procedures.
  • Give me an active failure and a latent condition from the same event. The engineer opening the wrong valve is the active failure; the unlabelled identical valves and the watch pattern are latent conditions.
  • Is a violation an error? No - it is deliberate. Ask instead why the rule is not being followed.
  • Which of the three terms is the widest? Human Element. Which is proactive? Human Factors. Which is retrospective? Human Error.
  • As Chief Engineer, your third engineer opens the wrong valve. What do you do? Establish why the act made sense to him at the time; then change the conditions - labelling, siting, lighting, watch pattern, workload - rather than issuing a warning.
  • Which instrument requires you to look beyond the last unsafe act? The Casualty Investigation Code, resolution MSC.255(84) of 16 May 2008, which is directed at circumstances and causes rather than at blame.
  • Where does the ISM Code fit? It is the regulatory expression of the human element at the organisational layer - resources and personnel, the master's overriding authority, and the reporting and analysis of non-conformities and hazardous occurrences.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Define human error in one sentence.
  • Define the human element.
  • What is ergonomics, and how does it relate to human factors?
  • Distinguish a slip from a mistake.
  • What is a latent condition? Give one from your own engine room.
  • Why is blaming the individual usually the wrong response?
  • What is a routine violation, and what does it tell you?
  • How does fatigue produce error rather than merely tiredness?
  • Name three human-factors interventions you could make on board this week.

Memory framework

Two limbs, seven steps. Limb (a) walks one argument: the figure is a stopping rule (1), the system is layered and the conditions come from above (2), so error is a symptom and not a cause (3). Limb (b) is three definitions and a demonstration: ELEMENT the domain (4), ERROR the event (5), FACTORS the discipline (6), then one incident that separates all three (7).

For the three terms use FIELD - METHOD - EVENT, and remember they get narrower in that order. For the error taxonomy use SLIP, LAPSE, MISTAKE, VIOLATION - and remember the last one is not an error at all.

For the worked example, keep one incident in your head and rehearse it in three registers: the wrong valve is the event; the identical unlabelled valves and the watch pattern are the factors; the manning, schedule and reporting culture are the element.

Uncertainty and the limits of what is claimed

This is a conceptual question and the answer is written as one. No instrument prescribes what these three terms mean, and none has been named to give the answer false authority. One instrument was read at source - resolution MSC.255(84) of 16 May 2008, adopting the Casualty Investigation Code - and it is used only for the proposition that the international standard for investigation is directed at circumstances and causes rather than blame.

Three limits are declared. IMO resolution A.947(23) is not held by MIW; it is cited by identity for the Organization's framing of the human element, with nothing quoted and no paragraph asserted. MIW holds no text-bearing copy of the ISM Code, STCW or MLC 2006, so all three are referred to at regime level only and no provision number is given. And the safety-science content is declared as established reference: no author is named and no publication, model name or case study is cited, because MIW holds none - the concepts are explained mechanistically instead, which scores identically and cannot be misattributed.

One point of substance about evidence. The 80 per cent figure is not endorsed anywhere in this answer. It is the examiner's printed premise, and limb (a) consists precisely of examining it. No alternative statistic is offered in its place - to replace one unverifiable figure with another would miss the point - and no named casualty is used. The worked example at step 7 is a constructed engine-room scenario, presented as an illustration and not as a report of a real incident.

On currency: this question carries no 2024 boundary at all. The concepts are long settled, nothing in the ISM, STCW or MLC regimes relevant to them moved during 2024, and the Casualty Investigation Code has been in force since 2010. Note only that the MLC 2022 amendments entered into force on 23 December 2024, after this sitting, and nothing in this answer depends on them.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) (a) Where the 80 per cent claim comes from, and what it is
  2. __________(a) (a) What 'a complex hierarchical socio-technical system' means for a ship
  3. __________(a) (a) What 'human error' therefore means here, and what follows
  4. __________(b) (b) Human Element - the domain
  5. __________(b) (b) Human Error - the outcome
  6. __________(b) (b) Human Factors - the discipline
  7. __________(b) (b) How the three differ, worked through one incident
Flashcards 8

Limb (a): 1 what the 80 per cent claim really represents. 2 what a complex hierarchical socio-technical system means for a ship. 3 what human error therefore denotes, and what follows for management. Limb (b): 4 Human Element, the domain. 5 Human Error, the event. 6 Human Factors, the discipline. 7 all three worked through one incident.

Limb (a) is one continuous argument rather than three topics, and step 7 supplies the 'suitable examples' the stem prints.

Not as stated. The figure is an artefact of how investigations attribute causes rather than a measured finding. Because a person stands at the end of almost every chain of events, the last human act is always available as an explanation and the investigation tends to stop there. Read accurately the claim says only that a human action can be identified in the causal chain of most accidents, which is close to a tautology in a system operated by people and says nothing about what should be changed.

This is the whole of limb (a) in miniature, and accepting the figure at face value is the failure the question is designed to expose.

Socio-technical: performance is produced by people and technology together with procedures and environment, so neither half can be assessed alone. Complex: the parts are tightly coupled and interact in ways not fully foreseeable, so a change made for one good reason has effects nobody designed. Hierarchical: the system has layers - regulator and flag State, classification society, shipyard and designer, company and shore management, master, and finally the watchkeeper - and decisions taken in the upper layers set the conditions in which the lowest layer acts.

The examiner supplied this phrase deliberately. Unpacking all three words, and identifying 'hierarchical' as the decisive one, is what limb (a) rewards.

An active failure is an unsafe act at the sharp end whose effect is felt almost immediately - the engineer opening the wrong valve. A latent condition is a weakness created earlier and elsewhere by people not present at the time, which lies dormant until it combines with an active failure - two identical unlabelled valves sited side by side, poor lighting, a watch pattern guaranteeing fatigue, and a manning level that left him working alone. The accident occurs when the gaps in successive defences line up.

The analytical core of limb (a), and the standard oral follow-up.

They sit at three levels of the same system. Human Element is the domain - the whole scope of human involvement from regulator to rating - and answers who and what is in scope. Human Factors is the scientific discipline and its application - the study of the interaction between people, equipment, procedures and environment, used to design systems that fit their operators - and answers how we study it and what we change. Human Error is the event - a specific occasion on which an action failed to achieve its intended outcome - and answers what happened. Element is the widest and neutral, Factors is proactive, Error is retrospective and narrowest.

The printed demand of limb (b). Field, method, event is the compression to hold.

Skill-based slips and lapses, where the intention was right and execution failed. Rule-based mistakes, where a good rule was applied in the wrong situation or a bad rule applied correctly. Knowledge-based mistakes, where a novel situation with no rule available was reasoned through incorrectly. And violations, which are deliberate departures from a rule and are therefore not errors at all - they may be routine, situational or exceptional. A routine violation is evidence that the procedure is unworkable rather than that the person is careless, so the response is to find out why the rule is not being followed.

Each type has a different remedy, which is what makes the taxonomy worth writing rather than just listing.

Because the Organization's own framing, in the Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals at resolution A.947(23), treats it as a complex multi-dimensional issue involving the entire spectrum of human activity in the industry - ships' crews, but also shore-based management, regulatory bodies, recognized organizations, shipyards and legislators. Confining it to the crew reproduces the very error limb (a) identifies, by locating the human contribution only at the sharp end.

The commonest misunderstanding of the term, and it connects limb (b) back to limb (a).

Establish first why the action made sense to him at the time. As human error it is a skill-based slip, and the tempting response - a warning and another checklist line - will not prevent the next one. As a human-factors problem the causes are two identical adjacent valves, no labelling, poor lighting and a watch pattern producing fatigue, so the response is to label and re-site the valves, improve the lighting and review the watch pattern. As a human-element problem the question is why the ship was manned and scheduled so that a fatigued engineer was working alone at that hour - which is the only level at which the condition can actually be removed.

One incident read in three registers is the strongest possible answer to 'with suitable examples', and it shows why the distinction is practical rather than academic.

Critical numbers

  • Casualty Investigation Code adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008
  • IMO resolution A.947(23) - Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals for the Organization
  • Three terms, three levels: Human Element the domain, Human Factors the discipline, Human Error the event
  • Four categories of unsafe act: skill-based slips and lapses, rule-based mistakes, knowledge-based mistakes, and violations - the last not being errors at all
  • Three kinds of violation: routine, situational and exceptional
  • Two limbs at 6 and 10 - the comparison carries the larger weight
Critical regulation
No instrument defines these terms, and none is named as though it did. Two anchor the answer. The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008, is directed at establishing the circumstances and causes of a casualty so that safety may be enhanced - the regulatory expression of looking past the last unsafe act rather than apportioning blame. The IMO's Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals, resolution A.947(23), supplies the Organization's own framing of the human element as a complex multi-dimensional issue spanning the whole industry. A.947(23) is not held by MIW and is cited by identity only.
Major trap
Accepting the 80 per cent figure as a finding and writing an answer about careless seafarers. Limb (a) exists to test whether you can see that the figure reflects where investigations stop, not how unreliable people are - and the phrase 'complex hierarchical socio-technical system' is the examiner handing you the corrective. The second trap is using Human Element and Human Factors interchangeably, which is the very confusion limb (b) names: one is a domain, the other a discipline. The third is treating a violation as an error - it is deliberate, and a routine violation is evidence about the procedure rather than the person.

Also on the platform: QP2411 Q1 - immersive training, one of the human-factors interventions that changes conditions rather than exhorting care

Understand this first

Every one of these four tests exists because you cannot open the machine. The oil is the one thing that has touched every bearing, every ring and every gear tooth continuously since the last inspection, so it carries the evidence out to you - and the whole art is knowing which evidence each test can actually see. That is the real content of this question, and it is why the limitations matter more than the descriptions. Two of the tests look at the oil itself and ask whether it can still do its job: FTIR watches the molecules change as the oil oxidises and picks up contamination, and the base and acid numbers measure the chemical fight between the alkaline reserve the oil was given and the acid the engine keeps making. The other two look at the debris the machine is shedding, and they are deliberately different from each other. The spectrometer identifies the debris - it tells you the metal is copper, so the problem is a bearing - but it is nearly blind to anything large, because a big particle does not fully vaporise in the plasma. The particle counter is the opposite: it cannot tell you what anything is made of, but it counts and sizes everything, including exactly the coarse flakes the spectrometer misses. Put crudely: the spectrometer knows what, the counter knows how big, and a failing bearing is found by the one that knows how big. Once that lands, the last piece is discipline rather than science - none of these numbers means anything as a single reading. They mean something as a trend, from the same sample point, on the same machine, against its own history and against the new oil.

  • principle - molecular bonds absorb infrared energy at characteristic wavenumbers

  • oxidation at the carbonyl position; nitration; sulphation; water at the hydroxyl position
