Model written answer

1. Why the oil is analysed, and the three questions the techniques answer

Used lubricating oil is the only sample that has been in continuous intimate contact with every working surface in the machine, so it carries a record of what is happening inside without opening anything. But the four named techniques are not four ways of doing the same thing - they answer three different questions, and that is the organising idea of the answer:

  1. Is the machine wearing? - answered by elemental spectrometric analysis and by particle count.
  2. Is the oil still fit for service? - answered by FTIR and by Base Number and Acid Number.
  3. Is anything getting in that should not be? - answered by all four, from different directions.

Throughout, the governing principle is that trend matters more than any single value. A result is compared with the new oil, with the history of that machine, and with the sister unit; an isolated number without a baseline is close to useless.

2. (a) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis

Principle. A measured quantity of oil is excited in a high-energy source - an inductively coupled plasma, or an arc struck across a rotating disc electrode. The energy strips electrons to higher states; as they fall back, each element emits light at its own characteristic wavelengths. The instrument disperses that light and measures the intensity at each wavelength, which is proportional to the concentration of that element, reported in parts per million.

What it measures. It answers three questions at once, and the diagnostic skill lies in knowing which family an element belongs to:

  • Wear metals - where the metal came from tells you which component is wearing. Iron from liners, rings, gears and shafting; copper from bearing shells, bushes, cooler tubes and thrust pads; lead and tin from white metal bearing overlays; chromium from plated rings and liner surfaces; aluminium from pistons and some bearing alloys; silver, nickel and molybdenum from more specific components.
  • Contaminants - silicon from airborne dust and dirt ingress, though it can also come from a silicone antifoam additive; sodium and boron from cooling water treatment, and sodium also from seawater; vanadium and nickel together from residual fuel, so their appearance in a system oil points to fuel entering the crankcase.
  • Additive elements - calcium and magnesium from the overbased detergents, and zinc and phosphorus from anti-wear additives. A falling trend indicates additive depletion, and a rising one usually means the wrong oil has been topped up.

3. (a) What spectrometric analysis cannot see, and why that governs its use

This is the point that separates a good answer from a description, and it follows from the principle. The technique depends on the particles being completely atomised in the excitation source. Fine debris is; large particles are not, so the response falls away progressively as particle size increases, and the method effectively reports only the fine fraction of the debris present.

The consequence is serious. Normal rubbing wear produces fine particles and is seen well. But the failure modes that matter most - bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting - produce comparatively large particles, and a machine can be shedding visible flakes of white metal while the spectrometric iron and lead trends remain unremarkable. A satisfactory spectrometric report is therefore not evidence that the machine is healthy. That is precisely why a particle count, and where necessary ferrography or a magnetic plug and filter inspection, must sit alongside it.

4. (b) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy

Principle. A thin film of oil is placed in an infrared beam. Molecular bonds absorb energy at characteristic wavenumbers determined by their mass and stiffness, so each species present removes energy at its own positions. The instrument gathers all wavelengths simultaneously as an interferogram and applies a Fourier transform to produce the spectrum - which is why the method is fast and needs only a very small sample.

The essential feature is that it is a differential measurement. The spectrum of the used oil is compared with, and subtracted from, the spectrum of the same oil unused, so what is reported is the change. It follows that a reference spectrum of the correct new oil is indispensable, and that FTIR results from an unknown or mixed oil are unreliable.

What it measures - oil condition and contamination, not wear:

  • Oxidation - growth of the carbonyl absorption near 1710 wavenumbers, the fundamental ageing mechanism, accelerated by temperature, aeration and catalytic wear metals. It leads to viscosity increase, varnish and sludge.
  • Nitration - absorption near 1630 wavenumbers from nitrogen oxides reacting with the oil; significant in gas engines and in high-NOx duty.
  • Sulphation - absorption near 1150 wavenumbers from sulphur oxides, tracking the acidic attack that the base number exists to neutralise.
  • Water - a broad hydroxyl absorption near 3400 wavenumbers.
  • Soot - a general rise in baseline absorbance rather than a discrete peak, since soot scatters broadly. The principal measure of combustion carry-over in a diesel engine.
  • Fuel dilution, glycol ingress and additive depletion - each with its own characteristic signature.

Strengths and limits. It is fast, needs a very small sample, and returns many parameters from one measurement, which makes it the natural screening tool. But it is semi-quantitative, it needs the matching new-oil reference, and it tells you nothing about wear metals or particles - it sees molecules, not debris.

5. (c) Particle count

Principle. The oil is passed through a cell and the solid particles are counted and sized. The common instrument is an automatic optical counter working by light obscuration - each particle crossing a laser beam casts a shadow on a photodetector whose magnitude gives the particle's size and whose occurrence is one count. Where the oil is too dark or too dirty for light to pass, a pore-blockage method is used instead, deducing the distribution from the rate at which a calibrated mesh blinds; microscopic counting remains the referee method.

What it reports. Not a mass or a concentration but counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands - typically particles larger than 4, 6 and 14 micrometres - conventionally condensed into a three-part ISO cleanliness code, one number for each band. What matters operationally is the trend of that code, and whether it is being held inside the level the equipment requires.

Where it matters and what it shows. It is decisive in hydraulic and servo systems - steering gear, controllable-pitch propeller, hydraulic governors, fuel-injection control oil - where clearances are measured in microns and a rising count predicts sticking valves and erosion long before a failure. It is also the direct measure of filtration and centrifuge performance and of ingress through breathers and seals. And, critically, it sees the large particles that spectrometric analysis misses, which is why the two are complementary rather than alternative.

Limits. An optical counter cannot distinguish a solid particle from a water droplet or an entrained air bubble, both of which are counted, so a wet or freshly circulated sample reads falsely high; and it gives no indication of what the particles are or where they came from.

6. (d) Base Number against Acid Number

Both are titrations and both are reported in the same unit - milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil - which is why they are confused. They measure opposite properties.

Base Number (BN)Acid Number (AN)
What it measuresThe ALKALINE RESERVE remaining in the oilThe ACIDIC constituents present in the oil
Where it comes fromOverbased detergent additives - calcium and magnesium sulphonates and phenatesStrong acids from combustion of sulphur, and weak organic acids from oxidation of the oil
Its jobTo neutralise acids as they form and keep surfaces cleanIt has no job - it is a product of degradation and contamination
Direction in serviceFALLS as the reserve is consumedRISES as acids accumulate
What a bad trend meansThe oil can no longer neutralise acid - corrosive wear of liners, rings and bearings followsThe oil is oxidising or being acid-contaminated - varnish, sludge, viscosity rise and corrosion follow
Where it governsEngine oils with an alkaline reserve, above all cylinder oil in a crosshead engineOils with little or no reserve - turbine, hydraulic, gear, stern tube and compressor oils

Two points that carry the marks. First, an oil can show both a base number and an acid number at the same time, and that is not a contradiction: they are determined by separate titrations, and weak organic acids can accumulate while an alkaline reserve is still present. Second, and more practically, two different standard methods for base number are in general use - one using a strongly acidic titrant, one a weaker one - and they return systematically different values on the same oil, the stronger method reading higher. Results obtained by different methods must never be trended against one another, which is a real and common error when a laboratory is changed.

The marine application. In a crosshead main engine, cylinder oil base number is chosen against the sulphur content of the fuel actually being burned, so that acid is neutralised without over-dosing - over-alkaline oil at low sulphur causes calcium deposits and can cause bore polishing. Since the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit came into force on 1 January 2020, low-sulphur operation calls for a lower base number oil while a ship using high-sulphur fuel with an exhaust gas cleaning system continues to need a high one, and vessels switching between them must manage two cylinder oils. In the system oil, base number depletion is a warning of acidic blow-by past the stuffing box. In turbine, hydraulic and stern tube oils, which carry no meaningful reserve, acid number is the governing parameter and its rise is the primary measure of oxidative life.

7. How the four fit together in practice

They are a layered set, and using them well means understanding what each cannot do.

  • FTIR plus BN and AN tell you about the oil - whether it is still capable of doing its job, and whether to change or replenish it.
  • Spectrometry plus particle count tell you about the machine - whether it is wearing, and how.
  • Spectrometry says what the debris is; particle count says how much and how big. Neither alone is sufficient, because spectrometry under-reports the large particles that signal a developing failure, and a particle count cannot say whether they are white metal or dirt.
  • Where both point to a problem, the next steps are physical: ferrography to classify particle morphology, and inspection of magnetic plugs, filters and centrifuge sludge.

For a Chief Engineer the discipline is unglamorous and decisive: sample from the same point, in the same way, at the same interval, on a running and circulating system, into a clean container; keep a new-oil reference; and read the trend against that machine's own history rather than against a general figure. Condemning and alarm limits are specific to the engine builder, the oil and the application, and applying a limit from one machine to another is itself a source of error. The purpose is not to pass a test but to convert a symptom into a maintenance decision early enough for it to be cheap.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem prints (16) against four lettered items with no individual allocation, so the safe assumption is roughly four marks each. That has a direct consequence for writing: the four sections must be of comparable length. The commonest failure on this question is a long, comfortable answer on spectrometric analysis and base number - the two most familiar items - and two thin paragraphs on FTIR and particle count, which throws away half the question.

For each item, answer the same three things in the same order: principle - what it measures - what it cannot do. That structure is worth marks on its own because it demonstrates understanding rather than familiarity, and the limits are what an examiner at management level is actually probing.

The idea that organises the whole question

Two of these techniques look at the OIL and two look at the MACHINE.

  • FTIR and BN/AN answer: is the oil still fit to do its job?
  • Spectrometry and particle count answer: is the machine wearing, and how?

And within the second pair there is a second split that is worth a mark by itself: spectrometry tells you WHAT the debris is; particle count tells you HOW MUCH and HOW BIG. Hold those two sentences and you can reconstruct the relationships between all four without recalling them.

The single most important technical point on this question

Emission spectrometry is particle-size limited. Large particles are not fully atomised in the plasma or arc, so the instrument progressively under-reports them and effectively measures only the fine fraction.

That matters because the failure modes you most want to catch produce large particles. Rubbing wear is fine and is seen well; bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting are coarse and may not be. A machine can be shedding flakes of white metal while the iron and lead trends look perfectly ordinary. A clean spectrometric report is not a clean bill of health - and stating that explicitly is the strongest single sentence available in limb (a).

Elements and where they come from

FamilyElementsPoints to
Wear metalsIronLiners, rings, gears, shafting
Wear metalsCopper, lead, tinBearing shells, bushes, white metal overlays, cooler tubes
Wear metalsChromium, aluminiumPlated rings and liner surfaces; pistons
ContaminantsSiliconDust and dirt ingress - but also a silicone antifoam additive, so check the new oil first
ContaminantsSodium, boron, potassiumCoolant treatment ingress; sodium also seawater
ContaminantsVanadium and nickel togetherResidual fuel - so fuel is entering the crankcase
AdditivesCalcium, magnesiumOverbased detergent - a falling trend means depletion
AdditivesZinc, phosphorusAnti-wear additive - a rising trend usually means the wrong oil was added

Common mistakes

  • Writing three long items and one short one. There are four and they are worth roughly the same.
  • Describing spectrometry without its particle-size limitation. That limitation is the reason the other techniques exist.
  • Saying FTIR measures wear. It sees molecules, not metal - it measures oil condition and contamination.
  • Forgetting that FTIR is a differential measurement needing a new-oil reference spectrum of the same oil.
  • Treating particle count as a measure of weight or concentration. It is counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands.
  • Forgetting that an optical counter counts water droplets and air bubbles as particles.
  • Saying an oil cannot have both a base number and an acid number. It can - different titrations.
  • Trending base numbers obtained by different standard methods against one another. They differ systematically.
  • Assuming higher base number is always better. Over-alkaline cylinder oil on low-sulphur fuel causes calcium deposits and can cause bore polishing.
  • Quoting a condemning limit, alarm level or ppm figure as a general rule. They are engine-builder and application specific.
  • Treating a single result as diagnostic. Trend against the machine's own history.

Examiner traps

  • Your spectrometric report is normal but the magnetic plug is furred. What is happening? The debris is coarse - fatigue or spalling - and spectrometry is under-reporting it. This is the flagship question on this topic.
  • Why is silicon high? Dirt ingress - or the antifoam additive in the new oil. Check the new-oil baseline before condemning the air filtration.
  • Vanadium and nickel appear in the system oil. So what? They are residual-fuel markers, so fuel is getting into the crankcase - past the stuffing box, or from a leaking fuel pump.
  • What does FTIR need that the other tests do not? A reference spectrum of the same oil unused.
  • Why does soot not give a peak? It scatters broadly and raises the whole baseline rather than absorbing at one bond position.
  • Can BN and AN both be present? Yes - separate titrations, and weak acids can accumulate while reserve remains.
  • Why not simply use the highest available BN cylinder oil? Over-alkalinity on low-sulphur fuel deposits calcium and can polish the bore. The BN is matched to the fuel actually burned.
  • Which parameter governs a turbine or stern tube oil? Acid number - there is no meaningful alkaline reserve to deplete.
  • Which two tests are complementary and why? Spectrometry and particle count - one identifies the debris, the other detects the large particles the first cannot see.

Likely oral follow-up

  • How does a spectrometer identify an element?
  • What size of particle does spectrometric analysis miss, and why?
  • What is ferrography and when would you ask for it?
  • What is the carbonyl peak and what does its growth mean?
  • How is a particle count reported?
  • How would you take a representative oil sample?
  • Define base number and acid number and give the units.
  • Your cylinder oil BN is falling faster than usual. What do you check?
  • You switch from high-sulphur to compliant fuel. What changes in the lubrication regime?

Memory framework

Four items, three questions, one discipline. Open by splitting them - OIL (FTIR, BN and AN) against MACHINE (spectrometry, particle count) - which is step 1 and buys the structure. Then take each item with the same three headings: PRINCIPLE, MEASURES, LIMITS.

For the elements use three families in order: WEAR - CONTAMINANT - ADDITIVE. Within wear, work outward from the combustion space: iron from liners and rings, chromium from plating, aluminium from pistons, then copper, lead and tin from bearings.

For FTIR remember the four Os and a soot: Oxidation, nitratiOn, sulphatiOn, water (hydrOxyl) - plus soot, which is a baseline rise, not a peak.

For limb (d) hold the opposition: BN is what the oil HAS and is spending; AN is what the oil has PICKED UP. BN falls, AN rises. Engine oils live by BN; turbine and hydraulic oils live by AN.

Uncertainty and the limits of what can be quantified

This is an instrumental-analysis and machinery-condition question and the answer is written as one. No IMO or statutory instrument prescribes a lubricating oil analysis programme, and none has been named to give the answer false authority. The physics and chemistry are declared as engineering and analytical-chemistry reference and are explained mechanistically rather than asserted.

No condemning limit, alarm level, parts-per-million value, ISO cleanliness code or base number figure is quoted as a general rule anywhere in this answer. That is deliberate and it is a safety point as much as an evidential one: every such value is specific to the engine builder, the oil, the application and the service, and a figure lifted from one machine and applied to another is a real source of error. The production protocol requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision.

Three narrower limits are declared. The infrared wavenumbers are approximate - they are properties of the molecular bonds and shift slightly with chemical environment, and the exact integration windows are instrument and method specific. The two standard base number methods are described by their chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy between them, but are not identified by standard number, because MIW holds no test-method standard. And the ISO cleanliness code is named as the reporting convention only; no code, band or table is reproduced. No oil company, analyser manufacturer, engine builder, laboratory or proprietary programme is named.

On currency: this question carries no temporal content at all and would be answered identically at any sitting in this period. The single dated fact used - the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit in force since 1 January 2020 - had been operative for almost five years at this sitting and is used only to explain why cylinder oil base numbers are now matched to the fuel actually burned.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Why the oil is analysed, and the three questions the techniques answer
  2. __________a) (a) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis
  3. __________a) (a) What spectrometric analysis cannot see, and why that governs its use
  4. __________b) (b) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy
  5. __________c) (c) Particle count
  6. __________d) (d) Base Number against Acid Number
  7. __________How the four fit together in practice
Flashcards 9

1 Why the oil is analysed and the three questions the techniques answer. 2 (a) Elemental spectrometric analysis. 3 (a) Its particle-size limitation. 4 (b) FTIR. 5 (c) Particle count. 6 (d) Base Number against Acid Number. 7 How the four fit together. Take each item under the same three headings: principle, what it measures, what it cannot do.

Four items at roughly four marks each with no printed split, so equal treatment is the structural requirement; the limitations are what earn management-level marks.

A measured sample is excited in a high-energy source - an inductively coupled plasma or an arc across a rotating disc electrode. Electrons raised to higher states fall back and emit light at wavelengths characteristic of each element, and the intensity at each wavelength gives that element's concentration in parts per million. It detects three families: wear metals such as iron, copper, lead, tin, chromium and aluminium; contaminants such as silicon, sodium, boron, vanadium and nickel; and additive elements such as calcium, magnesium, zinc and phosphorus.

Principle plus the three families is the whole of limb (a)'s descriptive content.

Because emission spectrometry is particle-size limited. The technique depends on particles being completely atomised in the plasma or arc, and large particles are not, so the response falls away progressively with size and the method effectively reports only the fine fraction. Rubbing wear produces fine debris and is seen well, but fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting produce comparatively large particles, so a machine can be shedding flakes of white metal with unremarkable iron and lead trends. A satisfactory spectrometric report is therefore not evidence of a healthy machine.

The single most important interpretive fact in the question, and the reason particle count and ferrography exist.

It measures oil condition and contamination by infrared absorption at wavenumbers characteristic of particular molecular bonds - oxidation at the carbonyl position, nitration, sulphation, water at the hydroxyl position, soot as a broad baseline rise rather than a peak, plus fuel dilution, glycol and additive depletion. It needs a reference spectrum of the same oil unused, because it is a differential measurement reporting only the change. It is fast, uses a very small sample and returns many parameters at once, but it is semi-quantitative and tells you nothing about wear metals or particles - it sees molecules, not debris.

The differential requirement and the blindness to wear are the two points that separate a real understanding of FTIR from a definition.

Solid particles are counted and sized, most commonly by an automatic optical counter using light obscuration, in which each particle crossing a laser beam shadows a photodetector; pore-blockage methods are used where the oil is too dark for light to pass, and microscopic counting is the referee method. Results are reported as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands, typically above 4, 6 and 14 micrometres, condensed into a three-part ISO cleanliness code. It matters most in hydraulic and servo systems - steering gear, controllable-pitch propeller, governors, injection control oil - where clearances are microns, and it is the direct measure of filtration performance and ingress.

Reporting as counts in size bands, not as a concentration, is the detail most often got wrong.

It cannot distinguish a solid particle from a water droplet or an entrained air bubble, so both are counted and a wet or freshly circulated sample reads falsely high. It gives no indication of what the particles are made of or where they came from. And it can be defeated altogether by very dark or sooty oil, which is why pore-blockage instruments are used on crankcase oils.

The complement to the spectrometry limitation - each technique's blind spot is the other's strength.

Both are titrations reported in milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil. Base Number measures the alkaline reserve conferred by overbased detergent additives, whose job is to neutralise acids as they form and keep surfaces clean; it falls in service as the reserve is consumed. Acid Number measures the acidic constituents present - strong acids from the combustion of fuel sulphur and weak organic acids from oxidation of the oil itself; it rises. Engine oils with a reserve are governed by BN; turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils, which carry no meaningful reserve, are governed by AN.

The identical unit is why the two are confused; the opposite direction of travel is the fastest way to keep them apart.

Yes. They are determined by separate titrations, and weak organic acids from oxidation can accumulate while an alkaline reserve is still present, so both values can be reported on the same sample without contradiction. The practical trap is that two distinct standard titration methods for Base Number are in general use - one with a strongly acidic titrant, one with a weaker one - and they return systematically different values on the same oil, the stronger reading higher. Results obtained by different methods must never be trended against one another, which is a common error when a laboratory is changed.

Two discriminating points in limb (d), and the method mismatch is a real operational error rather than an academic one.

Because the base number is matched to the sulphur content of the fuel actually being burned. Too little reserve leaves acid unneutralised and gives corrosive wear of liners and rings; too much, on low-sulphur fuel, leaves unreacted overbased additive to form hard calcium deposits and can lead to bore polishing and increased wear. Since the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit came into force on 1 January 2020, low-sulphur operation calls for a lower base number oil while a ship burning high-sulphur fuel with an exhaust gas cleaning system still needs a high one - so a vessel switching between them may need to manage two cylinder oils.

The applied end of limb (d), and it converts a laboratory parameter into an operating decision, which is what management level is being tested on.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Split them first. OIL - FTIR, BN/AN. MACHINE - spectrometry, particle count. (a) SPECTROMETRIC. Excite in ICP or rotating-disc arc; each element emits at characteristic wavelengths; intensity gives ppm. Wear - Fe liners/rings/gears, Cu/Pb/Sn bearings, Cr plating, Al pistons. Contaminant - Si dirt (or antifoam), Na/B coolant, Na seawater, V+Ni together = residual fuel in the crankcase. Additive - Ca/Mg detergent, Zn/P anti-wear; falling = depletion, rising = wrong oil added. THE LIMITATION. Large particles are not fully atomised, so the method reports the FINE FRACTION ONLY. Rubbing wear seen; fatigue, spalling, scuffing, pitting MAY NOT BE. A clean spectro report is not a clean bill of health. (b) FTIR. Bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers; interferogram + Fourier transform; DIFFERENTIAL against the same oil unused - a new-oil reference is essential. Measures oxidation (carbonyl ~1710), nitration (~1630), sulphation (~1150), water (hydroxyl ~3400), soot (baseline rise, not a peak), fuel dilution, glycol, additive depletion. Fast, tiny sample, many parameters - but semi-quantitative and blind to wear metals. (c) PARTICLE COUNT. Optical light obscuration; pore blockage for dark oils; microscopy as referee. Counts per ml in cumulative bands (>4, >6, >14 micron) as a three-part ISO cleanliness code. Decisive for hydraulics and servo systems; measures filtration and ingress; and SEES THE BIG PARTICLES SPECTROMETRY MISSES. Limits - water droplets and air counted as particles; says nothing about what they are. (d) BN vs AN. Both mg KOH/g. BN = alkaline reserve from overbased Ca/Mg detergents - it FALLS; its job is neutralising acid and keeping surfaces clean. AN = acids present from sulphur combustion and oxidation - it RISES. Both can be present at once - separate titrations. Two standard BN methods give different values - never trend them together. Cylinder oil BN matched to fuel sulphur (0.50% limit since 1 Jan 2020); over-alkalinity deposits calcium and can polish the bore. Turbine/hydraulic/stern tube = AN governs. No condemning limits quoted - they are engine-builder specific.

Critical numbers
  • NO condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, ISO cleanliness code or BN figure is quoted as a general rule - all are engine-builder, oil and application specific
  • Both BN and AN are expressed in milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil
  • Particle counts are reported per millilitre in cumulative size bands, typically greater than 4, 6 and 14 micrometres, condensed into a three-part ISO cleanliness code
  • Approximate FTIR positions: carbonyl (oxidation) near 1710, nitration near 1630, sulphation near 1150, hydroxyl (water) near 3400 wavenumbers; soot as a baseline rise rather than a peak
  • Two distinct standard titration methods for Base Number are in use and return systematically different values on the same oil, the stronger acidic titrant reading higher
  • The 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit has been in force since 1 January 2020
  • Four printed items with no separate allocation against a printed (16) - approximately four marks each
Critical regulation
There is no governing instrument. No IMO or statutory requirement prescribes a lubricating oil analysis programme, and none is named to give the answer false authority - it is machinery condition monitoring, governed by the engine builder's recommendations and the company's safety management system. The only dated fact used is the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit, in force since 1 January 2020, and it appears solely to explain why cylinder oil base number is matched to the sulphur content of the fuel actually burned.
Major trap
Describing spectrometric analysis without its particle-size limitation. Because large particles are not fully atomised in the excitation source, the method effectively reports only the fine fraction - so bearing fatigue, spalling and scuffing, which shed coarse debris, can be in progress while the iron and lead trends look normal. A clean spectrometric report is not a clean bill of health, and this is the reason particle count and ferrography exist. The second trap is saying an oil cannot show both a base number and an acid number - it can, because they are separate titrations. The third is assuming higher BN is always better: over-alkaline cylinder oil on low-sulphur fuel deposits calcium and can cause bore polishing.

Reuse tier C · Verification FRESH RESEARCH, NO DONOR. THIS IS AN INSTRUMENTAL-ANALYSIS AND MACHINERY-CONDITION QUESTION AND IT CARRIES NO REGULATORY CONTENT. NO IMO INSTRUMENT PRESCRIBES A LUBRICATING OIL ANALYSIS PROGRAMME AND NONE HAS BEEN NAMED TO GIVE THIS ANSWER FALSE AUTHORITY. The physics and chemistry - atomic emission from an excited plasma or arc and the particle-size dependence that follows from incomplete atomisation of large particles; infrared absorption at characteristic wavenumbers by specific molecular bonds and the differential measurement against a new-oil reference spectrum; optical light-obscuration and pore-blockage particle counting and the interference from water droplets, air and soot; and acid-base titration of an alkaline additive reserve against accumulated strong and weak acids - is declared as ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE under PASTPAPER_PRODUCTION_PROTOCOL section 2.1 and is explained mechanistically rather than asserted. TEST-METHOD AND REPORTING STANDARDS ARE NAMED BY IDENTITY ONLY AND ARE NOT HELD BY MIW: the ISO cleanliness code for particle-count reporting, and the two distinct standard titration methods for Base Number which give systematically different results on the same oil. THAT LAST POINT IS RECORDED AS A REAL PRACTICAL TRAP AND IS DECLARED AS RESTATEMENT, NOT AS PRIMARY. NO CONDEMNING LIMIT, ALARM LEVEL, PPM VALUE, CLEANLINESS CODE OR BN FIGURE IS QUOTED AS A GENERAL RULE ANYWHERE: every such value is engine-builder, oil-company and application specific, and section 2.1 requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision. The characteristic infrared wavenumbers are given as approximate positions because they are properties of the molecular bonds themselves rather than of any instrument or standard, and are declared as such. THE ONLY DATED CONTENT IS THE 0.50 PER CENT SULPHUR LIMIT, IN FORCE SINCE 1 JANUARY 2020 AND THEREFORE LONG OPERATIVE AT THIS SITTING, which is used solely to explain why cylinder oil base numbers are now matched to the fuel actually burned. Sources
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - atomic emission spectrometry: the sample is excited in a plasma or across a rotating-disc electrode arc, each element emits light at characteristic wavelengths, and the intensity at each wavelength gives the concentration of that element in parts per million. Explained mechanistically; declared under production protocol section 2.1.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - the particle-size limitation of emission spectrometry: large particles are not fully atomised in the excitation source, so the response falls away progressively with increasing particle size and the method effectively reports the fine fraction only. This is the single most important interpretive fact about the technique and it is explained by mechanism rather than by a quoted cut-off.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - Fourier Transform Infrared spectroscopy: an infrared beam is passed through a thin film of oil, molecular bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers, the interferogram is transformed to a spectrum, and the spectrum is subtracted from that of the same oil unused so that only the change is measured. The approximate characteristic positions - carbonyl for oxidation, nitro compounds for nitration, sulphate for sulphation, hydroxyl for water, and a broad baseline rise for soot - are properties of the bonds themselves and are given as approximate.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - particle counting by automatic optical light-obscuration, by pore-blockage, or by microscopic count; the reporting of results as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands and their expression as a three-part ISO cleanliness code; and the known interferences - water droplets and entrained air counted as particles, and dark or sooty oils defeating optical instruments. The ISO standard is named by identity only and is not held by MIW; no code table or band value is quoted.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - Base Number as the alkaline reserve conferred by overbased detergent additives, and Acid Number as the quantity of base required to neutralise the acidic constituents present, both expressed in milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil. That the two can coexist in the same sample, being determined by different titrations, is explained mechanistically.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - that two distinct standard titration methods for Base Number are in use, one employing a strongly acidic titrant and one a weaker acidic titrant, and that they give systematically different results on the same oil, the stronger method returning the higher value. Named by identity only; MIW holds no test-method standard and no procedure is quoted. Recorded because comparing results obtained by different methods is a real and common practical error.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020 under MARPOL Annex VI. Used solely to explain why cylinder oil base numbers are now selected against the fuel actually burned. No Annex VI provision is quoted and no regulation number is asserted, because this answer needs only the fact of the limit.
  • ENGINEERING JUDGEMENT - the analysis at steps 1 and 7: that the four techniques answer three separate questions, that spectrometry and particle counting are complementary rather than alternative, and the account of how a Chief Engineer sequences and acts on the results. Reasoned from the mechanisms above and declared as judgement.

Unresolved
  • NO CONDEMNING LIMIT, ALARM LEVEL, PPM VALUE, ISO CLEANLINESS CODE OR BASE NUMBER FIGURE IS QUOTED AS A GENERAL RULE ANYWHERE. Every such value is specific to the engine builder, the oil, the application and the service, and a single figure presented as general would be false precision and could be positively dangerous if applied to the wrong machine.
  • The characteristic infrared wavenumbers are given as APPROXIMATE positions. They are properties of the molecular bonds and vary slightly with the chemical environment; exact positions and the integration windows used are instrument and method specific.
  • The two standard Base Number titration methods are described by their chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy between them, and are NOT identified by standard number. MIW holds no test-method standard and no procedure is quoted.
  • The ISO cleanliness code is named as the reporting convention for particle counts but no code, band or table is reproduced, because MIW does not hold the standard.
  • No oil company, analyser manufacturer, engine builder, laboratory or proprietary condition-monitoring programme is named anywhere.
  • The sequencing and management advice at step 7 is engineering judgement reasoned from the properties of the techniques. It is not a manufacturer's recommendation and is not presented as one.

Q1 Virtual and Augmented Reality in Maritime Training and Maintenance
16 marks		 What VR and AR are, and why the difference decides the application → Integration into training → Integration into operations → Integration into maintenance → The benefits, against safety, efficiency and decision-making → The challenges, and a reasoned verdict There is no instrument governing VR or AR, and the answer says so rather than inventing one. The single genuine anchor is the STCW simulator regime: training and assessment may be conducted on a simulator, but the simulator and those conducting and assessing the training must meet the Convention's standards. An immersive trainer is therefore not automatically a recognised means of demonstrating competence.
No adoption rate, cost, training-time saving or retention figure is quoted anywhere - published values in this field are vendor-supplied and unverifiable		 Writing an enthusiastic essay on applications and never answering challenges, which the stem prints first. The second trap is using VR and AR interchangeably - they solve opposite problems, which is why one is a training tool and the other a working tool. The third is claiming VR training satisfies STCW; simulator-based training is recognised, but the simulator and the assessment must meet the standards.
Q2 Ammonia as a Marine Fuel
16 marks		 What ammonia is, and why it is proposed → Technical viability → Safety, and the regulatory route at this sitting → Operational viability → Commercial viability → The environmental balance → Reasoned conclusion SOLAS regulation II-1/55 - alternative design and arrangements. At this sitting this is how an ammonia-fuelled ship was approved, because the IGF Code's detailed provisions covered natural gas only and no IMO instrument governing ammonia as a fuel was in force or even approved. Equivalence to the Code's goal and functional requirements is demonstrated by risk assessment and approved by the Administration or a recognized organization.
Under half the energy of fuel oil per tonne - so about 2.5 to 3 times the tank volume		 Writing the IMO interim guidelines for ships using ammonia as fuel into a November 2024 answer. They were approved at MSC 109, 2-6 December 2024 - the month AFTER this examination - and issued in February 2025. The trap is sharper here than at earlier 2024 sittings precisely because the work was nearly finished: drafted is not approved, and approved is not in force. At this paper's date the correct answer is that no IMO instrument governed ammonia as a fuel and approval ran through SOLAS II-1/55.
Q3 Hull Form Optimisation for Energy Efficiency
16 marks		 Why the hull form governs the power the ship needs → How hull form optimisation is done, and what changed → (i) Forebody optimisation → (ii) Aft body optimisation → (iii) Twin skeg design → What limits the gains, and where they are measured MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, as revised by MEPC.328(76) and in force since 1 November 2022. Hull form is a design property, so it bears directly on attained EEDI (regulation 22) for a newbuilding and attained EEXI (regulation 23) for an existing ship, and in service on operational carbon intensity (regulation 28), planned through SEEMP Part III (regulation 26) under the 2024 Guidelines, MEPC.395(82).
No numeric saving is quoted - all are design-specific		 Saying twin skeg reduces resistance. It increases wetted surface and therefore friction; the gain is propulsive efficiency from larger, slower propellers in cleaner inflow. The second trap is presenting a bulbous bow as unconditionally beneficial. The third is the pre-2021 Annex VI numbering - regulation 21 is Functional requirements, not EEDI, and regulation 22 is attained EEDI, not SEEMP. That scheme was superseded when the revised Annex VI entered into force on 1 November 2022.
Q4 SUA Convention - Purpose, Offences and the 2005 Protocol
16 marks		 (a) Why the Convention exists - the gap it closed → (a) The purpose of the Convention, and how it works → (a) Its importance in enhancing maritime security → (b) The offences under the 1988 Convention → (b) The new offences added by the 2005 Protocol → (b) The boarding regime - the enforcement half of the 2005 Protocol → (b) How the amendments reflect evolving security concerns Article 3 of the SUA Convention 1988, which creates the offences and ties them to acts likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship, and Article 10, which obliges the State in whose territory the offender is found either to extradite him or to submit the case for prosecution. In the 2005 Protocol, Article 3bis creates the terrorism and proliferation offences and Article 8bis creates the flag-State-consent boarding procedure. MIW holds no copy of these instruments; they are cited by identity and nothing is quoted.
Achille Lauro seized October 1985 - the trigger		 Answering this as an anti-piracy question. SUA exists precisely because the conduct is not piracy - piracy requires private ends and two ships. The second trap is claiming Article 8bis permits a warship to board any suspect ship; it is a flag State consent procedure and flag State primacy is preserved. The third is listing offences for the whole of limb (b) and never answering the printed words 'how do these amendments reflect evolving security concerns', which is where the analytical marks are.
Q5 Salvage Convention 1989 - Articles 13 and 14
16 marks		 (a) What the Convention is, and the failure it was written to cure → (a) The primary objectives → (a) How it balances the interests of salvors and owners → (b) Article 13 - the criteria for fixing the reward → (b) Article 13 - apportionment, and the cap → (b) Article 14 - special compensation → (b) Why the two articles matter together Articles 13 and 14 of the International Convention on Salvage, 1989. Article 13 fixes the reward on ten unranked criteria - the second of which is the salvor's skill and efforts in preventing or minimising damage to the environment - paid by ship and cargo in proportion to salved values and capped at the salved value. Article 14 gives special compensation from the shipowner alone, measured on expenses, with an uplift of up to 30 per cent and a tribunal discretion never exceeding 100 per cent, payable only to the extent it exceeds the reward. MIW holds no copy of the Convention; both are cited by identity and nothing is quoted.
Adopted London 28 April 1989; entered into force 14 July 1996; replaced the Brussels Convention of 1910		 Saying the 1989 Convention abolished no cure, no pay. It did not - Article 12 retains it and Article 14 is a bounded exception. The second trap is treating special compensation as an addition to the reward; it is payable only to the extent it exceeds it. The third is presenting Article 14 as the only environmental provision: environmental effort is already the second of the ten Article 13 criteria. The fourth is giving 30 per cent as the absolute maximum - a tribunal may go beyond it, up to 100 per cent of expenses.
Q6 P&I and H&M Insurance Compared
16 marks		 (a) What hull and machinery insurance is → (a) What P&I insurance is → (a) The main differences in coverage and purpose → (a) Why a shipowner needs both → (b) Typical risks covered by H&M, and when it responds → (b) Typical risks covered by P&I, and when it responds → (b) The boundary - where both are engaged at once → (b) Their roles in managing liabilities and operational risk The Marine Insurance Act, 1963 is the operative Indian statute for a contract of marine insurance - insurable interest, utmost good faith (s.19), disclosure (s.20), warranties, classification of losses and general average loss (s.66). The UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 is its model but is not the law here, and the UK Insurance Act 2015 does not apply. Beyond that statute there is no governing instrument: the covers themselves are contract and market practice, resting on the standard hull forms and on the individual club's rules. The single regulatory contact point is that compulsory insurance certification under the liability conventions is supported by the club.
NO MONETARY FIGURE IS QUOTED ANYWHERE - International Group limits, retentions, pooling layers and call rates reset annually and cannot be verified		 Citing the UK Marine Insurance Act 1906 in an Indian examination - the operative statute is the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. Then: saying P&I covers damage to the ship, or that H&M covers pollution, either of which destroys the distinction the question is built on; calling a P&I contribution a premium when it is a call; answering limb (b) with more definitions and no incidents when the stem prints the word 'examples'; and quoting monetary limits, which are reset at every renewal.
Q7 IMO Decarbonisation Ambitions and the Two Short-Term Measures
16 marks		 What the operative Strategy is, and what it replaced → The vision, the levels of ambition and the indicative checkpoints → The pathway - short, mid and long term as the Strategy defines them → Where the process stood at this examination → The first short-term measure - EEXI → The second short-term measure - the operational carbon intensity rating → The guidelines that make the two measures workable → What the two measures do in practice, and the limits of the short-term approach Two instruments carry this answer. Resolution MEPC.377(80), the 2023 Strategy, which by operative paragraph 6 revokes the 2018 Initial Strategy and by section 4.5 requires the mid-term basket to contain both a goal-based marine fuel standard and a GHG pricing mechanism. And resolution MEPC.328(76), the 2021 Revised MARPOL Annex VI, in force 1 November 2022, which introduced the two short-term measures - attained EEXI at regulation 23 against required EEXI at regulation 25, and operational carbon intensity at regulation 28 fed by the data collection system at regulation 27 and discharged through the SEEMP at regulation 26.
MEPC.377(80) adopted 7 July 2023; revokes the 2018 Initial Strategy; review in 2028		 Three traps, and the middle one is specific to this sitting. First, naming the wrong two measures: it is EEXI and the operational carbon intensity rating, not EEDI and SEEMP - EEDI applies to newbuildings and the SEEMP is the vehicle, not a separate measure. Second, citing MEPC.346(78) as the current SEEMP guidelines: it was revoked by MEPC.395(82) on 4 October 2024, one month before this examination, so a September 2024 answer is correct and the same words here are wrong. Third, writing a post-2025 answer into a 2024 paper: the IMO Net-Zero Framework and the GHG Fuel Intensity measure came out of MEPC 83 in April 2025 and did not exist at this sitting.
Q8 Human Error, Human Element and Human Factors
16 marks		 (a) Where the 80 per cent claim comes from, and what it is → (a) What 'a complex hierarchical socio-technical system' means for a ship → (a) What 'human error' therefore means here, and what follows → (b) Human Element - the domain → (b) Human Error - the outcome → (b) Human Factors - the discipline → (b) How the three differ, worked through one incident No instrument defines these terms, and none is named as though it did. Two anchor the answer. The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008, is directed at establishing the circumstances and causes of a casualty so that safety may be enhanced - the regulatory expression of looking past the last unsafe act rather than apportioning blame. The IMO's Human Element Vision, Principles and Goals, resolution A.947(23), supplies the Organization's own framing of the human element as a complex multi-dimensional issue spanning the whole industry. A.947(23) is not held by MIW and is cited by identity only.
The 80 per cent figure is the examiner's premise and is NOT endorsed as a measured finding - no alternative statistic is offered		 Accepting the 80 per cent figure as a finding and writing an answer about careless seafarers. Limb (a) exists to test whether you can see that the figure reflects where investigations stop, not how unreliable people are - and the phrase 'complex hierarchical socio-technical system' is the examiner handing you the corrective. The second trap is using Human Element and Human Factors interchangeably, which is the very confusion limb (b) names: one is a domain, the other a discipline. The third is treating a violation as an error - it is deliberate, and a routine violation is evidence about the procedure rather than the person.
Q9 Lubricating Oil Condition Analysis Techniques
16 marks		 Why the oil is analysed, and the three questions the techniques answer → (a) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis → (a) What spectrometric analysis cannot see, and why that governs its use → (b) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy → (c) Particle count → (d) Base Number against Acid Number → How the four fit together in practice There is no governing instrument. No IMO or statutory requirement prescribes a lubricating oil analysis programme, and none is named to give the answer false authority - it is machinery condition monitoring, governed by the engine builder's recommendations and the company's safety management system. The only dated fact used is the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit, in force since 1 January 2020, and it appears solely to explain why cylinder oil base number is matched to the sulphur content of the fuel actually burned.
NO condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, ISO cleanliness code or BN figure is quoted as a general rule - all are engine-builder, oil and application specific		 Describing spectrometric analysis without its particle-size limitation. Because large particles are not fully atomised in the excitation source, the method effectively reports only the fine fraction - so bearing fatigue, spalling and scuffing, which shed coarse debris, can be in progress while the iron and lead trends look normal. A clean spectrometric report is not a clean bill of health, and this is the reason particle count and ferrography exist. The second trap is saying an oil cannot show both a base number and an acid number - it can, because they are separate titrations. The third is assuming higher BN is always better: over-alkaline cylinder oil on low-sulphur fuel deposits calcium and can cause bore polishing.
